The young man replied that the night before I had come to him, but, being a loyal friend to Yasha, he drove me away and left the house with the purpose of meeting him and informing him of the incident. Yasha only had sufficient self-control to thunder out:
“Swear, are you telling the truth?”
The young rascal answered:
“Certainly it’s the truth.”

===Chinese===

没有办法，只好空着手回去，肩上还压着在阿姆加欠下的债。摆在我面前的，是没完没了的苦熬，好还清亏欠。

夏天的一天，新一队流放犯到了阿姆加。其中有个约莫二十岁的年轻人。雅沙看上了他，提议让他留下住我们家，给我做个帮手。深知雅沙的醋性，我反对。

「雅沙，」我劝他，「你这是做什么？家里来一个殖民地的人你都吃醋，如今倒要我把这年轻人留在家里，你自己又成天在外头。你这是给我找事。我不要他，我用不着帮手。别把他压给我。」

「玛露霞，」雅沙温存地答，「我起誓，再也不吃醋了。不吃了，亲爱的。原谅我叫你受的那些苦。」

雅沙的话没有全安下我的心，可他驳回了我，许诺往后讲道理。当天下午，一个雅库特人来叫他，两人一同去了赌场。年轻人留在我这里。头一两天没事。随后一天夜里，我被惊醒：那年轻人正俯在我身上。我推开他，唤他的羞耻心；他还纠缠，我狠狠一击，跳下床，抄起椅子，扯着嗓子喊，把他轰出了屋子。

那是凌晨一点来钟。古捷穆罗夫亲王从朋友家夜谈回来，正撞见我把那年轻人赶进夜色里。这家伙却对我怀下了深深的报复心。他打定主意：在村外路上候着雅沙回来，把这事编排一套假话告诉他！

「你老婆可真不错，」雅沙一露面，他便讥诮地迎上去。

「什么意思？」雅沙激动地问。年轻人说：头天夜里我主动送上门去，他呢，念着是雅沙的朋友，把我轰走，离开屋子，特为来迎他，报信。雅沙只勉强把持住，吼出一句：

「起誓，你说的是真话？」

那小无赖答：

「当然是真的。」

===Original===

When Yasha appeared on the threshold I observed immediately with horror that he was in a ferocious mood, but was suppressing his fury. That made him the more dangerous. He spoke slowly, picking his words deliberately, words which struck terror to my soul.
“You are a faithless woman. You always have been faithless, deceiving me continually, but you are caught now, and you won’t escape. It’s fortunate that Dmitri is a decent young fellow and repelled your advances. You can say your last prayers, you base creature.”
While speaking thus Yasha proceeded in a cold, businesslike, purposeful manner to make a noose to hang me. It was this calm about Yasha’s actions, expressive of his terrible earnestness, that made me tremble all over.
“Yasha, I am innocent, Yasha,” I sobbed, throwing myself at his feet and kissing them. “I swear that I am innocent,” I cried. “Have mercy! think what you are doing! I tell you I am innocent!”
Yasha went on with his preparations undisturbed.
He attached the rope to a hook on the ceiling and tested the noose.
“Yasha, come to your senses,” I implored, hugging his legs.
He pushed me aside, placed a stool under the rope and ordered me, in a terrible voice, to stand up on it.
“Now say your last prayers,” he repeated.
He then placed the noose around my neck and jerked the stool from under my feet. In an instant it tightened about my throat, I wanted to cry out but could not, the pressure against the crown of my head was so terrific that it seemed about to crack open. Then I lost consciousness.
As the noose was tightening around my neck Yasha came to himself and hastened to loosen it. I dropped, lifeless, to the floor. In response to his calls for help several politicals, among whom were a couple of medical students, came running to the house. They made every effort to revive me, succeeding only after long and persistent attempts. When I opened my eyes, the whole colony was at my bedside. Pressed for an explanation of his inhuman act Yasha told Dmitri’s story.
Then Prince Gutemurov revealed what he had seen the previous night, on his way home. Yasha was overwhelmed. He fell on his knees and begged my forgiveness, cursing Dmitri and promising to make short work of him. But Yasha could not find him. Dmitri learned of the disclosure and disappeared forever from Amga.

===Chinese===

雅沙在门槛上一露面，我立刻惊骇地看出：他杀气腾腾，只是强压着火。这反倒更危险。他说话很慢，一字一字地掂着，那些字句句砸进我的魂里。

「你是个不忠的女人。你从来就不忠，一路哄我；如今叫人拿住了，休想脱身。幸得德米特里是个正派后生，拒绝了你的勾引。做你的临终祷告吧，下贱东西。」

说着这些，雅沙以一种冷静的、公事公办的、一步是一步的方式，动手挽一个吊我的绳套。正是雅沙这份安详——透着他那可怕的认真——叫我浑身发抖。

「雅沙，我是清白的，雅沙，」我扑在他脚下，吻着他的脚，哭着说。「我起誓我是清白的，」我喊。「发发慈悲！想想你在做什么！我告诉你，我是清白的！」

雅沙不动声色，继续他的准备。

他把绳子系上天花板的钩子，试了试绳套。

「雅沙，醒醒吧，」我抱住他的腿哀求。

他把我推开，把一只小凳放到绳下，用可怕的声音命令我站上去。

「现在做你的临终祷告，」他又说了一遍。

随后他把绳套套上我的脖颈，猛地抽走我脚下的凳子。绳套霎时勒紧喉咙；我想喊，喊不出；头顶上那股压力大得吓人，仿佛头壳就要裂开。随后我便失去了知觉。

绳套正在收紧的时候，雅沙醒了过来，赶忙去松。我死了似的跌在地上。他呼救，几个政治犯跑来，其中有两个学医的，用尽法子救我，好一番持久的功夫才把我救转。我睁开眼时，全殖民地的人都守在床边。被追问这桩兽行的缘由，雅沙讲了德米特里的那套话。

随后古捷穆罗夫亲王说出了他昨夜回家路上亲眼看见的一幕。雅沙傻了。他跪倒在地，求我饶恕，咒着德米特里，扬言要立刻收拾他。可雅沙找不着他。德米特里得信败露，从阿姆加永远消失了。

===Original===

Soon afterwards, another incident occurred which further embittered my life with Yasha. In his absence Vasili, a political, came and told me that the authorities were in receipt of an order to arrest and send him to Irkutsk to be tried on a new charge, which carried with it the death-sentence. It was a regular practice of the Tsar’s government to recall exiles for second trials on some additional bit of evidence.
Vasili asked me to lend him our horse, “Maltchik,” to help him escape. Knowing how attached Yasha was to the horse, I refused Vasili’s request. But he persisted in imploring me, claiming that Prince Gutemurov had seen the order for the arrest, and that the sheriff was already on his tracks.
“But how could the horse be returned?” I asked Vasili, touched by his continuous pleading. He replied that he would leave it with a certain Yakut friend of ours, some hundred versts away, and I finally yielded, although not without misgivings. As soon as he left with “Maltchik” my anxiety grew into alarm. I hurried to Prince Gutemurov to verify Vasili’s story. How thunderstruck I was upon learning from the Prince that he knew of no order to arrest Vasili, and that he had not even seen him. It was clear that I had been swindled and that I would never see the horse again.
“My God!” I thought, “what will happen upon Yasha’s return and his discovery that ‘Maltchik’ is gone?”
The fear of death rose up before me, the impression of my recent escape from hanging still fresh in my mind. I trembled at the thought of Yasha, with the feeling of an entrapped animal seeking an escape. But there seemed to be no remedy.
It was August, 1914. Rumours of the great conflict were just reaching the remote Siberian provinces. The order for mobilization came, and there was great excitement, even in the death-bound Arctic settlements, as if suddenly a new life had been infused into that land of monotony. Upon the heels of the call to arms came the Tsar’s Manifesto, abolishing the scourge of our national life﻿—vodka, and with it a gigantic wave of popular enthusiasm, sweeping the steppes, valleys, and forests of vast Russia, from Petrograd and Moscow, across the Ural mountains and Siberia, to the borders of China, and the Pacific coast.
There was something sublime about the nation’s response.

===Chinese===

此后不久又出一桩事，叫我同雅沙的日子更苦。他不在家时，政治犯瓦西里来告诉我：当局接了命令，要逮捕他、解往伊尔库茨克受新罪的审判——那罪是要掉头的。沙皇政府惯用这一手：凭一点新添的证据，把流放犯召回重审。

瓦西里求我把我们的马「马利奇克」借给他逃命。深知雅沙多爱那匹马，我回绝了。可他一再哀求，说古捷穆罗夫亲王亲眼见过那道逮捕令，县警已经追在他身后了。

「可马怎么还回来呢？」我被他缠得心软，问。他说他把它留在我们熟识的一个雅库特朋友那里，离此约一百俄里。我终于依了，心里到底不踏实。他牵着「马利奇克」一走，我的焦灼涨成了惊惶。我赶去古捷穆罗夫亲王家对质。亲王说他根本不知道有什么逮捕瓦西里的命令，连人也没见过。这才明白：我受骗了，马再也见不着了。

「我的天，」我想，「雅沙回来，发觉『马利奇克』没了，会出什么事？」

死的恐惧立在我面前——不久前那回逃出绞索的印象还新。一想到雅沙我就发抖，像一头落进陷阱的兽，四处寻活路。可似乎无路可走。

那是1914年8月。大战的风声刚刚传到偏远的西伯利亚各省。动员令下来了，连那些死气沉沉的北极流放地都轰动起来，仿佛那片单调的土地忽然灌进了一股新生命。征兵令接踵而至的，是沙皇的诏书：废除我们民族生活的祸根——伏特加；随之而来一股全民的热潮，席卷辽阔俄国的草原、河谷和森林——从彼得格勒和莫斯科，越过乌拉尔山和西伯利亚，直到中国边界和太平洋岸。

这个民族的回应里有一种崇高的东西。

===Original===

Old men, who had fought in the Crimean War, in the Turkish Campaign of 1877﻿–﻿78, and The Russo-Japanese War, declared that they never saw such exaltation of spirit. It was a glorious, inspiring, unforgettable moment in one’s life. My soul was deeply stirred, and I had a dim realization of a new world coming to life, a purer, a happier and a holier world.
And when Vasili robbed me of our horse, and I was filled with the dread of Yasha’s fury, intensified by my helplessness in the face of this misfortune, the thought, “**War**!” suddenly flashed into my mind.
“Go to war to help save the country!” a voice within me called.
To leave Yasha for my personal comfort and safety was almost unthinkable. But to leave him for the field of unselfish sacrifice, that was a different matter. And the thought of going to war penetrated deeper and deeper into my whole being, giving me no rest.
When Yasha returned, Prince Gutemurov and several other friends were in the house ready to defend me. He had already learned from the natives, on his way home, that Vasili had escaped on our horse. He could not believe that I would have given his favourite horse to anybody without his permission, and he therefore suspected that I had an intrigue with Vasili, and that I had despatched him to make preparations for an elopement. He made a violent scene, attacking me savagely, with showers of blows. My friends tore him away, which only infuriated him the more. This inability to give vent to his rage made him act like one demented.
His temper was clearly becoming a danger, which called for a remedy. A physician came to Amga only once a month. As Yasha considered himself in good health, there could be no question of suggesting to him that he should consult the physician. It was, therefore, agreed among my friends that Prince Gutemurov should take a walk about the village with the doctor when he arrived, pass by our house as if by accident, and that I should greet them with an invitation to come in for tea. Everything went smoothly. The physician was introduced to Yasha and immediately remarked upon his pallor and his bloodshot eyes.
“What ails you?” he asked Yasha, “you seem to have fever. Let me examine you.”
The result of the examination was the advice to Yasha to go to a hospital for treatment, which he, of course, scoffed at.

===Chinese===

打过克里米亚战争、打过1877-78年土耳其战役、打过日俄战争的老人们都说，从没见过这样的昂扬。那是一个人一生里光荣的、叫人奋起、终身难忘的时刻。我的灵魂深深震动了；我隐隐觉出一个新世界在降生——一个更纯洁、更幸福、更神圣的世界。

而就在瓦西里骗走我们的马、我被对雅沙暴怒的恐惧攥住、又被这灾祸面前的无助加深的时候，「**打仗**！」这个念头，忽然闪进我心里。

「去打仗，帮着救国！」我心里有个声音在唤。

为了个人的安逸和安全离开雅沙，那几乎想不得。可为了无私牺牲的战场离开他，那是另一回事。去打仗的念头越钻越深，钻进我的整个存在，叫我不得安生。

雅沙回来时，古捷穆罗夫亲王和几位朋友正守在屋里护我。他一路已从当地人嘴里听说瓦西里骑我们的马跑了。他不能相信我不经他允许会把心爱的马给任何人，便疑心我同瓦西里有私，打发他先去打点、准备私奔。他大闹一场，拳脚雨点似的落在我身上。朋友们把他拖开，这更激得他发狂。这股子发泄不出的怒，弄得他像个疯子。

他的火性分明成了危险，得想个法子。医生一个月才来阿姆加一回。雅沙自认身子结实，劝他去看医生是提也不必提。于是朋友们商定：等医生到时，由古捷穆罗夫亲王陪他在村里散步，装作顺路经过我们家，我出面招呼他们进来喝茶。一切顺当。医生被介绍给雅沙，当即说起他脸色发白、眼里带血丝。

「你怎么了？」他问雅沙，「像是在发烧。让我检查检查。」

检查的结果，是劝雅沙去医院治一治；他当然嗤之以鼻。

===Original===

Privately, the doctor informed Prince Gutemurov that Yasha’s nerves had broken down and that he was dangerous to live with, as he might kill me for some trivial cause. The physician urged that I should leave him at once. But I hesitated. Another quarrel, however, was not long in coming. Yasha actually made another attempt to kill me, but was stopped by our comrades. The cup was full. I decided to escape.
Day and night my imagination carried me to the fields of battle, and my ears rang with the groans of my wounded brethren. The impact of the mighty armies was heard even in uncivilized northern Siberia. There were rumours in the air, rumours of victory and of defeat, and in low voices people talked of torrents of blood and of rivers of maimed humanity, streaming back from the front, and already overflowing into the Siberian plains. My heart yearned to be there, in the seething cauldron of war, to be baptized in its fire and scorched in its lava. The spirit of sacrifice took possession of me. My country called me. And an irresistible force from within impelled me.
I only waited the opportunity when Yasha should be away for several days. It arrived one September day. Some Yakuts called for Yasha. As soon as he left I cut off my hair, dressed in men’s clothes and provided myself with two loaves of bread. I had no money to speak of, as I took none of the colony into my confidence.
It was evening when I stealthily hurried out of Amga and took the road to Yakutsk. I had before me a journey of over 130 miles. I ran at such a pace that night, since I could not expect to travel in the daytime without being recognized, that I covered thirty-three miles before dawn.
Several times I met Yakuts, and answered their greetings in their native dialect, with which I had grown familiar. In the dark they must have taken me for a Yakut. Otherwise, the journey was uneventful. The road was dry, the weather calm, and only the stars lit my way, while the loud throbbing of my heart echoed my footsteps.
When day broke I stopped beside a stream and breakfasted on bread and cold water. I then made a bed of twigs in a hole by the road, lay down, covered myself with branches and went to sleep for the day. I awoke when evening came, offered my prayers to God, dined on some more bread and water, and resumed my journey.

===Chinese===

私下里，医生告诉古捷穆罗夫亲王：雅沙的神经垮了，同他一起生活很危险，他可能为一点小事就杀了我。医生催我立刻离开他。可我犹豫。然而没等多久又吵了一场，雅沙果真又动了杀我的手，被同志们拦下。杯满了。我决意逃走。

白天黑夜，我的想象把我带到战场，耳朵里响着负伤同胞的呻吟。大军相撞的声浪，连蛮荒的北西伯利亚都听得见。风里满是传言——胜的、败的；人们低声讲着血流成河、残废的人流从前线涌回、已经漫进西伯利亚平原。我的心渴望去那里，去战争那口滚沸的大锅里，受它的火洗，受它的熔岩焦。牺牲的精神攫住了我。祖国在唤我。一股发自内里的、不可抗拒的力推着我。

我只等雅沙外出几天的机会。九月的一天，机会来了。几个雅库特人来叫雅沙。他一走，我铰了头发，换上男人的衣裳，带上两个面包。钱几乎一点没有——殖民地的人我一个也没有告知。

天擦黑，我悄悄出了阿姆加，上了去雅库茨克的路。摆在我面前的是一百三十多英里的旅程。因为白天赶路免不了被认出来，那一夜我拼命地赶，天亮前走了三十三英里。

路上几回遇见雅库特人，我用熟了的他们的土话应他们的招呼。黑暗里，他们必把我当成了雅库特人。除此之外一路无事。路面干，天色静，只有星星照路，而我的心大声的跳动，应着我的脚步。

天亮时，我在一条小溪边停下，就着冷水吃面包当早饭。随后在路旁一个坑里铺了树枝作床，躺下，拿枝子盖住，睡了白天一觉。傍晚醒来，向上帝祷告，又吃些面包喝水，重新上路。

===Original===

It took me six nights of walking to arrive at Yakutsk, living only on bread and water, and sleeping in hidden nooks by the road during the day.
There was a new Governor in Yakutsk. Baron Kraft had gone to western Europe to join his wife at some health resort, was stranded there after the outbreak of the war, and later died a prisoner in the hands of the enemy. The new Governor received me well, and granted my request to be sent home, to Tomsk, at the expense of the Government. He even offered me a convoy for protection.
My escape was a success, but my heart would not rejoice. The image of Yasha, stricken with grief, frantically searching for me, calling to me, rose before my eyes, and demanded an account from my conscience. Was it right, was it just, to leave poor Yasha all alone in forlorn Amga? Had I not vowed to remain eternally faithful to him? Was it not my bounden duty to stand by him to the end? Should I not return to him, then and give up this wild fancy of going to war?
I hesitated. Was it not true, on the other hand, that Yasha had become a professional gambler? Was not life with him a perilous adventure? Devotion to Yasha, a voice within me argued, did not mean perishing with him, but an effort to save him. Indeed, to get Yasha out of that wilderness was an idea which suddenly gripped my imagination. And how could I ever expect to find a better opportunity to do so than by distinguishing myself in war and then petitioning the Tsar in his behalf?
So there I was again in the magic circle of war. I asked an acquaintance to write a letter for me to Yasha. Apologizing for my strange departure, I informed him that I was going to Tomsk to enlist as a soldier, leave for the front and win distinction for bravery, then petition the Tsar to pardon him, so as to enable us to resume our peaceful life in Stretinsk.
It was a plan with which Destiny, which held no more peace for me, played havoc. The war was to continue as many years as I had expected it to last months, shrouding Russia in darkness, sowing revolution, bearing thunder and lightning in its wings, spreading famine and chaos and seeds of a new world order. In those stormy years Yasha was to retreat to the far background of my life, then vanish altogether.

===Chinese===

走了六个夜晚才到雅库茨克，一路只吃面包喝水，白天睡在路旁藏人的坑凹里。

雅库茨克换了省长。克拉夫特男爵战事一起，困在了西欧——他去某处疗养地同妻子会合——后来死在敌人手里，做了俘虏。新省长待我很好，准了我自费……由政府出资送回托木斯克老家，甚至提出派一名护兵保护我。

逃是逃成了，我的心却欢不起来。雅沙的形象立在我眼前：他悲恸欲绝，疯了一样找我、唤我，向我的良心讨一个交代。把可怜的雅沙孤零零丢在凄凉的阿姆加，对吗？公道吗？我不是起过誓，永远忠贞于他？陪他到底，难道不是我天经地义的本分？我不该回去，撂下从军这个狂想？

我犹豫了。可另一面不也是实情么：雅沙已经赌成了职业赌徒？同他一起生活，岂不是一场天天悬心的冒险？我心里的一个声音分辩：忠于雅沙，不是陪他一起毁，而是想法救他。真的，把雅沙弄出那片荒野——这个念头忽然攫住了我的想象。而建功战场、再向沙皇为他求赦，世上还能有比这更好的机会么？

于是我又站回了战争那个魔圈。我请一位熟人代笔给雅沙写信：为我不辞而别道歉，告诉他我这就去托木斯克从军，开赴前线，以英勇挣得功名，然后上书沙皇赦他，好教我们回斯特列津斯克重过太平日子。

这是一个被命运——它从此不再给我太平——砸得粉碎的计划。这场仗，要打我原以为只消几个月的那么多年，把俄国罩进黑暗，播下革命，翼下带着雷霆闪电，撒下饥荒、混乱和一个新世界秩序的种子。在那狂风暴雨的年代里，雅沙先是退到我生活的远处，随后整个不见了。

===Original===

But all my heart was with him that autumn day of 1914, when I turned my eyes toward the bleak north for the last time, as I boarded the barge that was to carry me to Irkutsk, thence to Tomsk, and thence to war.

===Chinese===

可1914年那个秋日，我的心整个在他身上：我登上那艘驳船——它要载我去伊尔库茨克，再去托木斯克，再去打仗——最后一次回望那荒凉的北方。

## XVII Facing Lenin and Trotsky / 十七 面对列宁与托洛茨基
===Original===

Petrograd seemed populated by Red Guards. One could not make a step without encountering one. They kept a strict watch over the station and all the incoming and outgoing trains. My escorts left me on the station platform, as they were to return to the front immediately.
I had hardly emerged from the station, intending to look for a cabman, when a Red Guard Commissary, accompanied by a private with a naked sword, stopped me with the polite query:
“Madame Bochkareva?”
“Yes.”
“Will you come with me, please?” he suggested.
“Where?” I asked.
“To the Smolny Institute.”
“But why?”
“Because I have orders to detain all officers returning from the front,” he replied.
“But I am only going home!” I tried to argue.
“Yes, I understand. But as an officer you will also understand that I must obey orders. They will probably release you.”
He hailed a cabman and we drove to the Smolny Institute, the seat of the Bolshevik Government. It impressed me as a strongly garrisoned fortress. There were armed sentries everywhere. Accompanied by Red Guards I was led inside. There were Guards at every desk. I was taken before a sailor. He was very rough and brusque.
“Where are you going?” he demanded curtly.
“I am going home, to a village near Tomsk,” I replied.
“Then why are you armed?” he sneered.
“Because I am an officer, and this is my uniform,” I answered.
He blazed up.
“An officer, eh? You will be an officer no more. Give me that pistol and sword!” he ordered.
The arms were those given to me at the consecration of the flag of the Battalion. I prized them too much to hand them over to this rogue of a sailor, and I refused to comply with his demand. He grew furious. It would have been useless to resist as the room was full of Red Guards. I declared that if he wanted my arms he could take them, but I would never surrender them myself.
He violently tore the pistol and sword from me and pronounced me under arrest. There was a dark cellar in the Institute which was used as a place of detention, and I was sent down there and locked up. I was hungry, but received no answer to all my calls, and remained in the hole till the following morning. As soon as I was brought upstairs I began to demand my arms. The various officials, however, remained deaf to my pleas.

===Chinese===

彼得格勒仿佛满城都是赤卫队，挪一步就撞见一个。他们对车站和所有进出列车严加监视。送我的人把我留在站台上——他们得即刻返回前线。
我刚出车站，正要找马车，一名赤卫队政委，身后跟着一个挎出鞘军刀的列兵，客客气气一句问话拦住了我：
「博奇卡列娃太太？」
「是。」
「请跟我来一趟，好吗？」他提议。
「去哪里？」我问。
「斯莫尔尼宫（Smolny Institute）。」
「可为什么？」
「我奉命扣留所有从前线回来的军官。」他答。
「可我只是回家呀！」我争辩。
「是，我明白。可你是军官，也该明白我得服从命令。多半会放你走的。」
他叫来一辆马车，我们驶往斯莫尔尼宫——布尔什维克政府的所在。它给我的印象是一座戒备森严的堡垒，处处是武装哨兵。赤卫队押着我走进去，每张桌子旁都有卫兵。我被带到一个水兵面前。他粗鲁得很，没好气。
「到哪里去？」他生硬地问。
「回家，回托木斯克附近的一个村子。」我答。
「那你还带武器？」他冷笑。
「因为我是军官，这是我的军装。」我答。
他炸了。
「军官，嗯？你这军官当到头了。把手枪和马刀给我！」他下令。
那套武器是营旗祝圣礼上授给我的，我珍爱万分，怎肯交给这个无赖水兵，便拒绝照办。他勃然大怒。屋里满是赤卫队，反抗也无益。我声明：他想要，可以来夺；要我自己交，办不到。
他动手从我身上硬扯下手枪和马刀，宣布我被捕了。宫里有一间黑牢，用作拘押处，我被押下去锁了进去。我饿着，喊破了喉咙也无人应，在那个坑里一直待到第二天早晨。一被提上楼，我就开始索要我的武器。可那些官员对我的恳求一概装聋。

===Original===

I was informed that I should be taken before Lenin and Trotsky, and was soon led into a large, light room where two men of contrasting appearance were seated, apparently expecting my entrance. One had a typical Russian face. The other looked Jewish. The first was Nikolai Lenin, the second Leon Trotsky. Both arose as I stepped in and walked toward me a few steps, stretching out their hands and greeting me courteously.
Lenin apologized for my arrest, explaining that he had learned of it only that morning. Inviting me to a seat, the two Bolshevik chiefs complimented me upon my record of service and courage, and began to sketch to me the era of happiness that they intended to procure for Russia. They talked simply, smoothly and very beautifully. It was for the common people, the toiling masses, the disinherited that they were fighting. They wanted justice for all. Wasn’t I of the working class myself? Yes, I was. Wouldn’t I join them and cooperate with their party in bringing happiness to the oppressed peasant and workman? They wanted peasant women like myself: they had the highest esteem for them.
“You will bring Russia not to happiness but to ruin,” I said.
“Why?” they asked. “We seek only what is good and right. The people are with us. You saw for yourself that the army is behind us.”
“I will tell you why,” I replied. “I have no objection to your beautiful plans for the future of Russia. But as for the immediate situation, if you take the soldiers away from the front, you are destroying the country.”
“But we do not want any more war. We are going to conclude peace,” the two leaders replied.
“How can you conclude peace without soldiers at the front? You are demobilizing the army already. You have got to make peace first and then let the men go home. I myself want peace, but if I were in the trenches I would never leave before peace had been signed. What you are doing will ruin Russia.”
“We are sending the soldiers away because the Germans will not advance against us, anyhow. They do not want to fight either,” was the reply.
It irritated me, this view of the Germans held by the men who now controlled the Government of my country.
“You don’t know the Germans!” I cried out. “We have lost so many lives in this war, and now you would give everything away without a struggle! You don’t know war!

===Chinese===

随后通知我：将被带去见列宁和托洛茨基。很快我被领进一间宽敞明亮的屋子，两个相貌迥异的人生在那里，显然正候着我进来。一个典型的俄国脸，另一个看去像犹太人。前者是尼古拉·列宁（Nikolai Lenin），后者是列昂·托洛茨基（Leon Trotsky）。我一进门，两人都起身，朝我迎了几步，伸出手来，殷勤致意。
列宁为我的被捕道了歉，解释说他是当天早晨才得知的。两位布尔什维克首领请我落座，夸赞我的战功和胆气，随即开始给我描画他们打算为俄国谋来的幸福时代。他们讲得朴素、流畅，而且非常动听。他们打仗，是为了平民，为了劳苦大众，为了被剥夺的人们。他们要人人得公道。我自己不就是劳动阶级吗？是。我不愿同他们合作、同他们的党一起给受压的庄稼人和工人带去幸福吗？他们要的正是我这样的农妇——她们在他们眼里地位最尊贵。
「你们带给俄国的不是幸福，是毁灭。」我说。
「为什么？」他们问。「我们求的只有善和正义。人民站在我们一边。军队拥护我们，你自己也看见了。」
「我告诉你们为什么。」我答。「你们给俄国未来画的那些美妙蓝图，我没有异议。可眼下的事是明摆着的：你们把士兵从前线撤走，就是在毁掉国家。」
「可我们不要再打仗了。我们就要缔结和约。」两位领袖答。
「前线没有士兵，你们拿什么缔结和约？你们已经在解散军队了。总得先缔了和，再放人回家。我自己也要和平，可我若还在战壕里，和约没签字，我绝不离开。你们这样做要毁掉俄国。」
「我们放士兵走，是因为德国人横竖不会朝我们推进。他们也不想打了。」回答是这样。
如今执掌我国政府的人，竟这样看德国人——这叫我火起。
「你们不认得德国人！」我喊出来。「这场战争我们死了多少人，如今你们竟要一枪不放把一切都送掉！你们不懂得战争！

===Original===

Take the soldiers away from the front and the Germans will come and seize upon everything they can lay hands on. This is war. I am a soldier and I know. But you don’t. Why did you take it upon yourselves to rule the country? You will ruin it!” I exclaimed in anguish.
Lenin and Trotsky laughed. I could see the irony in their eyes. They were learned and worldly. They had written books and travelled in foreign lands. And who was I? An illiterate Russian peasant woman. My lecture undoubtedly afforded them amusement. They smiled condescendingly at my suggestion that they did not know what war was in reality.
I rejected their proposal to cooperate with them and asked if I were free to leave. One of them rang a bell and a Red Guard entered. He was requested to accompany me out of the room and to provide me with a passport and a free ticket to Tomsk. Before leaving I asked for my arms, but was refused. I explained that they were partly of gold and given to me on an occasion that rendered them almost priceless to me. They answered that I would receive them back as soon as order was restored. Of course, I never got them back.
I left the room without saying goodbye. In the next room I was given a passport, and proceeded by tramcar to the station. I decided not to linger in Petrograd and to depart without even seeing any of my friends. On the way I was recognized everywhere, but was allowed to proceed unmolested. The same evening I boarded one of the three cars attached to a train that went to Irkutsk by way of Vologda and Chelyabinsk. I was going home. With me I had some two thousand roubles (about £211 2s. 3d.), saved during my command of the Battalion, when I had received a salary of four hundred roubles (about £42 4s. 5d.) a month.
The train was overcrowded with returning soldiers, almost all ardent Bolsheviks. I remained in the compartment for eight days, leaving it only occasionally at night. I sent out a companion passenger to buy food for me at the stations. As we neared Chelyabinsk, at the end of the eight days, the crowd had diminished in number, and I thought, I might safely go out on to the gangway and get off at the great station for a little walk. No sooner did I appear on the gangway than I was recognized by some soldiers.
“Oh, look who is here!” one exclaimed.
“It’s Bochkareva! The harlot!” a couple of others echoed.
“She ought to be killed!”

===Chinese===

把士兵从前线撤走，德国人就会来，把够得着的一切都抓到手里。这就是战争。我是军人，我懂。你们不懂。你们凭什么自任来统治国家？你们要毁了它！」我痛心地喊。
列宁和托洛茨基笑了。我看得出他们眼里的讥消。他们有学问，见过世面，写过书，出过洋。我算什么？一个不识字的俄国农妇。我这一课，想必给他们添了乐子。我说他们不懂得战争实际是什么，他们居高临下地朝我微笑。
我回绝了合作的提议，问可否自由离去。其中一位摇铃，进来一名赤卫队，奉命送我出屋，并给我办一张护照和一张去托木斯克的免费车票。临走前我讨要我的武器，被拒绝了。我说明那武器一部分是金的，授给我的场合使它们在我眼里近乎无价。他们答道：秩序一恢复便还我。当然，我再也没有见到它们。
我出了那间屋子，连再见也没说。在隔壁屋里领了护照，便乘电车去车站。我决定不在彼得格勒逗留，连朋友们也不见一面就走。一路上处处有人认出我，倒也没人纠缠。当晚我上了火车挂的三节车厢之一——那趟车取道沃洛格达和车里雅宾斯克去伊尔库茨克。我回家了。随身带着约两千卢布（约合211镑2先令3便士），是统带妇女营时省下的——那时我月薪四百卢布（约合42镑4先令5便士）。
车里挤满了返乡的士兵，几乎全是狂热的布尔什维克。我在包厢里待了八天，只在夜里偶尔出去一下，托同车的人到站台上替我买吃的。第八天临近车里雅宾斯克时，人少了些，我心想：到过道上去透透气、到大站台上走一走，总该安全了。谁知我一在过道露面，就有士兵认出了我。
「哟，瞧这是谁！」一个喊。
「是博奇卡列娃！那破鞋！」另外几个随声附和。
「该杀了她！」

===Original===

shouted somebody.
“Why?” I turned on them. “What harm have I done to you? Oh, you fools, fools!”
The train slowed down, approaching the station. I had scarcely turned my head away from the insolent fellows, when I was suddenly lifted by two pairs of arms, swung to and fro once, twice, three times, and thrown off the moving train.
Fortunately the momentum of the swinging was so great that I was thrown across the parallel tracks and landed in a bank of snow piled along the railway. It was the end of November, 1917. It was all so sudden that the laughter of the brutes behind me still rang in my ears as I became conscious of pain in my right knee.
The train was halted before pulling into the station. In a few moments a big crowd collected round me, composed of passengers, railway officials and others. All were indignant at the brutality of the soldiers. The Commandant of the station and members of the local committee hurried to the spot. I was placed on a stretcher and taken to the hospital. It was found that I had a dislocated knee, and my leg was bandaged. I then declared that I desired to continue the journey, and I was given a berth in a hospital coach attached to a train going east. There were attendants and a medical assistant on the car.
My injured leg grew more and more painful as I proceeded homeward. It began to swell, and the medical assistant telegraphed to the stationmaster of Tutalsk, the village in which my family now lived, to provide a stretcher for me.
My sister, Arina, was employed at the station as attendant at the tea-urn, which is always kept boiling at Russian railway stations. It was this employment of hers that had caused the family to move to Tutalsk from Tomsk, where they had no means of livelihood whatever. When the message from the doctor in charge of the car reached my sister and through her my parents, there was an outburst of grief. It was three years since they had seen their Marusia and now she was apparently being brought to them on her deathbed!
On the fourth day of the journey from Chelyabinsk the train stopped at Tutalsk. My leg was badly swollen and was as heavy as a log. The pains were agonizing. My face was deadly pale.
A stretcher was prepared for me at the station. My sisters, my mother and father and the stationmaster were at the door of the coach when I was carried out.

===Chinese===

不知谁喊了一声。
「为什么？」我朝他们反回去。「我害过你们什么？唉，你们这些傻子，傻子！」
火车减速进站。我刚把头从那几个无赖那边转开，忽然被四条胳膊抬了起来，前后悠了一下、两下、三下，扔下了行进中的火车。
幸而悠出去的势头极大，我飞过并行的几条铁轨，落在铁路边堆着的一垛雪里。那是一九一七年十一月底。一切来得那样快，等我觉出右膝的疼痛，身后那些畜生的笑声还在耳边响。
火车没进站便停住了。不一刻，一大群人围拢来，有旅客、铁路员工和其他人，人人愤慨于士兵的暴行。站长和地方委员会的委员们赶到。我被放上担架送进医院，查出膝关节脱臼，腿上了绷带。我随即声明要继续赶路，便被安置在东行列车加挂的医护车厢的一个铺位上，车上有勤务员和一名医士。
回家的路越走，伤腿越疼，肿了起来。医士给图塔利斯克（Tutalsk）站长拍了电报——我一家如今就住在那个村子——请他备一副担架。
我姐姐阿林娜（Arina）在站上管茶炊——俄国车站的茶炊总是滚着的。全家人在托木斯克本来生计无着，靠她这份差事才搬到了图塔利斯克。车上医士的电报一传到我姐姐、又通过她传到父母那里，家里顿时哭声一片。三年没见他们的玛鲁霞了，如今她竟像是要被抬着回来等死！
从车里雅宾斯克上路的第四天，火车停在图塔利斯克。我的腿肿得厉害，沉得像根木头，疼得钻心，脸白得像纸。
站上为我备好了担架。我被抬出来时，姐姐们、母亲父亲和站长都在车厢门口。

===Original===

My mother shrieked in heartrending tones, “My Marusia! My Manka!” stretched her hands toward heaven and threw herself full length on me, mourning over me as if I were ready for burial.
Her prodigal daughter had returned, my mother sobbed, but in what a condition! She thought that I must have been wounded and have asked to be sent home to die. I could not speak, I could only grasp her bony arms, as my throat was choked with a tempest of tears and sobs. Everybody was crying, my sisters calling me by caressing names, my father standing over me bent and white, and even the strange stationmaster.﻿ ﻿…
I became hysterical and the doctor was sent for. He had me removed home immediately, promising in response to my mother’s entreaties to do everything in his power for me. I was ill for a month, passing Christmas and meeting the New Year, 1918, in bed.
The two thousand roubles I had saved I gave to my parents. But this sum, which would have been reckoned a fortune before the war, was barely sufficient to keep us for a few months. It cost nearly a hundred roubles (about £10 11s.) to buy a pair of shoes for my youngest sister, Nadia, who was going about barefoot! It cost almost twice as much to buy her a secondhand jacket at the Tomsk market. Manufactured goods sold at a premium when they were to be had, but it was much more difficult to find what one needed than to pay an exorbitant price for it. There was plenty of flour in the country. But the peasants would not sell it cheaply because they could get nothing in town for less than fifty or a hundred times its former price. The result was that flour sold at sixty roubles (about £6 6s. 8d.) a pood (thirty-two pounds)! It may be imagined how far two thousand roubles would carry one in Russia.
Tutalsk had also been swept by the hurricane of Bolshevism. There were many soldiers who had returned from the front imbued with Bolshevik teachings. Just before my arrival the newly-fledged heretics even burned the village church, to the great horror of the older inhabitants. It was not an unusual case; it was typical of the time. Hundreds of thousands of deluded young men had returned from the trenches with the passion to destroy, to tear down everything that had existed before: the old system of Government, the church, nay, God Himself﻿—all in preparation for the new order of life they were going to establish.

===Chinese===

母亲一声撕裂人心的哭喊：「我的玛鲁霞！我的曼卡！」双手举向天，整个人扑伏在我身上，哭得像我已经入殓。
浪女回头了，母亲抽泣着说——可成了什么样子！她想我必是受了伤，求了人送回家来等死的。我说不出话，只能攥住她那双瘦骨嶙峋的胳膊，喉咙被一场泪水的风暴堵死。人人都在哭：姐姐们一声声唤着我的小名，父亲佝偻着站在我上方，脸白得吓人，连那素不相识的站长也哭了。…
我歇斯底里起来，人们去请医生。他当即把我挪回家去，又应着母亲的哀求，答应竭尽全力。我病了一个月，圣诞节在病床上度过，一九一八年新年也是在床上迎来的。
省下的那两千卢布，我交给了父母。可这笔战前要算一笔财产的钱，只够全家撑几个月。给最小的妹妹娜佳买一双鞋——她一直光着脚走来走去！——花了将近一百卢布（约合10镑11先令）；在托木斯克市场上给她买一件旧外套，花的钱几乎是鞋的两倍。工业品要是有货，卖得天价；可比起付天价，更难的是寻得到要买的东西。乡下面粉本不缺，可庄稼人不肯贱卖——进城买什么都得花从前五十倍、一百倍的价。结果是面粉卖到六十卢布（约合6镑6先令8便士）一普特（三十二磅）！两千卢布在俄国能顶多久，可想而知。
图塔利斯克也被布尔什维主义的飓风扫过。许多士兵从前线回来，满脑子布尔什维克的道理。就在我到家前不久，那伙新出炉的离经叛道者竟烧了村里的教堂，吓得老一辈魂飞魄散。这不是个别的事，是那个时代的常态。成千上万受了蛊惑的年轻人从战壕里回来，满腔的破坏欲，要拆毁先前存在过的一切：旧的政体，教会，乃至上帝本身——全为了给他们要建立的新秩序腾地方。

===Original===

But one institution﻿—the scourge of the nation﻿—they failed to wipe out. Nay more, they restored it. The Tsar had abolished vodka. The prohibition was continued in force by the new regime, but only on paper. Nearly every returned soldier took to distilling vodka at home, and the old plague of the country recovered its power and took its part in the building of the Bolsheviks’ new world.
Every town and village had its committee or Soviet. They were supposed to carry out orders from the Central Government. An order was issued to confiscate all articles of gold and silver. Committees searched every house for such belongings. There was, also, or was supposed to be, an order taxing furniture and clothes. When the taxes arbitrarily demanded were not paid, the furniture and clothes were taken away.
In the towns it was the townsmen who suffered, in the villages the peasants, all under the pretext of confiscating the riches of the bourgeoisie. It was sufficient for a peasant to buy a new overcoat, perhaps with his last savings, for him to be branded as an exploiter and lose his precious garment. The peculiar thing about such cases was the fact that the confiscated article would almost invariably appear on the back of one of the Bolshevik ringleaders. It was merely looting, and the methods were pure terrorism, practised mostly by the returned soldiers.
I received some letters at Tutalsk. One was from my adjutant, Princess Tatuyeva, who had arrived safely in Tiflis, her native town.
One morning I went to the post office to ask for letters.
“There goes Bochkareva!” I heard a man cry out.
“Ah, Bochkareva! She is for the old regime!” another fellow replied, apparently one of the Bolshevik soldiers.
There were several of them and they shouted threats and insults at me. I did not reply but returned home with a heavy heart. Even in my own home I was not safe.
“My God,” I prayed, “what has come over the Russian people? Is this my reward for the sacrifices I have made for my country?”
I resolved not to leave the house again. Surely this madness would not last long, I thought. I spent most of the day reading the Bible and praying to Heaven for the awakening and enlightenment of my people.
On the 7th of January, 1918, I received a telegram from Petrograd, signed by General X. It read:

> Come. You are needed.

===Chinese===

可有一样东西——民族的大祸——他们没有除掉。非但没有，还给它复了辟。沙皇废了伏特加。新政权把禁令照旧写在纸上，也只是写在纸上。返乡的士兵几乎家家私酿伏特加，这个国家的老瘟疫重新得了势，在布尔什维克的新世界的营造里有了一席之地。
每个城镇乡村都有自己的委员会或苏维埃，理应执行中央政府的命令。一道命令下来：没收一切金银器物。委员会挨家搜查。还有一道——或说传闻有一道——给家具和衣服课税的命令。蛮横索要的税交不出来，家具衣服便被抬走。
城里倒霉的是市民，乡下倒霉的是庄稼人——全都打着没收资产阶级财货的旗号。庄稼人哪怕用最后的积蓄买一件新大衣，也够得上「剥削者」的罪名，大衣便没了。这类案子有一桩怪处：被没收的东西，几乎必落到某个布尔什维克头目的背上。这不过是抢掠，手段是纯粹的恐怖，行凶的多半是返乡的士兵。
在图塔利斯克我收到几封信。一封是我的副官塔图耶娃公主写来的，她已平安抵达故乡第弗利斯（Tiflis）。
一天早晨我去邮局取信。
「博奇卡列娃来了！」我听见一个人喊。
「啊，博奇卡列娃！她是旧政权的！」另一个接口——看样子是布尔什维克士兵中的一个。
他们有好几个，朝我又骂又吓。我没有回嘴，心事重重地回了家。在自己家里，我也不安全了。
「我的上帝，」我祷告，「俄罗斯人这是中了什么邪？我为国家作的那些牺牲，就落得这个报答？」
我决意再不出门。这疯狂总不会长久的，我想。一天里我多半时候在读圣经，求上天唤醒我的民族、照亮他们。
一九一八年一月七日，我收到彼得格勒拍来的一封电报，署名是某将军（General X）。电文是：

> 来吧。需要你。

===Original===

The same day I bought a ticket for the capital, bade farewell to my family, and set out. I removed the epaulets from my uniform, thus appearing in the garb of a private.
About this time the Germans, to the profound shock of the revolutionary masses, began their sudden advance into Russia. It had an almost miraculous effect on the Bolshevik sympathizers. The train was as usual packed with soldiers, but there was a noticeable difference in their expression and conversation. All the braggadocio had been knocked out of them by the enemy’s action. They had been lulled into the sweet belief that peace had come and that a golden age was about to open for them. They could not reconcile that with the swift advance of the Kaiser’s soldiers toward Petrograd and Moscow.
It was refreshing, exhilarating to listen to some of the men.
“We have been sold!” one heard here and there.
“We were told that the German soldiers would not advance if we left the front,” was another frequent expression.
“It is not the common people, it is the German bourgeoisie that is fighting us now,” was an argument ordinarily given in answer to the first opinions, “and there is nothing to be afraid of. There will soon be a revolution in Germany.”
“Who knows,” some would doubtfully remark, “that Lenin and Trotsky have not delivered us into the hands of the accursed Germans?”
There were always delegates from local committees going somewhere, and they talked to the soldiers, answering questions and explaining things. They could not very well explain away the German treachery, but they held out the promise of a revolution in Germany almost any day. The men listened but were not greatly impressed by the assurances of the agitators. One felt that they were still groping in the dark, although the light was dawning on their minds. The awakening could not be long postponed.
I had a safe and comfortable journey to Petrograd. Nobody molested me, nobody threatened my life. I arrived at the capital on the 18th of January. The station was not as strongly guarded as two months before. Red Guards were not in such evidence in the streets, which appeared more normal. I went to one of my former patronesses and learned of the terror in which the capital lived.
The following day I called on General X, who greeted me cordially. Kiev, he told me, had just been captured by the Germans.

===Chinese===

当天我买了去首都的车票，辞别家人，动身了。我摘去军装上的肩章，扮作一个列兵。
大约这时，德国人突然向俄国推进，革命群众为之大骇。这对布尔什维克的同情者产生了几近奇迹的效力。火车照例塞满士兵，可他们的脸色和谈吐都显出不同：敌人的这一手把他们的大话全打掉了。他们先前被哄得甜丝丝的——以为和平来了，黄金时代就要为他们开启——如今却同德皇的军队朝彼得格勒和莫斯科疾进的事实对不上账。
听听有些人的话，叫人又提神又痛快。
「我们被卖了！」到处听得见这一句。
「原先说，只要我们撤出前线，德国兵就不会推进。」这也是常听的话。
「如今同我们打的不是老百姓，是德国资产阶级，」——这是通常拿来应答前一种议论的论据——「没什么可怕的。德国很快也要革命。」
「谁知道呢，」有人将信将疑，「列宁和托洛茨基是不是把我们交到了那些该死的德国人手里？」
地方委员会的代表们总是东奔西走，同士兵们讲话，答疑解惑。德国人的背信他们圆不过去，便许愿说德国革命指日可待。士兵们听着，却不大被这些鼓动者的保证打动。谁都觉得他们还在黑地里摸索，虽然光已经照进他们的脑子。醒悟不会拖太久了。
我一路平安顺当到了彼得格勒。没人纠缠，没人威胁性命。我于一月十八日抵达首都。车站不如两个月前戒备森严，街上的赤卫队也少见些，市面像样了些。我去到从前的一位女施主家，得知首都生活在怎样的恐怖里。
第二天我拜见某将军，他热诚地接待我。他告诉我：基辅（Kiev）刚被德国人攻占。

===Original===

They were threatening Petrograd, and the opposition of the Red Guards would not prevent or even postpone its capture by one day if the Germans were bent upon taking the city.
Red Terror was rampant in Petrograd. The river was full of corpses of officers who had been slain and lynched. Those who were alive were leading a wretched existence, fearing to show themselves in public because of the temper of the mob, and therefore on the verge of death from starvation. Even more harrowing was the situation of the country. It was falling into the hands of the enemy so rapidly that immediate action of some sort was imperative.
A secret meeting of officers and sympathizers had been held at which it was decided to get in touch with General Kornilov, who was reported as operating in the Don region. There were so many conflicting reports concerning Kornilov that it had been suggested that a courier should be sent to him to find out definitely his plans and his resources. After an exhaustive discussion General X suggested that I, as a woman, was the only person who could possibly get through the Bolshevik lines and reach Kornilov. Would I go?
“I would not join the officers here or Kornilov in the South for the purpose of waging war against my own people,” I replied. “I can’t do it because every Russian is dear to my heart, whether he be a Bolshevik, a Menshevik, or a Red Guard. But I will undertake to go to Kornilov, in order to satisfy your, as well as my own, desire for information.”
It was agreed that I should dress as a Sister of Mercy. A costume was obtained for me, and I put it on over my uniform. My soldier’s cap I tucked away in a pocket and donned the ordinary headgear of a Sister of Mercy, which left visible only my eyes, nose, mouth and cheeks, and made me look like a matron of about forty-five.
A passport was furnished to me, bearing the name of Alexandra Leontievna Smirnova, which was to be my name on the journey. As I wore army boots there was no danger of my trousers showing under the skirt. I took with me a letter from Princess Tatuyeva, in which she invited me to visit her in her home in the Caucasus. A ticket from Petrograd to Kislovodsk, a Caucasian health resort within several hundred miles of the place where Kornilov was stationed, was given me, to be used only in an emergency.

===Chinese===

他们正威胁着彼得格勒；德国人若决意取城，赤卫队的抵抗连一天也挡不住、迟不了。
红色恐怖在彼得格勒横行。河里漂满了被杀、被私刑处死的军官的尸体。活着的那些过着猪狗不如的日子——暴民的火气叫他们不敢抛头露面，也就离饿死不远了。国家的处境更叫人心焦：它正飞快落入敌手，不能不立即想办法了。
军官和同情者开了一次秘密会议，决计同科尔尼洛夫将军接上头——据报他正在顿河一带活动。关于科尔尼洛夫的传闻彼此矛盾，有人提议派一名信使去他那里，弄清他的计划和实力。详详细细讨论过后，某将军提出：我，作为一个女人，是唯一可能穿过布尔什维克防线到达科尔尼洛夫那里的人。我去不去？
「无论是此地的军官，还是南方的科尔尼洛夫，要同自己人开战，我都不加入，」我答。「我做不到——每一个俄国人在我心里都亲，不管他是布尔什维克、孟什维克，还是赤卫队。但去科尔尼洛夫那里一趟，我担下来——既解你们对消息的渴，也解我自己的。」
议定我扮作仁爱修女。装束弄来了，我把它套在军装外面。士兵帽塞进口袋，戴上修女的寻常头巾——只露出眼睛、鼻子、嘴和脸颊，看上去像个四十五上下的主妇。
发给我的护照上写着：亚历山德拉·列昂季耶夫娜·斯米尔诺娃（Alexandra Leontievna Smirnova）——这将是我在路上的名字。我穿着军靴，裙子底下也不怕露出裤脚。我带上塔图耶娃公主的一封信，信里请我去高加索她家中做客。还有一张彼得格勒到基斯洛沃茨克的车票——那是一处高加索疗养地，离科尔尼洛夫驻地只有几百英里——只备急用。

===Original===

It was agreed that in case of danger I should discard my garb of a Sister of Mercy, and disclose my identity, supported by the evidence of the emergency ticket to Kislovodsk and the letter from Princess Tatuieva, declare that I was on my way to take a cure at that place. In addition, I was, of course, provided with money for expenses.
It was very amusing to lose one’s identity and appear as a complete stranger. I was no longer Maria Bochkareva, but Alexandra Smirnova. And as I glanced at myself in the mirror it seemed even to my own eyes that I had been reincarnated from a soldier into a Sister of Mercy.
When I started from Petrograd my destination was Nikitino, a station which one would ordinarily pass on the way to Kislovodsk. Nobody recognized me on the train. Sometimes a soldier asked:
“Where are you going, little sister?”
“Home, to Kislovodsk,” was my usual answer.
The next question would be about the service I had seen at the front, and the sectors at which I worked. I would reply with facts from my actual experience as a soldier. There was nothing strange about a Sister of Mercy returning home, and as I preferred silence and solitude to conversation, I reached Nikitino, at the end of several days, without any trouble.
From Nikitino all trains were by order of the authorities switched off to other lines and sent to their destination by roundabout routes. The road running directly south from Nikitino was used for military purposes exclusively by the Bolshevik forces engaged in fighting Kornilov. Twenty miles farther on, at Zverevo, the so-called front began. Private passengers were therefore not allowed to go to Zverevo.
It was evident that vast preparations were being made for a campaign against General Kornilov. There were many ammunition trains and large numbers of men concentrated there waiting transportation. There was apparently no lack of money, and there was iron discipline, reminding one of the early days of the war. There was order everywhere.
The first problem confronting me was how to get to Zverevo. I went to the Commandant of the station, complained that I was penniless, that I could not wait indefinitely for the end of the fighting to return home to Kislovodsk, and urgently begged him to advise me what to do. I made such an appeal to him that he finally said:
“A munition train is just about to leave for Zverevo.

===Chinese===

约定：一旦遇险，我便脱掉修女装束，亮明身份，拿去基斯洛沃茨克的应急车票和塔图耶娃公主的信作证，只说自己是去那里疗养的。此外，路费自然也备给了我。
丢掉自己的身份、装成一个彻头彻尾的陌生人，很有几分好玩。我不再是玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃，而是亚历山德拉·斯米尔诺娃。对着镜子照一照，连我自己的眼睛看去，也像是脱胎换骨——从士兵转世成了一位仁爱修女。
从彼得格勒动身时，我的目的地是尼基季诺（Nikitino）——去基斯洛沃茨克寻常要经过的一个站。车上没有人认出我。有时有士兵问：
「小姊妹，上哪儿去？」
「回家，回基斯洛沃茨克。」这是我惯常的答话。
下一个问题多半是我在前线见过怎样的战事、在哪些地段服务过。我便拿自己当兵的真实经历作答。修女返乡没有什么可怪的，何况我宁要清静也不要攀谈，几天之后，安然到了尼基季诺。
从尼基季诺起，所有列车都奉当局之命改道别的线路，绕行赶路。从尼基季诺直下正南的那条铁路，专供同科尔尼洛夫作战的布尔什维克军队军用。再往前二十英里，到兹韦列沃，便是所谓的前线了。因此一般旅客不许去兹韦列沃。
那边显然正大张旗鼓地备战，要讨伐科尔尼洛夫将军。军列一趟趟，大批人马集结待运。钱显然不缺，铁的纪律，倒叫人想起战争初年的光景。处处井然有序。
我面临的头一个难题，是怎么到兹韦列沃去。我去找站长官，诉说自己身无分文，不能无限期等战事结束再回基斯洛沃茨克的家，再三求他指条路。我求的那样恳切，他末了说：
「一列弹药车正要开去兹韦列沃。

===Original===

Come, get into it and go to Zverevo. Perhaps they will pass you through the lines at the front. There is a second-class carriage attached to the train.”
He led me to the carriage, in which were only the five soldiers who were in charge of the train. He introduced me to the chief of them as a stranded Sister of Mercy and asked for their indulgence. I thanked the obliging Commandant profusely and from the bottom of my heart.
The train moved out of the station, but although satisfied with the first stage of my enterprise, I was by no means cheerful as to my prospects in Zverevo, the Bolshevik war zone. The head of the party sat down opposite me. He was a dirty, ugly muzhik. I did not encourage him to engage me in conversation, but he was evidently wholly insensible to my feelings in the matter.
After the preliminary questions, he expressed his surprise that I should have chosen such an inopportune moment to go to Kislovodsk.
“But my mother is ill there,” I lied, “perhaps she is dying now. It broke her heart when I went to the front.”
“Ah, that’s different,” he declared, moving over to my side. “They will pass you in that case.”
From an expression of sympathy he had no hesitation in proceeding to an attempt at flirtation. He moved closer to me and even touched my arm. It was a delicate situation. I could not well afford to provoke his antagonism, so I warded off his advances with a smile and a coquettish glance. He treated me to a good meal, during which the conversation turned to general conditions. He was, of course, a rabid Bolshevik and a savage opponent of Kornilov and all officers. My part in the conversation was confined to brief expressions of acquiescence, till suddenly he asked:
“Have you heard of the Women’s Battalion of Death?”
My heart thumped violently.
“What Battalion did you say?” I asked with an air of ignorance.
“Why, Bochkareva’s Battalion!” he replied in a loud voice.
“Bochkareva’s?” I asked reminiscently. “Oh, yes, Bochkareva; yes, I have heard about her.”
“The —﻿—﻿—! She is a Kornilovka!” he exclaimed. “She is for the old regime.”
“How do you know?” I asked. “I thought she was nonpartisan.”
“We know them all, the counter-revolutionists! She is one of them,” my companion declared emphatically.
“Well, but the Battalion of Death no longer exists, and Bochkareva has apparently vanished,” I suggested.
“Yes, we know how they vanish.

===Chinese===

来，上车，去兹韦列沃吧。兴许前线会放你过去。车上挂了一节二等车厢。」
他把我领到那节车厢，里面只有押车的五个士兵。他把我当作落难的修女介绍给领头那个，请他们多照应。我对这位好心的长官千恩万谢，谢得发自肺腑。
车开出了站。行程的第一步虽然顺当，一想到前面等着我的兹韦列沃——布尔什维克的战区——我实在乐不起来。押车的那位头目在我对面坐下。一个邋遢、丑陋的庄稼汉。我没有同他攀谈的意思，可他显然全觉不出我的冷淡。
例行的问话过后，他表示奇怪：我怎么挑了这样不合时宜的时候去基斯洛沃茨克。
「可我母亲病在那边，」我撒谎道，「说不定这会儿正在咽气。我上前线那阵，她心都碎了。」
「啊，那另说，」他说着，挪到我这边来。「那样他们会放你过去。」
由一句同情话，他毫不迟疑地过渡到动手动脚。他凑得更近，甚至碰我的胳膊。局面微妙。我不能开罪他，便用微笑和含羞的一瞥把他的殷勤挡回去。他请我饱餐了一顿，席间话头转到时局。他当然是个狂热的布尔什维克，科尔尼洛夫和一切军官的死敌。我在交谈里只做简短的应声，直到他突然问：
「听说过妇女敢死营（Women’s Battalion of Death）吗？」
我的心猛跳起来。
「你说哪个营？」我装出一脸茫然。
「嘿，博奇卡列娃的营啊！」他大声答。
「博奇卡列娃的？」我作追想状。「哦，对，博奇卡列娃；嗯，听说过她。」
「那个——！她是个科尔尼洛夫的人！」他嚷。「她是旧政权的。」
「你怎么知道？」我问。「我当她不偏不党呢。」
「反革命分子我们全有数！她就是一伙的。」我的旅伴斩钉截铁。
「可敢死营不是已经散了，博奇卡列娃人也失踪了嘛。」我提了一句。
「是，我们知道他们是怎样失踪的。

===Original===

Many of them have vanished like that. Kornilov had vanished, too. Then they all pop up again somewhere or other and cause trouble,” he declared.
“Now, what would you do to her if she were to pop up here?” I ventured to inquire.
“Kill her. She would never get away alive, you may stake an oath on that,” he assured me. “We have the photographs of all the leading counterrevolutionaries, so that they can’t conceal their identity if they are caught.”
The conversation then took a more profitable turn for me. I learned all about the plans of the Bolshevik force against Kornilov. The arrival of the train at Zverevo put an end to my association with my travelling companion. I thanked him warmly for all his kindness to me.
“You know, Sister,” he unexpectedly declared before parting, “I like you. Will you marry me?”
I was not prepared for this. It rather took me aback. He was such a dirty, repulsive-looking creature, and the proposal was so ludicrous that it was with difficulty that I controlled my desire to laugh. The situation was not one for merriment.
“Yes, with pleasure,” I responded to his offer, with as much graciousness as I could command, “but after I have seen my mother.”
He gave me his address and asked me to write to him, which I promised to do. Perhaps he is still waiting for a letter from me.
I left him at the train and went toward the station. There were Red Guards, sailors, soldiers, even Cossacks, who had joined the Bolsheviks, on the platform and inside the station. But there were no private citizens in sight. I sat down in a corner and waited. I was taken for a nurse attached to the Bolshevik army, and was not molested. One, two, three hours passed and still I could find no opportunity to proceed to my destination. A civilian, who somehow found his way into the station, was placed under arrest before my eyes without any preliminaries. I, therefore, preferred to sit quietly in my corner rather than move about.
Finally a pleasant looking young soldier became interested in me. He walked up and asked:
“Why are you waiting here, Sister?”
“I am waiting for a comrade,” I answered.
“What is his name?” he inquired, interested.
“Oh, that is a secret,” I replied in a teasing manner.
He sat down near me, and asked me if I had worked at the front. I said that unfortunately I had been detailed only to hospitals in the rear.
“Why was that man arrested?”

===Chinese===

他们那样失踪的多了。科尔尼洛夫不也失踪了吗。可他们回头又会在这儿那儿冒出来兴风作浪。」他说。
「喏，她要是在这儿冒出来，你们拿她怎么办？」我大着胆子问。
「杀了她。她休想活着走脱，这个你放得下心。」他向我担保。「头号反革命的照片我们都有，落到手里也甭想瞒住身份。」
随后话头转到一个对我更有利的方向。布尔什维克军队对科尔尼洛夫的全部计划，我听了一个全乎。火车到兹韦列沃，我同旅伴的交道到此为止。我热诚地谢了他一路的关照。
「我说，小姊妹，」临别他突然说，「我喜欢你。嫁给我好吗？」
这我可没防备，着实愣了一下。他那样一个邋遢讨嫌的东西，这提亲又这样滑稽，我好容易才按住笑。可这场合不宜发笑。
「好呀，乐意。」我拿出所能有的和气回应他，「不过得等见过我母亲。」
他给我留了地址，要我写信给他，我也应承了。说不定他如今还在等我的信呢。
我离开他下了车，朝车站走去。站台上、站房里，有赤卫队、水兵、士兵，甚至还有投了布尔什维克的哥萨克，却不见一个平头百姓。我掠了个角落坐下等。人家当我随布尔什维克军队的护士，没有来搅扰。一个钟头、两个钟头、三个钟头过去，我还是找不着前往目的地的机会。一个不知怎么混进站里的平民，当着我的面，没说二话便被扣下了。于是我宁可安安静静坐在角落里，不四处走动。
末了，一个模样讨人喜欢的年轻士兵对我上了心。他走过来问：
「小姊妹，在这儿等什么？」
「等一位同志。」我答。
「他叫什么？」他兴致勃勃地追问。
「哦，这可是个秘密。」我打趣地答。
他在我旁边坐下，问我在前线做过事没有。我说不巧，我只被派在后方的医院里。
「刚才那人为什么被抓？」

===Original===

I ventured to ask.
“Because he had no papers from the Soviet,” was the reply. “He will be shot immediately.”
“Do you execute everybody who has no papers?” I asked.
“Everybody, without distinction.”
“Even women?” I inquired.
“Yes, even women,” was the reply. “This is a war zone.”
“Holy Mother!” I exclaimed in horror. “How terrible! You really slay them all? Without even a trial?”
“There is little time for trials here. Once fallen here, there is no escape. Our firing squads make an end of all suspected persons on the spot,” he informed me kindly. “Come, would you like to see the execution-grounds? They are quite near here.”
I followed him reluctantly. A few hundred feet away from the station we stopped. I could go no further. The field in front of us was covered with scores of mangled, naked corpses. It made my flesh creep.
“There are about two hundred of them here, mostly officers who had joined or sought to join Kornilov,” he explained.
I could not help shivering. The dreadful scene nearly shattered my nerves and it was all I could do not to collapse.
“Ah, you women, women,” my escort nodded sympathetically. “You are all weak. You don’t know what war is. Still,” he admitted, “there are some who can compare with men. Take Bochkareva, for instance, she would not shudder at sights like this.”
“Who is she, this Bochkareva?” I was curious.
“Haven’t you heard of her?” he asked in surprise. “Why, she was a soldier of the old regime and organized the Women’s Battalion of Death. She is for Kornilov and the bourgeoisie. They gave her an officer’s rank and bought her over to their side, although she is of peasant blood.”
It was all very interesting, this theory of my corruption. I had heard it before, but not stated in such definite terms. At the same time I was haunted by the picture of those mangled bodies, and the thought rankled in my mind of the treacherous Bolsheviks who had opposed capital punishment in the war against Germany but introduced it in the most brutal fashion in the war against their own brothers.
I then told my friend of the trouble in which I found myself, that I was penniless, that I had to get home to Kislovodsk and that I did not know how to get through the front. He explained to me that the so-called front was not a continuous line but a series of posts, maintained on this side by the Bolsheviks and on the opposite side by Kornilov.

===Chinese===

我大着胆子问。
「因为他没有苏维埃的证件。」回答是。「马上就枪毙。」
「没有证件的你们都枪毙？」我问。
「都枪毙，不分彼此。」
「女人也枪毙？」我问。
「对，女人也枪毙。」回答是。「这里是战区。」
「圣母啊！」我惊骇地喊。「多可怕！你们真把他们全杀掉？连个审判都没有？」
「这儿没工夫审判。落在这儿，就别想活。行刑队把一切嫌疑犯就地了结。」他和和气气地告诉我。「来，想看看刑场吗？就在近处。」
我勉强跟着他。离车站几百英尺，我们站住了。我再也走不动。面前那片野地里，横七竖八满是被肢解的赤裸尸体，看得我肉皮发麻。
「这儿约莫两百具，大半是投了科尔尼洛夫、或想去投的军官。」他解释。
我止不住发抖。那可怕的场面险些击碎我的神经，全凭一口气才没有瘫倒。
「唉，你们这些女人啊，」我的向导同情地点头。「都是软骨头，不懂战争是什么。不过，」他也承认，「有几个比得上男人的。比方博奇卡列娃，这种场面她就不带眨眼的。」
「她是谁呀，这个博奇卡列娃？」我装作好奇。
「没听说过她？」他诧异。「嘿，她是旧政权的兵，组织过妇女敢死营。她向着科尔尼洛夫，向着资产阶级。他们给了她军官衔，把她收买了——尽管她身上流的是庄稼人的血。」
这套说我堕落的理论，听得人很有兴味。从前也风闻过，只是没有这样明白的讲法。与此同时，那些碎尸的景象缠着我不去；还有一桩想头在我心里溃烂：虚伪的布尔什维克，对德战争时反对死刑，对自家兄弟的战争里，却用最残暴的手段行起了死刑。
随后我把我自己的难处讲给这位朋友：我身无分文，必须回基斯洛沃茨克的家，却不知怎么穿过前线。他给我讲明：所谓前线并不是一条连续的线，而是一连串哨位——这一边由布尔什维克守，那一边由科尔尼洛夫守。

===Original===

“Sometimes,” he added, “the peasants of the neighbouring villages are allowed by both sides to pass through to Novocherkassk, Kornilov’s headquarters. If you follow that road,” and he pointed to it, “you will come to a village about three miles from here. One of the peasants may be willing to convey you across.”
I thanked him for the valuable information, and we parted friends. The walk to the village was uneventful. On the outskirts of it I saw an old muzhik working outside of his hut. There was a stable and horses attached to it.
“Good day, grandfather!” I greeted the old man.
“Good day, little sister,” he answered.
“Would you drive me to the city?” I asked.
“Great God! How is it possible? The Bolsheviks are fighting in front of the city, and they don’t let anybody pass,” he said.
“But people do go sometimes, don’t they?”
“Yes, sometimes they do.”
“Well, I will give you fifty roubles for driving me to the city,” I offered.
The muzhik scratched his neck, reconsidering the matter.
“But aren’t you a political?” he inquired cautiously.
“No,” I assured him, “I am not.”
He went into the cabin to talk it over with his baba. It was a tempting offer and her consent was apparently quickly obtained, for he soon returned and said:
“All right, we will go. Come into the house. We will have tea and something to eat.”
The invitation was welcome indeed, as I had grown hungry during my long wait at the station and the walk to the village. When we had finished our tea and lunch and the peasant harnessed his horse, I asked for a large apron, which I put on over my clothes. I then asked for the baba’s winter shawl and wrapped it over my head and shoulders, almost completely covering my face, so that I no longer looked like a Sister of Mercy, but one of the local peasant-women.
Praying to God to grant me a safe journey, I seated myself in the cart. The horse started off along the road.
The Bolshevik front was still ahead of me. But I was making progress.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「有时候，」他补了一句，「邻近村里的庄稼人，两边都放行，许他们去诺沃切尔卡斯克——科尔尼洛夫的大本营。你顺着那条路走，」他指给我，「到离这儿三英里左右的村子。兴许有哪个庄稼人肯送你过去。」
我谢过这条金贵的消息，我们好聚好散。去村子的路上无事。村口我看见一个老庄稼汉在自家小屋外干活，旁边带一间马棚，拴着马。
「您好，老爷爷！」我向老人问好。
「您好，小姊妹。」他答。
「能赶车送我进城吗？」我问。
「老天爷！怎么送得成？布尔什维克正在城前打仗，谁也不放。」他说。
「可有人有时候也过去，不是吗？」
「是，有时候有。」
「那这样，送我进城，我给你五十卢布。」我开价。
老汉挠着脖子，重新掂量起来。
「你可别是个政治的？」他小心探问。
「不是，」我向他保证，「不是。」
他进屋去同老婆娘商量。价钱诱人，她显然一口就应了，不多时他出来，说：
「行，去。进屋吧。喝口茶，吃点东西。」
这邀请正中下怀——在车站久等、又走了这一路，我早饿了。茶点用完，庄稼人套马的时候，我讨了一条大围裙，罩在衣裳外面；又讨了老婆娘的冬头巾，裹在头上肩上，几乎盖没了整张脸。这下我看上去不再是仁爱修女，而是本地一个农妇了。
求上帝保佑我一路平安，我坐上马车。马顺着大路走起来。
布尔什维克的防线还在前头。可我在往前赶。…

## VI I Enlist by the Grace of the Tsar / 六 我蒙沙皇恩准从军
===Original===

I spent nearly two months travelling homeward from Yakutsk, by water, rail and foot. The war was everywhere. The barge on the Lena was filled with recruits. In Irkutsk the uniform was much in evidence, and every now and then a regiment of soldiers would march through the streets on the way to the station, arousing one’s martial spirit. My convoy left me upon my arrival there, and I had to appeal to the authorities for funds to continue my journey.
My heart was beating furiously when I reached Tomsk, after an absence of about six years. Tears dimmed my eyes as I walked the familiar streets. Here, in this two-storied house, I had first learned the fickleness of man’s love. That was ten years ago, during the Russo-Japanese War, when I was only fifteen years old. There, in that dilapidated little shop, where I can see the figure of Nastasia Leontievna bent over the counter, I spent five years of my early youth, waiting on customers, scrubbing floors, cooking, washing and sewing. That long apprenticeship, under the stern eyes of Nastasia Leontievna, served me in good stead in later years, I must admit. The smoking chimney yonder belongs to the house in which I was married, some eight years ago, only to gain experience at first hand of man’s brutality. And here, in this basement, my father and mother have been dwelling for seventeen years.
I swung open the door. My mother was baking bread and did not turn immediately. How old she had grown! How bent her shoulders, how white her hair! She turned her head and stared at me for a second. A lump rose in my throat, rendering me speechless.
“Mania!” she exclaimed, rushing toward me and locking me in her arms.
We wept, kissed each other, and wept again. My mother offered prayers to the Holy Mother and swore that she would never let me leave her side again. The bread was almost burned to charcoal, having been forgotten in the oven in the excitement of my return. My father came in, and he also was greatly aged. He greeted me tenderly, the years having softened the harshness of his nature.
I paid some visits to old friends. Nastasia Leontievna was overjoyed to see me. The sister of Afanasy Bochkarev, my first husband, also welcomed me cordially, in spite of the fact that I had escaped from her brother. She realized well enough how brutal and rough he was.

===Chinese===

从雅库茨克回家，水路、火车加步行，我走了将近两个月。战争无处不在。勒拿河上的驳船满载新兵。伊尔库茨克满眼军装，时不时一个团的士兵穿过街道开往车站，撩人胸中的杀气。护兵到那里就离开了我，我只得向当局讨盘缠继续赶路。

约六年不见，重到托木斯克时，我的心狂跳。走在熟悉的街上，泪水模糊了我的眼。这边，这座两层的房子里，我头一回领教了男人的爱多么靠不住。那是十年前，日俄战争的时候，我才十五岁。那边，那间破旧的小铺，我望得见娜斯塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜（Nastasia Leontievna）俯在柜台上的身影；我在那里度过早年五年的光阴，招待顾客，擦地板，做饭，洗衣，缝纫。必须承认，娜斯塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜严厉眼皮底下的那段学徒生涯，后来多年于我大有益处。远处那根冒烟的烟囱，是我嫁人的那座房子——约八年前——为的是亲身体验一回男人的粗暴。而这里，这间地下室，我的父亲和母亲住了十七年。

我推开门。母亲正在烤面包，没有立刻回头。她老成什么样了！背驼得多厉害，头发白得多厉害！她回头，怔怔看了我一秒。一团东西涌上我的喉咙，叫我开不了口。

「玛尼娅！」她喊起来，扑向我，把我搂进怀里。

我们哭，亲吻，又哭。母亲向圣母祷告，起誓再也不放我离开她身边。面包忘在炉里，几乎烧成了炭。父亲进来，他也老多了；他温存地迎接我，岁月磨软了他天性里的粗暴。

我拜访了几家旧友。娜斯塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜见了我欢喜极了。我的第一任丈夫阿法纳西·博奇卡列夫的妹妹也热络地迎我，尽管我是从她哥哥手里逃出来的——他多粗多暴，她心里有数。

===Original===

She told me that Afanasy had been called in the first draft, and that it was reported that he was among the first prisoners taken by the Germans. I have never heard of him again.
I rested for about three days. The news from the front was exciting. Great battles were raging. Our soldiers were retreating in some places and advancing in others. I longed for wings to fly to their help. My heart yearned and ached.
“Do you know what war is?” I asked myself. “It is no work for a woman. You must make sure before starting out, Marusia, that you won’t disgrace yourself. Are you strong enough in spirit to face all the trials and dangers of this colossal war? Are you strong enough in body to shed blood and endure the privations of war? Are you firm enough at heart to withstand the temptations that will come to you, living among men? Search your soul for a brave and truthful answer.”
And I found strength enough in me to answer “yes” to all these questions. I suppressed the hidden longing for Yasha in the depths of my being, and made the fateful decision. I would go to war and fight till death, or, if God preserved me, till the coming of peace. I would defend my country and help those unfortunate ones on the field of slaughter who had already made their sacrifices for their country.
It was November, 1914. With my heart steeled in the decision I had made, I resolutely approached the headquarters of the Twenty-fifth Reserve Battalion stationed in Tomsk. Upon entering a clerk asked me what I wanted.
“To see the Commander,” I replied.
“What for?” he inquired.
“I want to enlist,” I said.
The man looked at me for a moment and burst out laughing. He called to the other clerks. “Here is a baba who wants to enlist!” he announced jokingly, pointing at me. There followed a general uproar. “Ha! ha! ha!” they chorused, forgetting their work for the moment. When the merriment subsided a little I repeated my request to see the Commander, and his adjutant came out. He must have been told that a woman had come to enlist, for he addressed me gaily:
“What is your wish?”
“I want to enlist in the army, your Excellency,” I answered.
“To enlist, eh? But you are a baba,” he laughed. “The regulations do not permit us to enlist women. It is against the law.”
I insisted that I wanted to fight, and begged to see the Commander.

===Chinese===

她告诉我，阿法纳西头一批应征，据说在德国人最早捉去的俘虏之列。此后我再没有听到他的音讯。

我歇了约三天。前线的消息激动人心，大会战正酣：我们的士兵这里退、那里进。我巴不得生出翅膀，飞去帮他们。我的心又疼又渴。

「你知道什么是打仗么？」我问自己。「这不是女人干的活。上路之前你得想扎实，玛露霞：别丢自己的脸。你灵魂上担得起这场滔天大战的一切试炼和凶险么？你身体上担得起流血、担得起战争的种种匮乏么？你心性上把得稳么——同男人们一起过活，诱惑会找上门来？往你灵魂深处找，找一个勇敢而老实的回答。」

而我在我里面找到了力，对这些问题统统答得出「是」。我按下心底对雅沙那点藏着的思念，下了这步决定命运的决定。我要去打仗，打到死；倘若上帝留我命，打到和平来。我要保卫我的国家，帮一帮屠场上那些已经为祖国舍过身的可怜人。

那是1914年11月。主意定了，心也硬了，我毅然走进驻托木斯克的第二十五预备营的营部。一进门，一个文书问我来意。

「见营长，」我答。

「什么事？」他问。

「我要从军，」我说。

那人打量了我一眼，放声大笑，叫起别的文书：「来了个要当兵的娘们！」他指着我，开玩笑地宣布。于是一阵哄堂大笑。「哈！哈！哈！」他们齐声笑，手头的活都撂了。笑劲略退，我又重申求见营长；他的副官出来了。想必有人告诉了他来了个要从军的女人，他快快活活地迎我：

「你有什么事？」

「我要从军，大人，」我答。

「从军，嗯？可你是个娘们，」他笑。「条令不许我们收女人。那是犯法的。」

我坚持要打仗，求见营长。

===Original===

The adjutant reported me to the Commander, who ordered that I should be shown in.
With the adjutant laughing behind me, I blushed and became confused when brought before the Commander. He rebuked the adjutant and inquired what he could do for me. I repeated that I wanted to enlist and fight for the country.
“It is very noble of you to have such a desire. But women are not allowed in the army,” he said. “They are too weak. What could you, for instance, do in the front line? Women are not made for war.”
“Your Excellency,” I insisted, “God has given me strength, and I can defend my country as well as a man. I have asked myself before coming here whether I could endure the life of a soldier, and found that I could. Cannot you place me in your regiment?”
“My dear,” the Commander declared gently, “how can I help you? It is against the law. I have no authority to enlist a woman even if I wanted to. You can go to the rear, enlist as a Red Cross nurse or in some other auxiliary service.”
I rejected his proposal. I had heard so many rumours about the women in the rear that I had come to despise them. I therefore insisted on my determination to go to the front as a regular soldier. The Commander was deeply impressed by my obstinacy, and wanted to help me. He suggested that I should send a telegram to the Tsar, telling him of my desire to defend the country, of my moral purpose, and beg him to grant me special permission to enlist.
The Commander promised to draw up the telegram himself, with a recommendation of his own, and to have it sent from his office. He warned me, however, to consider the matter again, to think of the hardships I should have to bear, of the soldiers’ attitude toward me, and the universal ridicule that I should provoke. But I did not change my mind. The telegram was sent at my expense, costing eight roubles, which I obtained from my mother.
When I disclosed to my family the nature of my visit to the Commander of the Twenty-fifth Battalion they burst into tears. My poor mother cried that her Mania must have gone out of her senses, that it was an unheard-of, impossible thing. Who ever heard of a baba going to war? She would allow herself to be buried alive before letting me enlist. My father supported her. I was their only hope now, they said. They would be forced to starve and go begging, without my help.

===Chinese===

副官把我报了上去，营长吩咐放我进去。

副官在我背后笑着；被带到营长面前时，我红了脸，慌了神。营长申斥了副官，问有什么可以为我办的。我重说了一遍：我要从军，为国而战。

「你有这份心愿，很高尚，」他说。「可军队不许女人进来。她们太弱。譬如你，到了前线上能做什么？女人不是生来打仗的。」

「大人，」我坚持，「上帝给了我力气，我保国保得不比男人差。来之前我问过自己受不受得了当兵的日子，我受得了。不能把我编进您的团么？」

「亲爱的，」营长温和地说，「我怎么帮得了你？那是犯法的。就算我想收，也无权收一个女人。你可以到后方去，当红十字护士，或者进什么辅助队。」

我谢绝了。后方的那些女人，风声我听得太多，心里早已看轻。于是我咬定要以正规士兵的身份上前线。我的固执打动了营长，他想帮我。他提议：给沙皇打一封电报，陈明我保国的志愿、我的道义用心，求他特准我从军。

营长答应亲自草那封电报，附上他自己的保荐，从营部发出。只是他叮嘱我再想一想：想想要吃的苦，想想士兵们会怎样待我，想想我将招来的四下嘲笑。可我不改主意。电报发了，钱是我出的——八个卢布，问母亲要的。

我把去第二十五营营部的缘由向家里一说，全家放声大哭。可怜的母亲哭喊着：她的玛尼娅准是疯了，这是没听说过的事、不可能的事。谁听说过娘们去打仗？把她活埋了她也不能依。父亲帮腔。他们说，如今我是他们唯一的指望；没有我，他们只有挨饿讨饭。

===Original===

And the house was filled with sobs and lamentation, the two younger sisters and some neighbours joining in.
My heart was rent in twain. It was a cruel, painful choice that I was called upon to make, a choice between my mother and my country. It had cost me so much to steel myself to that new life, and now, when I was seemingly near the goal, my long-suffering mother called upon me to give up this ideal that possessed me, for her sake. I was tormented and agonized by doubt. I realized that I must make a decision quickly, and, with a supreme effort and the help of God, I resolved that the call of my country came before the call of my mother.
After some time had passed a soldier came to the house.
“Is Maria Bochkareva here?” he questioned.
He came from headquarters with the news that a telegram had arrived from the Tsar, authorizing the Commander to enlist me as a soldier, and that the Commander wanted to see me.
My mother did not expect such an answer. She grew frantic. She cursed the Tsar with all her might, although she had always revered him as the Little Father. “What kind of a Tsar is he?” she cried, “if he takes women to war? He must have lost his senses. Who ever heard of a Tsar calling women to arms? Hasn’t he enough men? Goodness knows, there are myriads of them in Mother Russia.”
She seized the Tsar’s portrait on the wall, before which she had crossed herself every morning, and tore it to bits, stamping them on the floor, with imprecations and anathema on her lips. Never again would she pray for him, she declared. “No, never!”
The soldier’s message had an opposite effect on me; and I was in high spirits. Dressing in my best clothes, I went to see the Commander. Everybody at headquarters seemed to know of the Tsar’s telegram, smiles greeting me everywhere. The Commander congratulated me and read its text in a solemn voice, explaining that it was an extraordinary honour which the august Emperor had conferred on me, and that I must make myself worthy of it. I was so happy, so joyous, so excited. It was the most blissful moment of my life.
The Commander called in his orderly and instructed him to obtain a full soldier’s outfit for me.

===Chinese===

于是满屋是抽泣和哀号，两个小妹妹和几个邻居也加入进来。

我的心撕成了两半。这是一副残忍、剜心的选择：一头是母亲，一头是祖国。我费了多大的劲才把自己锻进那种新生活；如今眼看快到目的了，受尽苦的母亲却求我看在她的份上，撂下这个攥住我的理想。我被疑虑折磨着、熬煎着。我知道必须快决断；凭着拼死的一把劲、凭着上帝帮扶，我定下：祖国的召唤，先于母亲的召唤。

过了一阵，一个士兵来到家里。

「玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃住在这儿么？」他问。

他从营部带来消息：沙皇回电到了，准营长收我当兵；营长要见我。

母亲没有料到这样的回音。她疯了似的，用尽全身的力气咒沙皇——尽管她一向敬他如「小父亲」。「这算个什么沙皇？」她喊，「倒叫娘们去打仗？他准是疯了。谁听说过沙皇征娘们当兵？他还嫌男人不够？老天爷知道，俄罗斯母亲那儿男人多得数不清。」

她一把摘下墙上沙皇的像——每天早上她都在像前画十字的那张——撕成碎片，踩在地板上，嘴里咒着、骂着。她宣称再不为他祷告。「不，永不！」

士兵带来的消息在我身上是另一个效果：我兴致飞扬。穿上最好的衣裳，我去见营长。营部里人人似乎都知道了沙皇的电报，一路都是笑脸迎我。营长道了贺，用庄重的声调念了电文，说这是至尊的皇帝赐给我的殊荣，我须得配得上它。我那样快活、那样欢喜、那样兴奋。那是我一生最至福的时刻。

营长唤来勤务兵，吩咐给我置办一身齐全的士兵行头。

===Original===

I received two complete undergarments made of coarse linen, two pairs of foot-rags, a laundry-bag, a pair of boots, one pair of trousers, a belt, a regulation blouse, a pair of epaulets, a cap with the insignia on it, two cartridge pockets and a rifle. My hair was clipped short.
There was an outburst of laughter when I appeared in full military attire, as a regular soldier of the Fourth Company, Fifth Regiment. I was confused and somewhat bewildered, being hardly able to recognize myself. The news of a woman recruit had preceded me at the barracks, and my arrival there was the signal for riotous mirth. I was surrounded on all sides by raw recruits who stared at me incredulously, but some were not satisfied with mere staring, so rare a novelty was I to them. They wanted to make sure that their eyes were not deceived, so they proceeded to pinch me, jostle me and brush against me.
“Nonsense, she isn’t a baba,” remarked one of them.
“Indeed, she is,” said another, pinching me.
“She’ll run like the devil at the first German shot,” joked a third, provoking roars of laughter.
“We’ll make it so hot for her that she’ll run before even getting to the front,” threatened a fourth.
Here the Commander of my company interfered, and the men dispersed. I was granted permission to take my things home before settling permanently at the barracks. I asked to be shown how to salute. On the way home I saluted every uniform in the same manner. Opening the door of the house, I halted on the threshold. My mother did not recognize me.
“Maria Leontievna Bochkareva here?” I asked sharply, in military fashion. Mother took me for some messenger from headquarters, and answered, “No.”
I threw myself on her neck. “Holy Mother, save me!” she exclaimed. There were cries and tears which brought my father and little sister on the scene. My mother became hysterical. For the first time I saw my father weep, and again I was urged to come back to my senses and give up this crazy notion of serving in the army. The landlady and old Nastasia Leontievna were called to help dissuade me from my purpose.
“Think what the men will do to a solitary woman in their midst,” they argued. “Why, they’ll make a prostitute of you. They will kill you secretly, and nobody will ever find a trace of you. Only the other day they found the body of a woman along the railroad track, thrown out of a troop-train.

===Chinese===

我领到两套粗麻布的贴身衣、两副裹脚布、一只洗衣袋、一双靴子、一条裤子、一条腰带、一件制式军上衣、一副肩章、一顶缀着帽徽的军帽、两个子弹袋和一支步枪。头发铰短了。

我一副全副武装露面——第五团第四连的一名正牌士兵——营里炸开一片大笑。我又窘又懵，几乎认不出自己。女人当兵的新闻先我一步进了营房，我一到，便是狂欢的信号。新兵们四面围住我，不信似的盯着我看；有的光看还不满足——我于他们是太稀罕了。他们要验一验眼睛没骗他们，便上来拧我、挤我、蹭我。

「瞎说，她不是娘们，」一个说。

「就是，」另一个拧了我一把说。

「德国人第一炮响，她跑得比鬼还快，」第三个打趣，惹来一片大笑。

「我们先把她烤得受不了，不等上前线她就得跑，」第四个撂下狠话。

这时我们连长出来干涉，人群散了。批准我把东西送回家，再长住营房。我请人教我怎样敬礼；回家路上，我见了军装就照样敬一个。推开家门，我在门槛上立定。母亲没认出我。

「玛丽亚·列昂季耶夫娜·博奇卡列娃住这儿么？」我用军人的腔调厉声问。母亲当我是个营部来送信的，答：「不住。」

我扑上去搂她的脖子。「圣母啊，救救我！」她喊起来。哭喊和眼泪把父亲和小妹妹引了来。母亲歇斯底里了。我头一回见父亲落泪；又一次，他们求我醒醒，撂下当兵这个疯念头。房东太太和老板塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜都被请来劝我回心。

「想想一群男人中间孤零零一个女人会落什么下场，」她们劝。「哼，他们要把你作践成娼妇。他们会偷偷弄死你，尸首都没处找。前几天铁路边才发现一具女尸，是从运兵车上扔下来的。

===Original===

You have always been such a sensible girl. What has come over you? And what will become of your parents? They are old and weak, and you are their only hope. They always said that when Marusia came back they would end their lives in peace. Now you are shortening their days, driving them to their graves in sorrow.”
For a little while I hesitated again. The fierce struggle in my bosom between the two conflicting calls was renewed. But I held by my decision, remaining deaf to all entreaty. Then my mother grew angry and, crying out at the top of her voice, she shouted:
“You are no longer my daughter! You have forfeited your mother’s love.”
With a heavy heart I left the house for the barracks. The Commander of the Company did not expect me, and I had to explain to him why I could not pass that night at home. He assigned to me a place in the general sleeping-room ordering the men not to molest me. On my right and on my left were soldiers, and that first night in the company of men will ever stand out in my memory. I did not close my eyes once during the night.
The men were, naturally, unaccustomed to such a strange creature as myself and took me for a woman of loose morals who had made her way into the ranks for the sake of carrying on her illicit trade. I was, therefore, compelled constantly to fight off intrusions from all sides. As soon as I made an effort to shut my eyes I would discover the arm of my left-hand-neighbour round my neck, and would restore it to its owner with a push. While keeping an eye on his movements, however, I offered an opportunity for my neighbour on the right to get too near to me, and I would savagely kick him in the side. All night long my nerves were taut and my fists busy. Toward dawn I was so exhausted that I nearly fell asleep, when I discovered a hand on my chest, and before the man realized my intention, I struck him in the face. I continued to rain blows till the bell rang at five o’clock, the hour for rising.
Ten minutes were given us to dress and wash, tardiness being punished by a rebuke. At the end of ten minutes the ranks formed and every soldier’s hands, ears and foot-rags were inspected. I was in such haste to be in time that I put my trousers on inside out, provoking roars of laughter.

===Chinese===

你一向是个明白孩子。你怎么了？你爹娘往后可怎么办？他们老了、弱了，你是他们唯一的指望。他们总说，等玛露霞回来，他们就太太平平过完这辈子。如今你在折他们的寿，把他们往坟里赶。」

我又犹豫了一小会儿。胸中那两股相争的呼唤重新开战。可我守住决定，对一切哀求充耳不闻。母亲于是恼了，扯开嗓子喊：

「你不再是我的女儿！你把娘的爱丢了。」

我揣着一颗沉重的心离家去了营房。连长没料到我来，我只得对他说明为什么那晚不能宿在家里。他派我在大通铺里一个铺位，命令士兵们不许搅扰我。左右都是士兵；在男人中间度过的头一夜，我一辈子忘不了。那一夜我没有合一回眼。

男人们自然没见过我这样古怪的造物，把我当成了混进队伍做皮肉买卖的浪荡女人。于是我不得不时刻招架四面八方的侵犯。刚要合眼，就发觉左邻的胳膊搭上了我的脖子，我把它一推，还给原主；可盯他的时候，又给了右边的邻铺可乘之机，凑得近了，我便狠狠踹他肋上一脚。一整夜我的神经绷着，拳头忙着。近天亮我乏得几乎睡着，又发觉一只手按在我胸口；趁那人没明白我的意思，我一拳打在他脸上。我一顿乱拳打下去，直打到五点钟起床的钟响。

给我们十分钟穿衣洗漱，迟了要挨申斥。十分钟一到就列队，逐个检查士兵的手、耳朵和裹脚布。我赶得急，把裤子穿反了，惹来哄堂大笑。

===Original===

The day began with a prayer for the Tsar and country, following which every one of us received the daily allowance of two-and-a-half pounds of bread and a few cubes of sugar from our respective squad commanders. There were four squads to a company. Our breakfast consisted of bread and tea and lasted half an hour.
At the mess I had an opportunity to get acquainted with some of the more sympathetic soldiers. There were ten volunteers in my company, and they were all students. After eating, there was roll-call. When the officer reached my name he read: “Bochkareva,” to which I answered, “Aye.” We were then taken out for instruction, since the entire regiment had been formed only three days before. The first rule that the training officer tried to impress upon us was to pay attention, and to watch his movements and actions. Not all the recruits could do it easily. I prayed to God to enlighten me in the study of a soldier’s duties.
It was slow work to establish proper relations with the men. The first few days I was such a nuisance to the Company Commander that he wished me to ask for dismissal. He hinted as much on a couple of occasions, but I continued to mind my own business and never reported the annoyances I endured from the men. Gradually I won their respect and confidence. The small group of volunteers always defended me. As the Russian soldiers call each other by nicknames, one of the first questions put to me by my friends was what I would like to be called.
“Call me Yashka,” I said, and that name stuck to me ever after, saving my life on more than one occasion. There is so much in a name, and “Yashka” was the sort of name that appealed to the soldiers and always worked in my favour. In time it became the nickname of the regiment, but not before I had been tested by many additional trials and found to be a comrade, and not merely a woman, by the men.
I was an apt student and learned almost to anticipate the orders of the instructor. When the day’s duties were completed and the soldiers gathered into groups to while away an hour or two in games or story-telling, I was always asked to take part. I came to like the soldiers, who were good-natured fellows, and to enjoy their sports. The group which Yashka joined would usually prove the most popular in the barracks, and it was sufficient to secure my cooperation in some scheme to make it a success.

===Chinese===

一天从为沙皇和祖国的祷告开始；随后每人从各自的班长手里领一份每天二磅半的面包和几块方糖。一个连四个班。我们的早饭是面包加茶，吃半个钟头。

吃饭时我有机会结识了几个比较投缘的士兵。我们连有十名志愿兵，全是学生。饭后点名。军官念到我的名字，念的是「博奇卡列娃」，我应一声「到」。随后带出去操练——整个团三天前才编成。教官要我们先记牢的头一条规矩，就是用心，看他的动作和行事。不是每个新兵都容易做到。我求上帝开我的窍，学好当兵的本分。

同男兵们把关系摆顺，是桩慢工。头几天我给连长添的那份麻烦，叫他想撺掇我请退。他旁敲侧击过两回；我只管做我的事，男兵们给我的气受，从不去报告。渐渐地我赢得了他们的敬重和信任。那一小群志愿兵总是护着我。俄国士兵彼此都叫外号，朋友们问我的头一桩事，就是要我叫什么好。

「叫我雅什卡，」我说。这名字从此钉在我身上，不止一回收过我的命。名字里的讲究多着呢，「雅什卡」正是士兵们喜欢的那种名字，回回替我讨好。后来它成了全团的外号——不过那是在我又经了许多考验、男兵们认了我这个同志、而不只是一个女人之后。

我学得快，几乎能抢在教官口令前头。一天的功课完了，士兵们聚成一堆堆，下棋讲故事消磨一两个钟头，回回都拉我入伙。我渐渐喜欢这些好性子的汉子，喜欢上他们的玩闹。雅什卡加入哪一堆，哪一堆在营房里准最热闹；什么事只要拉上我搭伙，就准办得成。

===Original===

There was little time for relaxation, however, as we went through an intensive training course of only three months before we were sent to the front. Once a week, every Sunday, I would leave the barracks and spend the day at home, my mother having reconciled herself to my enlistment. On holidays I would be visited by friends or relatives. On one such occasion my sister and her husband called. I had been detailed for guard duty in the barracks that day. While on such duty a soldier is forbidden to sit down or to engage in conversation. I was entertaining my visitors when the Company Commander passed.
“Do you know the rules, Bochkareva?” he asked.
“Yes, your Excellency,” I answered.
“What are they?”
“A soldier on guard duty is not allowed to sit down or engage in conversation,” I replied. He ordered me to stand for two hours at attention at the completion of my guard duty, which took twenty-four hours. Standing at attention, in full military equipment, for two hours is a severe task, as one has to remain absolutely motionless under the eyes of a guard, and yet it was a common punishment.
During my training I was punished in this manner three times. The second time it was really not my fault. One night I recognized my squad commander in a soldier who annoyed me, and I dealt him as hard a blow as I would have given to any other man. In the morning he placed me at attention for two hours, claiming that he had accidentally brushed against me.
At first there was some difficulty in arranging for my bathing. The bathhouse was used by the men, and so I was allowed one day to visit a public bathhouse. I thought it a good opportunity for some fun. I came into the women’s room, fully dressed, and there was a tremendous uproar as soon as I appeared. I was taken for a man. However, the fun did not last long. In an instant I was attacked from all sides and only narrowly escaped serious injury by crying out that I was a woman.
In the last month of our training we engaged in almost continuous rifle practice. I applied myself zealously to acquiring skill in handling a rifle and won an honourable mention for good marksmanship. This considerably enhanced my standing with the soldiers and strengthened our feeling of comradeship.
Early in 1915 our regiment received orders to prepare to proceed to the front. We received a week’s leave.

===Chinese===

不过闲暇很少：我们受的是三个月的强化训练，期满便开赴前线。每星期日，我出营回家过一天——母亲对我的从军已经认了命。逢节日，亲友来看我。有一回，妹妹同妹夫来了。那天我正好被派在营房站岗。站岗时士兵不得坐，不得交谈。我正陪着客人，连长走了过。

「条令你知道吗，博奇卡列娃？」他问。

「知道，大人，」我答。

「是什么？」

「站岗的士兵不得坐下，不得交谈，」我答。他命令我在二十四小时的岗满之后，再罚立正两小时。全副武装立正两小时是桩苦差，因为得在一旁看守的眼皮底下纹丝不动；可这是寻常的处罚。

受训期间我这样受罚三回。第二回实在不怪我。一天夜里，我认出了搅扰我的人是我的班长，便照着对任何男人一样，狠狠给了他一拳。第二天早上他罚我立正两小时，说自己只是无意蹭了我一下。

起初，洗澡的事不好安排。澡堂是男兵用的，于是准我一天去公共澡堂。我想这倒是个取乐的机会，便一身行头走进女部；我一露面便炸了窝，被当成了男人。不过乐子没过多久：一眨眼我四面受攻，高喊我是女人才勉强躲过一顿好打。

训练的最后一个月，我们几乎不停地练射击。我发狠练枪，得了个射术优良的表扬。这大大抬高了我任士兵中间的身价，也更紧了我们同志的情分。

1915年初，我们团接到命令：准备开赴前线。给了我们一个星期的假。

===Original===

The soldiers passed these last days in drink and revelry and gay parties. One evening a group of boys invited me to go along with them to a house of ill repute.
“Be a soldier, Yashka,” they urged me laughingly, scarcely expecting me to accept their invitation.
A thought flashed through my mind.
“I will go with them, and learn the soldier’s life, so that I may understand his soul better.” And I expressed my willingness to go. Perhaps curiosity had something to do with my decision. It was greeted with an explosion of mirth. Noisily we marched through the streets, singing and laughing, until we came to our destination.
My knees began to tremble as the party was about to enter the house. I wanted to turn back and flee. But the soldiers would not let me. The idea of Yashka going with them to such a place took a strong hold on their imagination.
