「我去见彼得格勒的政府首脑，人家不见我。我差点被打死，扔出楼外。为什么？
「自由！
「德国人见什么拿什么，同时赤卫队却在扩充，好去打谁——打德国人吗？不，打所谓资产阶级！可他们不也是我们的骨肉兄弟？一边叫我们自相残杀，一边任德国人蹂躏我们的国土，凭谁的名义？
「自由的名义！
「国家受了辱、遭了殃，还叫我们去消灭自己的知识分子。
「这叫自由吗？
「我听说在莫斯科，一千个残废人被撵到了街上。

===Original===

These invalids are soldiers like yourselves and myself, only maimed and crippled for life. Why were they thrown out?
“For the sake of liberty!”
We were all deeply impressed by this speech. Not a single voice was raised in protest. Every heart felt that the liberty we had received was not the kind of liberty we had dreamed of. We wanted peace, happiness, brotherhood, not civil war, foreign invasions, strife, starvation and disease.
Another speaker arose and said:
“He is right. We have been deceived and disgraced. We go hungry and no one cares. But how can we get out of this shameful situation? We should have to overthrow the present leaders, and reestablish the front. The Japanese are already moving into Siberia, and the Germans are occupying Russia, all because we are divided. We shall be under some foreign yoke if we don’t join our forces. We quarrelled with our officers, but how can we ever hope to do anything without officers? We might make peace with them, but where can we get arms to overthrow our present leaders, who have surrounded themselves with bands of Red Guards?”
For a moment the vast gathering remained silent. It was a pathetic calm. There was a painful sense that our much-cherished freedom had turned into an oppressive bondage.
Suddenly a couple of men raised their voices, shouting protests, denouncing the speaker, even threatening him. They were promptly seized and placed under arrest, and quiet was restored.
“Allow me to answer the question!” I shouted to the chairman from the distant place I occupied.
“Bochkareva! It’s Bochkareva!” a number of voices passed the word to the platform, and immediately I was lifted up and carried on to the platform.
“It’s a pleasure to speak to you now,” I began, “only a few weeks ago you would have torn me to pieces.”
“Yes, it’s true! We killed many!” several men interrupted. “But we were told that the officers wanted to enslave us, that’s why we killed them. We now see that our real enemies are not the officers, but the Germans.”
“Before I answer the question put by the previous speaker, let me ask you what your attitude is toward the Allies?” I said.
“America, England, and France we trust. They are our friends. They are free countries. But we distrust Japan. Japan wants Siberia,” came in reply from many directions.
Here a soldier interrupted and asked permission to ask a question. It was granted.

===Chinese===

这些残废人是同你我一样的士兵，只是终身残了缺了。他们为什么被撵出来？
「为了自由！」
这篇演说深深打动了我们所有人，没有一声抗议。每一颗心都觉得：我们到手的这个自由，不是我们梦里的那种自由。我们要的是和平、幸福、兄弟情义，不是内战、外侵、争斗、饥饿和瘟病。
另一个演说者站起来说：
「他说的对。我们受了骗、受了辱。我们挨饿，没有一个人管。可怎么才能脱出这个可耻的境地？就得推翻如今的当家人，重建前线。日本人已经进了西伯利亚，德国人占着俄国，全因为我们四分五裂。再不合力，我们就得套上外国的轭。我们同军官闹翻了，可没有军官能成什么事？同他们讲和倒可以，可哪里去弄枪，来打倒如今这些拿赤卫队围护着自己的当家人？」
大片人群静了片刻。那是一种凄凉的平静。人人痛感我们珍爱的自由已变成一副压人的枷。
忽然有几个人高声反对，痛斥演说者，甚至威胁他。他们当场被抓住押走，秩序恢复了。
「这个问题让我来答！」我从远处朝主席喊。
「博奇卡列娃！是博奇卡列娃！」许多声音把话传到台上。我立刻被举起来，抬上了讲台。
「今天同你们讲话是件快事，」我开讲，「几个星期前，你们还会把我撕了。」
「是，是真的！我们杀了许多！」几个汉子插话。「可那时说军官要奴役我们，我们才杀的。如今我们看清，真正的敌人不是军官，是德国人。」
「我先问一句：你们对协约国是什么看法，再答前一位的问题。」我说。
「美国、英国、法国，我们信。他们是朋友，是自由的国家。日本我们不信任，日本想要西伯利亚。」四下里这样回答。
这时一个士兵插进来，请求提问。得到了准许。

===Original===

“I can’t understand why our Allies do not defend us,” he said. “Not even one of them has come to our help at a time when Germany is devouring our land. The Allied envoys are running away from Russia, and those that remain do not listen to the voice of the masses, but to the representatives of Lenin and Trotsky. At Moscow I saw an official of the Soviet escort an Englishman to a train. I was hungry. There were hundreds of soldiers like me at the station. Our hearts were aching. We wanted to give him a message, but he did not even turn to us. Instead, he warmly shook hands with the Soviet official.”
“What if we should appeal to the Allies, to America, England, and France, to furnish us with bread, arms and money for the reconstruction of the front?” I resumed.
“How can we trust them?” I was interrupted again. “They will come here and work in league with Lenin and his band of bloodsuckers.”
“Why not join forces and elect a Constituent Assembly, and let your own leaders cooperate with the Allies?” I suggested.
“But whom could we choose?”
“That we would decide later. There are plenty of good men still left in Russia,” I answered. “But what if I, for instance, should want to do something, would you trust me?”
“Yes, yes! We know you! You are of the people!” hundreds of voices cried out.
“Well, let me tell you then, I am going to America and England. If I should succeed and come back with an Allied force, would you come to aid me in saving Russia?”
“Yes, we will! Yes, yes!” the crowd roared.
With this the meeting ended. The train was now about to start and we hurried towards it, singing on the way. I felt happy and hopeful. Several thousand soldiers were not to be disregarded. They were almost unanimous in their new view of the country’s condition. Taken in combination with what I had observed in Moscow and on the way to Petrograd, this meeting further reinforced my hopes for Russia’s salvation. It was obviously a phenomenon of widespread occurrence, this awakening of the soldiers.
My mother had received Petrukhin’s letter, and for six weeks had mourned me as dead. She was overwhelmed with joy upon my return, but became a little uneasy as she perceived a long line of girls, many of them almost barefoot, file after me into the little hut.

===Chinese===

「我不明白，协约国为什么不救我们，」他说。「德国正在吞我们的地，他们连一个来帮忙的都没有。协约国的使节正从俄国逃跑；留下的那些人不听民众的声音，只听列宁和托洛茨基的代表。在莫斯科，我亲眼看见一个苏维埃的官员送一个英国人上火车。我饿着。站上像我一样的士兵几百个，心都在痛。我们想给他带句话，他连头也不回，却同那个苏维埃官员热烈握手。」
「如果我们向协约国——美、英、法——求援，请他们给面包、枪械和钱来重建前线，怎么样？」我接着说。
「凭什么信他们？」又有人打断。「他们会来，同列宁和他那伙吸血鬼勾结在一起。」
「何不合力选出一个立宪会议，让你们自己的领袖同协约国合作？」我提议。
「可选谁呢？」
「那个以后再定。俄国的好人还多得很。」我答。「可比方说，我想出来做点事，你们信得过我吗？」
「信，信！我们认得你！你是老百姓的人！」几百个声音喊。
「那好，我告诉你们：我要去美国、去英国。若我成了，带着协约国的军队回来，你们肯来助我救俄国吗？」
「肯！肯！」人群吼道。
集会就这样散了。火车将开，我们赶回车去，一路唱着歌。我心里欢喜，充满指望。几千名士兵的声音不容小看。他们对国事的新看法几乎完全一致。同我在莫斯科和去彼得格勒一路的所见合起来看，这场集会更添了我对俄国得救的希望。士兵的觉醒，显然已是遍地皆是的现象。
母亲收到了彼得鲁欣的信，为我守了六个星期的丧。见我回来，她喜出望外；可一见一长列姑娘跟着我鱼贯走进小屋——许多几乎光着脚——她心里又发起虚来。

===Original===

She took me aside and asked what it meant, confiding to me that she had only fifty roubles left of the money I had brought home on my previous visit. I begged her to be patient and assured her that I would arrange matters promptly. I immediately went to the owner of the hut and several others of the leading peasants of the community, got them together, explained to them the situation, informed them that I had only one thousand roubles to spend toward the support of the girls, and asked if they would undertake to feed and house them on credit till my return from America.
“I swear that I will pay every kopeck due to you. I will get enough money to pay not only the debts, but to ensure for them sustenance and shelter to the end of their lives. Now I want you to keep a record of all your expenses. Will you trust me?”
“Yes,” replied the peasants. “We know that you have done a great deal for Russia, and we have confidence in you.”
This was the arrangement under which the thirty invalids of my Battalion of Death were left by me in the village of Tutalsk in March, 1918. The thousand roubles I gave to my mother with instructions to buy shoes for the most destitute of the girls. Of the five hundred roubles given to me by the Consul, I gave three hundred to my mother. I decided to take my youngest sister, Nadia, with me to America. Accompanied to the station by my parents, the thirty girls, and half the community, I started eastward, for Irkutsk and Vladivostok, dressed once more as a woman.
At the station in Irkutsk I noticed a young girl, with two tiny children in her arms. Somehow her face looked familiar to me, but I could not recall who she was. She was evidently in trouble, poor and ragged. For a while she stared at me. Then she ran up and cried out breathlessly:
“Mania!”
She was the younger daughter of the woman Kitova, whose husband had killed the dog-catcher and who had accompanied him into exile, at the same time that I had gone into exile with Yasha. Then she was only a little girl, not more than eleven or twelve years old. Now she was the mother of two children.
For three days, she told me, her mother and herself had been living on the floor of the station. They had only seventy kopecks left in their possession. With this money the mother had gone to the town to find a lodging!

===Chinese===

她把我拉到一边问是怎么回事，又吐露：我上次带回家的钱，只剩五十卢布了。我求她耐心，保证即刻把事情安顿好。我马上去见小屋的主人和村里另外几位头面庄稼人，把他们聚到一处，讲了情况，告知我手头只有一千卢布可供姑娘们度日，问他们可愿意赊账供她们食宿，等我从美国回来。
「我发誓，欠你们的每一个戈比都还。我不仅要弄到还债的钱，还要弄到保她们终身衣食的钱。你们只管把一切开销记下来。肯信我吗？」
「肯，」庄稼人们答。「你为俄国做过许多，我们信你。」
一九一八年三月，我的敢死营的三十名病员就是这样留在图塔利斯克村的。那一千卢布交给母亲，嘱咐她给最困乏的姑娘们买鞋。领事给我的五百卢布，我拿出三百给母亲。我决定带小妹妹娜佳同去美国。父母、三十名姑娘和半个村子的人送我到车站。我重新换回女装，向东——往伊尔库茨克和符拉迪沃斯托克——出发了。
在伊尔库茨克车站，我注意到一个年轻女子，怀里抱着两个很小的孩子。她的脸不知怎么看着面熟，却想不起是谁。她显然落了难，又穷又褴褛。她盯了我一阵，忽然跑上来，气喘吁吁地喊：
「玛妮娅！」
她是基托娃家的小女儿——就是那家男人打死捕狗人、妻子随他流放的基托娃；她家随流放动身，正同我随雅沙流放同时。那时她还是个小姑娘，不过十一二岁，如今已是两个孩子的母亲。
她告诉我：她同母亲在车站地板上已经睡了三天，手里统共只剩七十戈比。母亲拿这点钱进城找住处去了！

===Original===

More than three months they had been travelling from Yakutsk, where this girl had married a political. All the money in my purse was two hundred roubles. I gave forty and then another twenty to the poor girl.
While I was nursing one of the two babies, an official approached me.
“Are you Bochkareva?” he asked.
“Yes,” I answered.
He wanted to detain me, but some of the soldiers who had travelled on the same train with me hurried to my defence. There was a hot argument. I drew out my pass from the Soviet and claimed the freedom to go wherever I pleased. I was finally left in peace.
I waited for the return of old Kitova to the last minute, desirous to see her and especially to learn about Yasha and other friends in North Siberia. Her daughter could only tell me that Yasha had married a Yakut woman, after the local fashion, and was still in Amga when last she heard.﻿ ﻿…
We resumed the journey eastward. At Khabarovsk, about 460 miles from Vladivostok, we changed trains and had to accommodate ourselves for the night at the station in the women’s waiting-room. When I was about to settle down for the night, the door opened and a voice behind me called out sharply:
“Commander Bochkareva?”
“Yes,” I replied, alarmed at this form of address.
“Are you going to England?” was the next question.
“No.”
“Where, then, are you going?”
“To Vladivostok, to stay with some relatives.”
The official then demanded my baggage in order that he might search it. He found a letter from the Moscow Consul to his Vladivostok colleague. I explained that, the Consul had helped me in Moscow and now asked the English representative at Vladivostok to help me also. The official told me in a whisper that he was only fulfilling orders, but did not sympathize any longer with Lenin’s regime. He had left four soldiers outside the room in order to facilitate matters for me. His eyes then fell on a photograph of me in the trunk. It showed me in full uniform and was the last copy in my possession. He asked for it and my autograph, and to win his good will I gave it to him without demur. He then advised me to conceal the letter from the Consul, and I sent it by Nadia to Ivanov, one of my fellow-travellers outside. One of these was a member of a provincial Soviet, an ex-Bolshevik.

===Chinese===

母女俩从雅库茨克一路辗转了三个多月——姑娘在那里嫁给了一个政治犯。我钱包里一共二百卢布，给了那可怜的姑娘四十，又添了二十。
我正抱着两个婴孩中的一个喂着，一个官员朝我走来。
「你是博奇卡列娃？」他问。
「是。」我答。
他想扣我，同车来的几个士兵急忙上前护我。一场激烈的争执。我掏出苏维埃的通行证，声称我想到哪里便到哪里。末了他们放过了我。
我等到最后一刻，盼着老基托娃回来，想见她一面，尤其想打听雅沙和北西伯利亚其他朋友的下落。她女儿只说得出：雅沙按当地的风俗娶了个雅库特女人，最后听到的信儿时他还在阿姆加。……
我们继续东行。在哈巴罗夫斯克（Khabarovsk）——离符拉迪沃斯托克约四百六十英里——我们换车，只得在车站女候车室里过夜。我正要安顿睡下，门开了，身后一个声音厉声问：
「博奇卡列娃营长？」
「是。」我答，这个称呼叫我心里一惊。
「你要去英国吗？」下一句问。
「不。」
「那你要去哪里？」
「去符拉迪沃斯托克，投亲眷。」
官员随即要检查我的行李，翻出一封莫斯科领事写给他符拉迪沃斯托克同僚的信。我解释说，领事在莫斯科帮过我，如今请符拉迪沃斯托克的英国代表也帮我。那官员低声告诉我，他只是奉命行事，心里早不同情列宁的政权了。他把四个士兵留在门外，好给我行方便。他的目光落在箱子里我的一张相片上——我着全副军装，是我手里的最后一张。他讨了相片，又讨签名；为换他的好感，我一口应了。他随即劝我把领事的信藏起，我便托娜佳把信送交给外面的旅伴伊万诺夫（Ivanov）——同行者之一，还是省苏维埃的成员，一个前布尔什维克。

===Original===

He and other soldiers aided me while I was on the train to evade the Red Guards, who used to search it daily, at various stations, for officers going to join General Semenov. More than once, in an emergency, I was concealed under their overcoats. When the Guard asked:
“Who’s there?”
“A sick comrade,” was the answer, and they passed on.
The official had received orders to take me to the town and detain me. Escorted by the four Guards, Nadia and I were taken to the police station. I was locked up, while the official went to call a meeting of the local Soviet. Nadia remained outside the cell, and I suddenly heard her cry for help. Rushing to the door, I saw through the keyhole that the Red Guards were annoying her. I banged at the door, shouting to the rascals to leave her alone, appealing to their sense of shame, but they only jeered and continued to torment her. My helplessness behind the locked door infuriated me. I dare not think of what the ruffians would have done to Nadia had not my friend Ivanov come in with two other soldiers to plead for me.
They found Nadia crying and me banging at the door in a white fury. I told them of the behaviour of the four Red Guards toward my sister, and a sharp quarrel ensued. Presently the chairman of the local Soviet and the majority of its members arrived. My case was taken up. It appeared that orders had been received from Moscow or Irkutsk to detain me. As the search had not led to the discovery of any incriminating evidence against me, my claim that I was going to Vladivostok could not be refuted.
Ivanov and the two soldiers put up a valiant defence, arguing that I was a sick woman, that they had come to know me during our companionship on the train as a real friend of the people, and that it would be a disgrace to arrest me and send me back with no evidence against me. But for these three defenders, I should in all probability have been dispatched under escort to Moscow or Tutalsk. With their aid, I was able to make such a favourable impression on the Khabarovsk Soviet that I was permitted to proceed to Vladivostok, where I arrived about the middle of April, 1918, with five roubles and seventy kopecks in my purse.
The Soviet in Vladivostok kept a close watch over all the people who were arriving and departing.

===Chinese===

在车上，多亏他和其他士兵相助，我一次次避开赤卫队——他们每天在各站搜查去投奔谢苗诺夫（Semenov）将军的军官。不止一次情急之中，我被压在他们的军大衣底下。卫兵问：
「谁在那儿？」
「一个病了的同志。」回答是这样，他们便过去了。
那官员奉命把我带进城扣押。由四名卫兵押着，我和娜佳被带进警察所。我被锁起来，官员去召集地方苏维埃开会。娜佳守在牢房外，我忽然听见她喊救命。扑到门边，从锁眼里看见赤卫兵们正在纠缠她。我捶着门，喝令那伙无赖放了她，诉诸他们的廉耻，他们只是讥笑，照旧作践她。锁门里的无能为力叫我气得发疯。若不是我的朋友伊万诺夫带两个士兵进来为我求情，那伙恶棍会对娜佳做出什么，我不敢想。
他们见娜佳哭着、我怒不可遏地捶门。我把四个赤卫兵对我妹妹的行径讲了，一场尖锐的争吵随即爆发。不久，地方苏维埃的主席和大多数委员到了，开始审理我的案子。看来莫斯科或伊尔库茨克来了扣留我的命令。可搜查既没有拿到任何不利于我的罪证，我要去符拉迪沃斯托克的说法便驳不倒。
伊万诺夫和那两个士兵挺身力辩：我是个病女人；同车一路，他们认得我，我是人民真朋友；无凭无据逮捕我、把我押回去，是耻辱。没有这三位辩护人，我多半已被押解回莫斯科或图塔利斯克。靠他们的助力，我给哈巴罗夫斯克苏维埃留下了那样的好感，获准继续前往符拉迪沃斯托克。一九一八年四月中旬到埠时，钱包里剩五卢布七十戈比。
符拉迪沃斯托克的苏维埃对所有进出的人严加监视。

===Original===

As soon as Nadia and I reached a lodging house, our documents were demanded in order that they might be sent to the Soviet for inspection. Nadia had a regular passport, while I made use of the paper from the Moscow Soldiers’ Section. It is usual for such documents to be returned to their owners with the stamp of the local Soviet on the back. But ours were slow in arriving﻿—not a good omen.
I went to the English Consul and was received in his office by an elderly Russian Colonel, who served there in the capacity of secretary and interpreter. He recognized me at once, as a telegram from Moscow announcing my coming had preceded me. The Consul was very kind and cordial when I was shown into his study, but declared that his position was such that he could not take it upon himself to obtain a passport for me from the Soviet, as he was suspected of counterrevolutionary activities.
Without revealing to the Consul the true purpose of my journey, I explained to him that my journey to London was undertaken not merely as a social visit to Mrs. Pankhurst, but as an escape from the terror of Bolshevism, which made life perilous for me anywhere in Russia. He advised me to go to the local Soviet, tell them of my desire to go to Mrs. Pankhurst, of whom the Bolsheviks had certainly heard, and ask for passports. The Consul thought that the Soviet could not find anything suspicious about my journey to his country, and would allow me to proceed unmolested. I replied with an account of some of the things I had endured at the hands of Lenin’s government and said that I was certain that my formal application for a passport would be the end of my adventure. He then telephoned to the American Consul at Vladivostok, informed him of my arrival and my plight, and enlisted his interest.
I returned to the hotel, with three hundred roubles in my purse, given me by the Consul. The place was dirty and without conveniences, but it was almost impossible to obtain decent accommodation in the city. However, the proprietor of the inn was very helpful and later saved me from trouble.
The following day the Consul told me that all efforts to win the goodwill of the Soviet toward me had not only failed but had been met with threats. The Bolsheviks might even send me back, I learned. I renewed my entreaties to the Consul to send me away, even without the Soviet’s passport.

===Chinese===

我和娜佳刚到一家客栈，就有人来收证件，要送苏维埃查验。娜佳持的是正规护照，我用的还是莫斯科士兵科那纸证明。这类证件通常加盖本地苏维埃的背戳后发还本人。可我们的迟迟不到——不是好兆头。
我去见英国领事，在办公室里接待我的是一位年长的俄国上校，在那里当秘书兼通译。莫斯科来电通报过我来的消息，他一眼便认出了我。被领进领事书房时，领事十分和蔼亲热，只是说他的处境尴尬：他已被怀疑从事反革命活动，不便再出面替我向苏维埃办护照。
我不曾向领事透露此行的真正目的，只向他解释：我去伦敦不纯是拜访潘克赫斯特太太的社交之行，而是逃离布尔什维主义的恐怖——那恐怖使我在俄国任何地方都性命难保。他劝我去找本地苏维埃，讲明我想去见潘克赫斯特太太——布尔什维克必听说过她——请发护照。领事认为，苏维埃挑不出我英国之行有什么可疑处，会放我安安静静地走。我回答时讲了几件我在列宁政府手里受过的苦，说正式申请护照之日，多半就是我这趟冒险的末日。他随即给符拉迪沃斯托克的美国领事去了电话，告知我的到来和处境，请他关照。
我回到客栈时，钱包里有了领事给的三百卢布。那地方肮脏，没有像样的设备，可城里几乎租不到体面的住处。所幸店主很肯帮忙，后来还救了我一场麻烦。
第二天领事告诉我，为赢得苏维埃对我好感的一切努力非但没有成功，反而招来威胁。我听说布尔什维克说不定还要把我押回去。我重新恳求领事送我走，哪怕没有苏维埃的护照。

===Original===

He would not promise to do so, but under the pressure of my appeals finally showed an inclination to consider the matter.
Upon leaving the Consulate I was stopped in the street by a soldier.
“Bochkareva?” he asked.
“Yes,” I answered.
“Why did you come here to tramp the streets?” was the next question.
“I came to visit my relatives, but found that they had moved, so I am going back soon.”
He let me go my way. As soon as I arrived at the inn, the proprietor took me aside to tell me that representatives of the Soviet had called in my absence, and inquired as to my doings and my plans. He had informed them that I had come to visit some relatives, but was unable to find where they were living. They had left with the threat that they would return to arrest me. I did not intend to wait for their arrival and allow myself to be detained and sent back. I telephoned to the Consul and told him of the latest development. Fortunately he had some good news for me. An American transport was to touch at Vladivostok two days later!
Nadia and I hurried to the Consulate. The Consul declared that the Bolsheviks had threatened him if he should be found aiding me to get away. Meanwhile he proceeded to have all the necessary foreign passports prepared for us, and we were photographed for that purpose. The difficulty of leaving Vladivostok without a pass from the Soviet still confronted us. The harbour was under strict supervision, and the boats that were used to ferry passengers from the shore to the steamships were manned and inspected by Bolsheviks.
For nearly two days I remained in my room, in constant dread of the appearance of Red Guards to arrest me. They did not come, however, apparently convinced that I could not escape them anyway. They had ample reason afterwards to change their minds about this. I then went to the Consul again. The American transport *Sheridan* was due that night, he said, but he was not sure yet if the Captain would be willing to take me on board.
Meanwhile we sought a means to elude the inspectors at the port. A large travelling basket was tried, and I managed to pack myself into it, but the Consul decided that I might be suffocated in case the basket should be left at the pier for a couple of hours. So I got out of the basket.
The transport arrived in the evening, and the Captain expressed his willingness to carry me across the Pacific.

===Chinese===

他不肯许诺，但被我求不过，终于显出肯考虑的意思。
一离开领事馆，我在街上就被一个士兵拦住。
「博奇卡列娃？」他问。
「是。」我答。
「来这里逛什么街？」下一句问。
「来投亲眷，他们搬走了，我很快回去。」
他放我走了。我一回到客栈，店主就拉我到一边，说苏维埃的人趁我不在来过，打听我的行止和打算。他回他们说我是来投亲、没寻见下落的。他们撂下话要回来抓我。我不想坐等他们来扣我押我，便打电话告诉领事这最新的事态。碰巧他有好消息：两天后，一艘美国运输舰要停靠符拉迪沃斯托克！
我和娜佳赶到领事馆。领事说，若被发现助我出走，布尔什维克威胁过他。可他随即着手替我们置办全部必需的外国护照，我们还为此照了相。没有苏维埃的通行证如何出符拉迪沃斯托克，仍是我们面前的难关。港口盘查森严，接送旅客登轮的驳船，船工和检查都是布尔什维克的人。
我在房间里躲了将近两天，时刻担心赤卫队破门来捕。他们没有来——显然断定我反正逃不出他们的手心。事后他们有足够的理由改变这个看法。我再去见领事。他说美国运输舰谢里登号（Sheridan）当夜抵港，只是船长肯不肯载我，还没有准话。
与此同时，我们设法躲过港口的检查。试了一只大旅行篮，我居然把自己塞了进去，可领事断定篮子若在码头上放上两个钟头，我会闷死。我只好又爬出来。
运输舰傍晚到了，船长表示愿意载我横渡太平洋。

===Original===

At the request of the Consul I remained in his house, while my sister, accompanied by an officer, went to the inn to get my things, and with them left for the vessel. Two hours later I called up the inn to find out whether Nadia had been there with the officer. The proprietor informed me that about fifty Red Guards had just been there looking for me, and had been disagreeably surprised to learn that I had departed already.
“Where did she go?” they asked the proprietor.
“To the railway station, to take a train,” he lied.
“What train?” they shouted indignantly. “There are no trains leaving tonight.” With that they went away, presumably to search for me.
I communicated to the Consul what I had learned, and he hid me in a closet. Shortly afterwards several Red Guards arrived, asking for Bochkareva. The Consul denied knowledge of my whereabouts, declared that I had come to him only once, as a result of which he had applied to the Soviet for a passport for me, but since he was refused he washed his hands of my case. The Red Guards said that I had been observed entering the Consulate, but had not been seen to leave it. They glanced about for me and then left, after the Consul’s denial of my visit.
The old Colonel returned, after taking Nadia aboard the transport, with the news that I should have company on the way, as eight Russian officers were to be passengers on the same vessel. Hundreds of Russian officers had arrived in Vladivostok in the belief that they could join the British army there and be transported to France. Unfortunately the Allies would not accept their services and they found themselves in difficult circumstances, without means to return to European Russia and with no desire to do so, as long as Bolshevism was still rampant there. Some of them succeeded by various means in making their way to the United States or Canada.
The Colonel asked me if I wanted to meet my fellow-travellers. I answered in the affirmative, and as they were at the moment at the Consulate, he took me into the room in which they were waiting. Scarcely had I crossed the threshold, when, glancing at the small group of officers, my eyes suddenly fell on Leonid Grigorievitch Filippov, my former battle adjutant, who had carried me unconscious under German fire to safety in that unhappy advance of the Battalion.
“What are you doing here?”

===Chinese===

应领事的要求，我留在他家里，由妹妹偕一位军官去客栈取我的行李，随行李直接上船。两个钟头后我打电话到客栈问娜佳到了没有。店主告知：约五十名赤卫队刚来搜过我，听说我已离埠，大为扫兴。
「她去哪儿了？」他们问店主。
「去火车站赶火车了。」他撒谎。
「什么火车？」他们怒喝。「今晚根本没有车！」说完便走了，想必是追我去了。
我把听来的告知领事，他把我藏进一间壁橱。不多时几名赤卫队进门，指名找博奇卡列娃。领事矢口不知我的下落，声明我只来过一次，他还为此向苏维埃申请过护照，遭了拒绝，此后便撒手不管我的案子。赤卫队说，有人看见我进领事馆，没见我出来。他们四下张了张，被领事一口否认后，走了。
老上校送娜佳上了运输舰回来，捎来消息：路上我有伴了，同船将有八名俄国军官乘客。几百名俄国军官到了符拉迪沃斯托克，以为能在那里参加英军、被运去法国。不幸协约国不收，他们陷入窘境：既无钱回俄国欧洲部分，也不愿回去——布尔什维主义还在那里横行。其中一些人各显神通，辗转去了美国或加拿大。
上校问我愿不愿见见同路人。我说愿意。他们此刻就在领事馆，他便领我进他们候着的房间。我一跨过门槛，目光扫过那一小群军官，猛地落在列昂尼德·格里戈里耶维奇·菲利波夫（Leonid Grigorievitch Filippov）身上——我从前的战斗副官，在那次不幸的营队攻势里，是他在德军炮火下把我昏死的身体抢到了安全地带。
「你怎么在这里？」

===Original===

both of us asked each other simultaneously, astonished at this unexpected meeting.
I had always felt that I owed my life to Lieutenant Filippov after I had been stunned by a shell and injured while running from the enemy at the end of the fruitless offensive launched by the Battalion. He had taken charge of the Battalion after I had been sent to the Petrograd hospital, and later left for Odessa to train as an aviator.
From a short private conversation I learned that Lieutenant Filippov was in the same plight as all the other officers who had come to Vladivostok under the impression that they would be accepted by the Allies. I decided to ask the Consul to allow him to assume his former post of adjutant to me and let him become a member of my party. The Consul graciously consented, and I was happy at the thought of journeying to foreign lands in the company of an educated friend, with a knowledge of languages, peoples and geography, who was also devoted to Russia with all his heart.
After another conference with the Consul, it was decided that I should be dressed as an Englishwoman and as such make an effort to get to the American transport. The necessary clothes were obtained, and in fifteen minutes I appeared no longer as a soldier, but as a veiled foreign lady who did not understand a word of Russian. Accompanied by the Colonel, I left for the harbour, after having expressed my deepest thanks to the Consul for his great sacrifices in my behalf.
I was supposed to play a speechless role and leave everything to my escort. This I did, although more than once my heart jumped when a guard seemed to scrutinize me closely, and now and then I had to suppress an impulse to laugh when the Colonel, in reply to questions, said that I was an Englishwoman returning home. It was dark when I was ferried to the transport, and everything went off without mishap. But that was not the end of the adventure.
The transport had to remain for another day in the harbour, and it was expected that the Soviet would search it for me. To baffle all attempts to discover me I was placed in a cabin, the entrance and all approaches to which were guarded. Nobody was allowed to come near the room, all inquirers being told that an important German general was detained there on his way to an American prison camp.

===Chinese===

我们不约而同地问，彼此都为这场邂逅惊愕。
我一直觉得，我的命是菲利波夫中尉救的——敢死营那场徒劳的攻势末尾，我被炮弹震昏、溃逃时受了伤。我被送进彼得格勒医院后，营队由他接掌；后来他去了敖德萨，学做飞行员。
短短一番密谈，我知道菲利波夫中尉同所有到符拉迪沃斯托克指望协约国收留的军官一样落魄。我决意求领事准他重任我从前的副官，让他加入我们一行。领事慨然应允。想到将由一位受过教育的朋友同行——他通语言、识民情、晓地理，又一心忠于俄国——我心里欢喜。
同领事再商议之后，议定我扮作一个英国女人，以这副打扮设法登上美国运输舰。衣裳置办齐了，一刻钟后，我不再是军人，而是一位蒙着面纱、一个俄文字也不懂的外国太太。由上校陪着，我向领事深深道谢——谢他为我作出的重大牺牲——随后动身去港口。
我该演一个不开口的角色，一切交给陪同。我照办了，只是卫兵稍一细看，我的心就不免一跳；上校答话时说我是一位回国的英国太太，我不止一次要按住笑。摆渡上舰时天已黑透，一切顺利无惊。可这还不是历险的尽头。
运输舰还得在港里再停一天，估计苏维埃会上舰搜我。为断绝一切搜查的指望，我被安置在一间舱房里，入口和各处通道都有人把守，任何人不得靠近；来打听的人，一律被告知：一位重要的德国将军被拘押在里头，正待解往美国的战俘营。

===Original===

Even Lieutenant Filippov did not know of the trick and was greatly worried over my non-arrival as the hour for the departure of the ship drew near. If any Bolshevik emissary was sent on board the vessel to look for me, he was stopped in front of a certain cabin by American soldiers and informed that no one would be permitted to get within so many feet of the imprisoned enemy general.
When the anchors of the *Sheridan* were raised and the ship began to move, I came out of the cabin, to the liveliest merriment of everybody who had expected to see a stern Teuton general emerge from the door.
I was free!
It was April 18, 1918, when I left Russian soil for the first time in my life. Under the American flag, on an American transport, I was heading for that wonderful land﻿—America﻿—carrying in my breast the message of the Russian peasant-soldier to the Allies:
“Help Russia to release herself from the German yoke and become free﻿—in return for the five million lives that she has sacrificed for your safety, the security of your liberties, the preservation of your own lands and lives!”

===Chinese===

连菲利波夫中尉也不知道这个障眼法。开船时刻临近，见我还不到，他急得团团转。布尔什维克果真派人上舰找我，那人在某间舱房前被美国士兵拦住，并被告知：任何人不许靠近被囚的敌国将军多少英尺之内。
谢里登号起锚、船身移动起来时，我走出舱房，把等着看一位威严的条顿将军出门的人们逗得哄堂大笑。
我自由了！
一九一八年四月十八日，我平生第一次离开俄国的土地。在美国旗下，乘着美国的运输舰，我正驶向那片神奇的土地——美国——胸前揣着俄国庄稼人士兵捎给协约国的口信：
「请助俄国挣脱德国的枷锁、重获自由——她已经为你们的安全、为保你们的自由、保你们自己的土地和性命，牺牲了五百万条生命！」

## Introduction / 引言
===Original===

In the early summer of 1917 the world was thrilled by a news item from Petrograd announcing the formation by one Maria Bochkareva of a women’s fighting unit under the name of “The Battalion of Death.” With this announcement an obscure Russian peasant girl made her début in the international hall of fame. From the depths of dark Russia Maria Bochkareva suddenly emerged into the limelight of modern publicity. Foreign correspondents sought her, photographers followed her, distinguished visitors paid their respects to her. All tried to interpret this arresting personality. The result was a riot of misinformation and misunderstanding.
Of the numerous published tales about and interviews with Bochkareva that have come under my observation, there is hardly one which does not contain some false or misleading statement. This is partly due to the deplorable fact that the foreign journalists who interpreted Russian men and affairs to the world during the momentous year of 1917 were, with very few exceptions, ignorant of the Russian language; and partly to Bochkareva’s reluctance to take every adventurous stranger into her confidence. It was her cherished dream to have a complete record of her life incorporated in a book some day. This work is the realization of that dream.
To a very considerable extent, therefore, the narrative here unfolded is of the nature of a confession. When in the United States in the summer of 1918, Bochkareva determined to prepare her autobiography. Had she been educated enough to be able to write a letter fluently, she would probably have written her own life-story in Russian and then had it translated into English. Being semi-illiterate, she found it necessary to secure the services of a writer commanding a knowledge of her native language, which is the only tongue she speaks. The procedure followed in the writing of this book was this: Bochkareva recited to me in Russian the story of her life, and I recorded it in English in longhand, making every effort to set down her narrative verbatim. Not infrequently I would interrupt her with a question intended to draw out some forgotten experiences. However, one of Bochkareva’s natural gifts is an extraordinary memory. It took nearly a hundred hours, distributed over a period of three weeks, for her to tell me every detail of her romantic life.

===Chinese===

1917年初夏，彼得格勒传来的一条消息震动了世界：一个叫玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃的，组建了一支名为「敢死营」的妇女战斗部队。随着这纸通告，一位无名的俄国农家女登上了国际声望的殿堂。从黑暗的俄罗斯深处，玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃骤然走到现代舆论的聚光灯下。外国记者寻访她，摄影师追随她，显赫的访客向她致敬。人人都想解说这个叫人一见难忘的人物，结果是一片讹传与误解的乱麻。

在我见过的已刊出的关于博奇卡列娃的种种报道和访谈里，几乎找不到不含虚假或误导之处的。这一半要归咎于一个可叹的事实：在1917年那个重大的年头里向世界解说俄国人同俄国时事的那些外国记者，除极少数外，全然不懂俄语；一半也归咎于博奇卡列娃本人不肯把每个冒险家的陌生人都引为知己。她的夙愿，是有一天把自己一生的完整记录收进一本书里。这部书，就是那个梦的实现。

因此，这里展开的叙述，在很大程度上带有自白的性质。1918年夏在美国时，博奇卡列娃决意筹备她的自传。倘若她受过足够的 教育、能够流利地写信，她多半会亲手用俄语写下自己的生平，再请人译成英文。她识字不多，便不得不请一位通晓她的母语——她唯一会讲的语言——的捉笔者。本书的写法是这样：博奇卡列娃用俄语向我讲述她的生平，我用英文长hand逐字记录，尽力把她的叙述一字一句照录下来。我不时打断她发问，好勾出些忘了的经历。不过，博奇卡列娃的天赋之一是一副惊人的记性。她把自己浪漫一生的每个细节讲给我听，前后用了将近一百个钟头，分布在三个星期里。

===Original===

At our first session Bochkareva made it clear that what she was going to tell me would be very different from the stories about her related in the press. She would reveal her innermost self and break open for the first time the sealed book of her past. This she did, and in doing so completely discredited several widely circulated tales about her. Perhaps the chief of these is the statement that Bochkareva had enlisted as a soldier and gone to war to avenge her fallen husband. Whether this invention was the product of her own mind or was attributed to her originally by some prolific correspondent, I do not know. In any event it was a convenient answer to the eternal question of importunate journalists how she came to be a soldier. Unable to explain to the conventional world that profound impulse which really drove her to her remarkable destiny, she adopted this excuse until she had an opportunity to record the full story of her courageous life.
This book will also remove that distrustful attitude based on misunderstanding that has been manifested toward Bochkareva in radical circles. When she arrived in the United States, she was immediately hailed as a “counterrevolutionary,” royalist and sinister intriguer by the extremists. That was a grave injustice to her. She is ignorant of politics, contemptuous of intrigue, and spiritually far and above party strife. Her mission in life was to free Russia from the German yoke.
Being placed virtually in the position of a father confessor, it was my privilege to commune with the spirit of this remarkable peasant-woman, a privilege I shall ever esteem as priceless. She not only laid bare before me every detail of her amazing life that memory could resurrect, but also allowed me to explore the nooks and corners of her heart to a degree that no friend of hers ever did. Maintaining a critical attitude from the beginning of our association, I was gradually overwhelmed by the largeness of her soul.
Wherein does the greatness of Bochkareva lie? Mrs. Emmeline Pankhurst called her the greatest woman of the century. “The woman that saved France was Joan of Arc﻿—a peasant girl,” wrote a correspondent in July, 1917, “Maria Bochkareva is her modern parallel.” Indeed, in the annals of history since the days of the Maid of Orleans we encounter no feminine figure equal to Bochkareva.

===Chinese===

头一次见面，博奇卡列娃就讲明白：她要告诉我的，会同报刊上流传的她的故事大不一样。她要袒露最内里的自己，头一回翻开她过去那本封了口的书。她这样做了；这一做，就擢破了好几桩流传甚广的关于她的故事。其中主要的，也许要算这一桩：说博奇卡列娃从军打仗，是为阵亡的丈夫报仇。这个编造究竟出自她自己的脑子，还是起初某个多产的记者安在她头上的，我不知道。无论如何，对纠缠不休的记者们那个永恒的问题——她怎么会当了兵——它是一句现成的回答。她无法向世俗的世界解释那股真正把她推向不寻常命运深处的冲动，便采纳了这个托辞，直到有机会把她的勇敢一生完整地记录下来。

这本书也将除去激进圈子因误解而对博奇卡列娃摆出的那副不信任的脸。她一到美国，就被极端派们欢呼作「反革命」、保皇党和阴险的阴谋家。这对她是严重的不公。她不懂政治，鄙夷阴谋，灵性上远在党争之上。她一生的使命，是把俄国从德国的枷锁下解放出来。

我实际被放在了听忏悔的神父的位置上，得与这位不寻常的农妇的灵交通——这一份特权，我将永远视若珍宝。她不但把记忆所能唤起的她惊人一生的每个细节都摊开在我面前，还容我探进她心底的角落，深到她的任何一个朋友都不曾到过。从我们合作之初我便持着审慎的态度，可渐渐地，我被她灵魂之大压倒了。

博奇卡列娃的伟大在哪里？埃米琳·潘克赫斯特夫人称她为本世纪最伟大的女性。「拯救法兰西的那个女人是贞德——一个农家女，」1917年7月一位记者写道，「玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃是她的现代对影。」确实，自奥尔良少女以来，历史的记载里再没有出现过堪与博奇卡列娃并肩的女性形象。

===Original===

Like Joan of Arc, this Russian peasant girl dedicated her life to her country’s cause. If Bochkareva failed﻿—and this is yet problematical, for who will dare forecast the future of Russia﻿—it would not lessen her greatness. Success in our materialistic age is no measure of true genius.
Like Joan of Arc, Bochkareva is the symbol of her country. Can there be a more striking incarnation of France than that conveyed by the image of Joan of Arc? Bochkareva is an astounding typification of peasant Russia, with all her virtues and vices. Educated to the extent of being able to scribble her own name with difficulty, she is endowed with the genius of logic. Ignorant of history and literature, the natural lucidity of her mind is such as to lead her directly to the very few fundamental truths of life. Religious with all the fervour of her primitive soul, she is tolerant in a fashion befitting a philosopher. Devoted to her country with every fibre of her being, she is free from impassioned partisanship and selfish patriotism. Overflowing with good nature and kindness, she is yet capable of savage outbursts and brutal acts. Credulous and trustful as a child, she can be easily incited against people and things. Intrepid and rash as a fighter, her desire to live on occasions was indescribably pathetic. In a word, Bochkareva embodies all those paradoxical characteristics of Russian nature that have made Russia a puzzle to the world. These traits are illustrated almost in every page of this book. Take away from Russia the veneer of western civilization and you behold her incarnation in Bochkareva. Know Bochkareva and you will know Russia, that inchoate, invincible, agonized, striving, rising colossus in all its depth and breadth.
It must be made unmistakably clear here that the motives responsible for this book were purely personal. In its origin this work is exclusively a human document, a record of an exuberant life. It was the purpose of Bochkareva and the writer to keep the narrative down to a strict recital of facts. It is really incidental that this record is valuable not only as a biography of a startling personality, but as a revelation of certain phases of a momentous period in human history; not only as a human document, but as an historical document as well.

===Chinese===

同贞德一样，这个俄国农家女把一生献给了祖国的事业。倘若博奇卡列娃败了——这一点尚属悬案，因为谁敢预测俄国的未来呢——那也损不了她的伟大。在我们这个唯物的时代，成功不是真正天才的量尺。

同贞德一样，博奇卡列娃是她的国家的象征。还能有比贞德的形象更醒目的法兰西化身么？博奇卡列娃是农民俄国的一个惊人的典型，连同它全部的美德和毛病。她受的教育，勉勉强强够她费力涂出自己名字，却天生一副逻辑的天才。她不识历史与文学，心智天然的清明却引她直抵人生那寥寥几条根本的真理。她以原始灵魂的全部热忱信教，又以一种配得上哲学家的宽容易人。她以每一根纤维爱着祖国，却又没有狂热的党争和自私的爱国。她满心良善和慈爱，却也能爆出野性的发作、干出粗暴的事。她像孩子一样轻信，也就容易被煽起来仇人恨事。她作为战士无畏而莽撞，求生的意志在有些场合却悲怆得难以形容。一句话，博奇卡列娃体现了俄国天性里一切看似矛盾的特点——正是它们使俄国成为世界的哑谜。这些特点几乎在这本书的每一页里都有写照。把西方文明那层釉从俄国身上剥去，你便看见她在博奇卡列娃身上的化身。认识了博奇卡列娃，你就认识了俄国——那个混沌的、不可战胜的、痛苦的、奋争的、正在起身的巨人，它的全部深和全部广。

必须在这里一清二楚地说明：促成这本书的动机纯属个人。就其本源而言，这是一部纯粹的人的文献，一段蓬勃生命的记录。博奇卡列娃和捉笔者的用意，是把叙述严格限定在事实的直陈上。这份记录之所以可贵——不但是一位惊人人物的传记，还是人类历史上一个重大时期某些侧面的揭示；不但是一部人的文献，也是一部历史文献——那只是适逢其会。

===Original===

Because Bochkareva always has been and still is strictly nonpartisan and because she does not pretend to pass judgment upon events and men, her revelations are of prime importance. The reader gets a picture of Kerensky in action that completely effaces all that has hitherto been said of this tragic but typical product of the Russian intelligentsia. Kornilov, Rodzianko, Lenin and Trotsky and some other outstanding personalities of the Russian revolution appear in these pages exactly as they are in reality.
Not a single book, as far as I know, has appeared yet giving an account of how the Russian army at the front reacted to the Revolution. What was the state of mind of the Russian soldier in the trenches, which was after all the decisive factor in the developments that followed, during the first eight months of 1917? No history of unshackled Russia will be complete without an answer to this vital question. This book is the first to disclose the reactions and emotions of the vast Russian army at the front to the tremendous issues of the revolution, and is of especial value coming from a veteran peasant soldier of the rank and file.
Perhaps surpassing all else in interest is the horrible picture we get of Bolshevism in action. With the claims of theoretical Bolshevism to establish an order of social equality on earth Bochkareva has no quarrel. She said so to Lenin and Trotsky personally. But then come her experiences with Bolshevism in practice, and there follows a blood-freezing narrative of the rule of mobocracy that will live forever in the memory of the reader.
Bochkareva left the United States towards the end of July, 1918, after having attained the purpose of her visit﻿—an interview with President Wilson. She went to England and thence to Archangel, where she arrived early in September. According to a newspaper despatch she caused the following proclamation to be posted in village squares and country churches:

===Chinese===

因为博奇卡列娃历来并且至今严格置身党派之外，因为她并不假装评判事件和人物，她的揭示就有了头等的分量。读者从这里得到的克伦斯基行事的面貌，把先前关于这个俄国知识分子的悲剧性但典型的人物所说过的一切整个抹掉了。科尔尼洛夫、罗将柯、列宁和托洛茨基，还有俄国革命的其他几个显赫人物，在本书这几页里现身，一如其人。

据我所知，至今还没有一本书，讲过前线的俄国军队对革命的感应。1917年头八个月，堑壕里的俄国士兵——说到底，这才是后来一切事态的决定因素——心里是什么状态？不回答这个生死攸关的问题，任何一部挣脱镣铐的俄国史都写不全。本书是头一部披露前线浩浩俄国大军对革命种种巨题的感应与情绪的书；它出自一名行伍出身的农民老兵，尤为可贵。

也许比一切都更抓人的，是我们从这里得到的布尔什维主义实际运行的可怖图景。对于理论布尔什维主义要在人间建立社会平等秩序的主张，博奇卡列娃并无争辩——她当面对列宁和托洛茨基这样说过。可接着便是她同实际布尔什维主义的遭遇；随之而来的是一段令人血凝的暴民统治的叙述，将长存在读者的记忆里。

博奇卡列娃达到了她此行的目的——觐见威尔逊总统——于1918年7月末离开美国。她去了英国，再从那里去阿尔汉格尔斯克，9月初抵达。据一家报纸的电讯，她叫人把下面这张告示张贴在乡村广场和乡村教堂里：

===Original===

> I am a Russian peasant and soldier. At the request of the soldiers and peasants I went to America and Great Britain to ask these countries for military help for Russia.
> The Allies understand our own misfortunes and I return with the Allied armies, which have come only for the purpose of helping to drive out our deadly enemies, the Germans, and not to interfere with our internal affairs. After the war is over the Allied troops will leave Russian soil.
> I, for my own part, request all loyal free sons of Russia, without reference to party, to come together acting as one with the Allied forces, who, under the Russian flag, come to free Russia from the German yoke and to help the new free Russian army with all forces, including Russia, to beat the enemy.
> Soldiers and peasants! Remember that only a full, clean sweep of the Germans from our soil can give you the free Russia you long for.

Isaac Don Levine.

===Chinese===

> 我是俄国的农民和士兵。应士兵和农民的请求，我去到美国和英国，为俄国向这两国请求军事援助。
> 协约国懂得我们的不幸；我随协约国军队回来了。他们来，只为帮我们赶走死敌德国人，无意干涉我们的内政。战争结束后，协约国军队将离开俄国的土地。
> 我自己则请求俄罗斯一切忠贞的自由之子，不分党派，团结起来，同协约国军队合力行动——他们在俄国旗下前来，使俄国摆脱德国的枷锁，并协助新的自由俄军，连同俄国在内，以全部力量击取敌人。
> 士兵们，农民们！记住：只有把德国人从我们的土地上彻底、干净地清除出去，才能给你们渴望的自由俄罗斯。

艾萨克·唐·莱文（Isaac Don Levine）

===Chinese===

1. 玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃着军装的肖像，下有她的签名。她神情严峻，胸前佩戴勋章和奖章。

## II Marriage at Fifteen / 二 十五岁的婚姻
===Original===

Then came the Russo-Japanese War. And with it, Siberia, from Tomsk to Manchuria, teemed with a new life. It reached even our street, hitherto so lifeless and uneventful. Two officers, the brothers Lazov, one of them married, rented the quarters opposite Nastasia Leontievna’s shop. The young Madame Lazov knew nothing of housekeeping. She observed me at work in the shop, and offered me work in her home at seven roubles a month.
Seven roubles a month was so attractive a sum that I immediately accepted the offer. What could not one do with so much money? Why, that would leave four roubles for me, after the payment of my mother’s rent. Four roubles! Enough to buy a new dress, a coat, or a pair of fashionable shoes. Besides, it gave me an opportunity to release myself from the bondage of Nastasia Leontievna.
I took entire charge of the housekeeping at the Lazovs. They were kind and courteous, and took an interest in me. They taught me how to behave at table and in society, and took care that I appeared neat and clean.
The younger Lazov, Lieutenant Vasili, began to notice me, and one evening invited me to take a walk with him. In time Vasili’s interest in me deepened. We went out together many times. He made love to me, caressing and kissing me. Did I realize clearly the meaning of it all? Hardly. It was all so new, so wonderful, so attractive. It made my pulse throb at his approach. It made my cheeks flame with the heat of my young blood.
Vasili said he loved me. Did I love him? If I did, it was more because of the marvellous world into which he was to lead me, than on account of himself. He promised to marry me. Did I particularly want to marry him? Scarcely. The prospect of marriage was more enticing to me because of the end it would put to my life of drudgery and misery than on account of anything else. To become free, independent, possessed of means, was the attractive prospect that marriage held for me.
I was fifteen and a half when Vasili seduced me by the promise of marriage. We lived together for a short while, when orders came to the Lazovs to leave for a different post. Vasili informed me of the order.
“Then we shall have to get married quickly, before you go,” I declared. But Vasili did not think so.
“That’s quite impossible, Marusia,” he said.
“Why?” I inquired sharply, something rising in my throat, like a tide, with suffocating force.

===Chinese===

随后日俄战争来了。西伯利亚从托木斯克到满洲，处处涌动起新的生活，连我们那条一向死气沉沉、无事可记的街也波及了。两名军官——拉佐夫家两兄弟，一个成了家——在娜斯塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜的铺子对面租了住处。年轻的拉佐夫太太全然不会持家；她见我在铺子里做活，便请我去她家帮工，每月七卢布。

每月七卢布，这个数目太诱人了，我一口应下。这么多钱能做什么啊！付了母亲的房租，还能剩四卢布归我。四卢布！够买一条新裙子、一件外套，或一双时兴的鞋。何况，这也给了我一个机会，挣脱娜斯塔西娅·列昂季耶夫娜的束缚。

我整个接管了拉佐夫家的家务。他们夫妇和气有礼，也关心我，教我席上、人前怎样举止，留心我穿戴得齐整干净。

弟弟瓦西里——瓦西里中尉——开始留意我，一天晚上邀我同他散步。渐渐地，瓦西里对我越来越上心。我们一同出去过许多回。他向我求爱，抚我，吻我。这一切的含义，我清楚么？谈不上。一切都太新鲜、太奇妙、太诱人。他一走近，我的脉搏就跳；年轻的血一热，我的脸颊就烧。

瓦西里说他爱我。我爱他么？就算爱，也多半是爱他将领我进去的那个奇妙世界，不是为了他这个人。他答应娶我。我特别想嫁他么？也谈不上。婚事之所以引我，多半因为它能了结我做牛做马的苦日子，不为别的。变得自由、独立、手里有钱——这才是婚姻摆在我面前的好前景。

我十五岁半，瓦西里用婚姻的许诺占有了我。我们同居了短短一阵，调令下来，拉佐夫家要开拔去别的任上。瓦西里把调令告诉了我。

「那我们得赶紧结婚，趁你还没走，」我说。可瓦西里不这样想。

「那完全办不到，玛露霞，」他说。

「为什么？」我厉声问，一样东西涌上喉咙，潮水一般，堵得我透不过气。

===Original===

“Because I am an officer, and you are only a plain *moujitchka*. You understand, yourself, that at present we can’t marry. Marusenka, I love you just as much as ever. Come, I’ll take you home with me; you’ll stay with my parents. I’ll give you an education, then we will get married.”
I became hysterical, and throwing myself at him like a ferocious animal, I screamed at the top of my voice:
“You villain. You deceived me. You never did love me. You are a scoundrel. May God curse you.”
Vasili tried to calm me. He tried to approach me, but I repulsed him. He cried, he begged, he implored me to believe that he loved me, and that he would marry me. But I would not listen to him. I trembled with rage, seized by a fit of uncontrollable temper. He left me in tears.
I did not see Vasili for two days. Neither did his brother nor sister-in-law. He had disappeared. When he returned, he presented a pitiable sight. His haggard face, the appearance of his clothes, and the odour of vodka told the story of his two-days’ debauch.
“Ah, Marusia, Marusia,” he lamented, gripping my arms. “What have you done, what have you done? I loved you so much. And you would not understand me. You have ruined my life and your own.”
My heart was wrung with pity for Vasili. Life to me then was a labyrinth of blind alleys, tangled, bewildering. It is now clear to me that Vasili did love me genuinely, and that he had indulged in the wild orgy to forget himself and drown the pain I had caused him. But I did not understand it then. Had I loved him truly, it might all have been different. But a single thought dominated my mind. “He had promised to marry me and failed.” Marriage had become to me the symbol of a life of independence and freedom.
The Lazovs left. They gave me money and gifts. But my heart was like a deserted ruin in the winter, echoing with the howls of wild beasts. Instead of a life of freedom, my parents’ basement awaited me. And deep in my bosom lurked a dread of the unknown.﻿ ﻿…
I returned home. My sisters had already noticed a different air about me. Perhaps they had seen me with Vasili at one time or another. Whatever the cause, they had their suspicions, and did not fail to communicate them to my mother. It required little scrutiny for her to observe that from a shy little girl I had blossomed forth into a young woman. And then there began days and nights of torture for me.

===Chinese===

「因为我是军官，你不过是个乡下丫头。你自己也明白，眼下我们结不成婚。玛露申卡，我爱你，同从前一样爱。来，我把你带回家去；你住我父母那儿，我供你念书，然后我们结婚。」

我发起了歇斯底里，像一头凶兽似的扑上去，扯着嗓子尖喊：

「你这恶棍。你骗了我。你从来没有爱过我。你是无赖。叫上帝咒你。」

瓦西里劝我安静，想挨近我，我把他推开。他哭了，他求，他哀告我信他爱我、会娶我。可我不听。我气得发抖，一阵按不住的火性攫住了我。他流着泪走了。

我两天没见着瓦西里，他的哥哥嫂嫂也没见着。他不见了。回来时，那副样子可怜见：熬得灰败的脸，皱乱的衣裳，一身伏特加的气味，把他两天的荒唐讲了个明明白白。

「啊，玛露霞，玛露霞，」他攥着我的胳膊哀哭。「你干了什么啊？我那样爱你，你却不肯懂我。你毁了我的命，也毁了你自己。」

我的心为瓦西里绞着疼。那时的生活于我是一座堵死的小巷结成的迷宫，乱作一团。如今我看明白了：瓦西里是真的爱我，他那场放荡的狂饮，是为了忘掉自己、淹掉我给他的痛。可当时我不懂。倘若我真爱他，一切本可以两样。可我脑子里只扭着一个念头：「他答应娶我，没有做到。」婚姻于我，已经是独立和自由的那种生活的象征。

拉佐夫家走了。他们给了我钱和礼物。可我的心像一座冬天荒废的破屋，回响着野兽的嚎。等着我 的不是自由的生活，是父母的地下室。而我的胸口深处，潜着一样对未知的恐惧。……

我回了家。妹妹们早已看出我身上不一样了，也许她们哪一回撞见过我同瓦西里在一起。不管缘由是什么，她们起了疑，又没有不告诉母亲的。母亲稍一打量便看出：一个羞怯的小姑娘，开成一朵年轻的女人了。于是我的日日夜夜，开始了受刑。

===Original===

My father quickly got wind of what had happened at the Lazovs. He was merciless and threw himself upon me with a whip, nearly lashing me to death, accompanying each blow with epithets that burned into me more than the lashes of the whip. He also beat my mother when she attempted to intervene on my behalf.
My father would come home drunk almost every day, and immediately take to lashing me. Often he would drive me and my mother barefoot out of the house, and sometimes we shivered for hours in the snow, hugging the icy walls.
Life became an actual inferno. Day and night I prayed to God that I might fall ill and die. But God remained deaf. And still I felt that only illness could save me from the daily punishment. “I must get ill,” I said to myself. And so I lay on the oven at night to heat my body, and then went out and rolled in the snow. I did it several times, but without avail. I could not fall ill.
Amid these insufferable conditions, I met the new year of 1905. My married sister had invited me to take part in a masquerade. My father would not hear, at first, of my going out for an evening, but consented after repeated entreaties. I dressed as a boy, this being the first time I ever wore a man’s clothes. After the dancing we visited some friends of my sister’s, where I met a soldier, just returned from the front. He was a common muzhik, of rough appearance and vulgar speech, and at least ten years older than myself. He immediately began to court me. His name was Afanasy Bochkarev.
It was not long afterwards that I met Bochkarev again in the house of a married sister of his. He invited me to go out for a walk, and then suddenly proposed that I should marry him. It came to me so unexpectedly that I had no time for consideration. Anything seemed preferable to the daily torments of home. If I had sought death to escape my father, why not marry this boorish muzhik? And I consented without further thought.
My father objected to my marrying since I was not yet sixteen, but without avail. As Bochkarev was penniless, and I had no money, we decided to work together and save. Our marriage was a hasty affair. The only impression of it that remains with me is my feeling of relief at escaping from my father’s brutal hands. Alas! Little did I then suspect that I was exchanging one form of torture for another.

===Chinese===

父亲很快听到了拉佐夫家出的事。他毫不留情，抄起鞭子扑到我身上，差点把我抽死；每一鞭都伴着一句骂，那些字比鞭梢烙得更深。母亲上来护我，他也打。

父亲几乎天天醉醺醺回家，进门就抽我。他常把我同母亲光着脚赶出家门，我们有时在雪地里抖上几个钟头，抱着冰墙取暖。

生活成了实实在在的地狱。我日夜求上帝叫我生病、叫我死。可上帝聋着。我仍觉得只有生病能救我出这每日的刑罚。「必须生病，」我对自己说。于是夜里我躺上炉子把身子烤热，再出去滚进雪里。干了好几回，全然无用。我病不起来。

在这熬不下去的境地里，我迎来1905年新年。成了家的姐姐邀我去参加一场化装舞会。起初父亲听也不听我晚上出门，磨破了嘴才点头。我扮成男孩——这是我头一回穿男人的衣裳。跳完舞，我们去姐姐的几个朋友家，在那里遇见一个刚从前线回来的士兵。他是个地道的庄稼汉，模样粗、言语俗，至少比我大十岁。他当即向我献起殷勤。他叫阿法纳西·博奇卡列夫（Afanasy Bochkarev）。

不久，我在他一个成了家的姐姐家里又遇见博奇卡列夫。他邀我出去散步，走着走着，忽然开口向我求婚。事情来得太突，我根本来不及掂量。什么似乎都比家里每日的折磨强。既然为躲父亲我连死都想过，嫁这个粗汉又何妨？我没再多想，应了。

父亲以我不满十六为由反对这门婚事，没有用。博奇卡列夫身无分文，我也没有钱，我们决定一起做工、攒钱。婚事办得潦草。我对它剩下的唯一印象，是逃出父亲毒手时的那阵轻松。唉！那时我何曾疑心，我不过是用一种刑罚换另一种刑罚。

===Original===

On the day following our marriage, which took place in the early spring, Afanasy and I went down to the river to hire ourselves as day labourers. We helped to load and unload lumber barges. Hard work never daunted me, and I would have been satisfied, had it only been possible for me to get along with Afanasy otherwise. But he also drank, while I did not, and intoxication invariably brutalized him. He knew of my affair with Lazov, and would use it as a pretext for punishing me.
“That officer is still in your head,” he would shout. “Wait, I’ll knock him out of it.” And he would proceed to do so.
Summer came. Afanasy and I found work with an asphalte business. We made floors at the prison, university and other public buildings. We paved some streets with asphalte. Our work with the firm lasted about two years. Both of us started at seventy kopeks (about 1s. 5½d.) a day, but I rose to the position of assistant foreman in a few months, receiving a rouble and fifty kopeks (about 3s. 2d.) a day. Afanasy continued as a common labourer. My duties required considerable knowledge in the mixing of the various elements in the making of concrete and asphalte.
Afanasy’s low intelligence was a sufficient trial. But his heavy drinking was a greater source of suffering to me. He made a habit of beating me, and grew to be unendurable. I was less than eighteen years old, and nothing but misery seemed to be in store for me. The thought of escape dug itself deeper and deeper into my mind. I finally resolved to run away from Afanasy.
My married sister had moved to Barnaul, where she and her husband worked as servants on a river steamer. I saved some twenty roubles, and determined to go to my sister, but I needed a passport. Without a passport one could not move in Russia, so I took my mother’s.
On the way, at a small railway station, I was held up by a police officer.
“Where are you going, girl?” he asked brusquely, eyeing me with suspicion.
“To Barnaul,” I replied, with a sinking heart.
“Have you a passport?” he demanded.
“Yes,” I said, drawing it out of my bag.
“What’s your name?” was the next question.
“Maria Bochkareva.”
In my confusion I had forgotten that the passport was my mother’s, and that it bore the name of Olga Frolkova. When the officer unfolded it and glanced at the name, he turned on me fiercely:
“Bochkareva, ah, so that is your name?”

===Chinese===

婚事是早春办的。第二天，阿法纳西同我下到河边去做零工，帮着装卸木排驳船。苦活从没吓退过我；只要同阿法纳西还能过，我就知足。可他也喝酒，我不喝，一醉他就变畜生。他知道我同拉佐夫的事，就拿它当由头治我。

「那军官还占着你脑袋呢，」他吼。「等着，我把他打出去。」接着他就动手打。

夏天来了。阿法纳西同我进了一家沥青行。我们给监狱、大学和别的公家房子铺地面，给几条街铺沥青。在那家行里，我们干了约莫两年。两人起头都是每天七十戈比（约合先令一先令五便士半），几个月后我升到副工头，每天一卢布五十戈比（约合三先令两便士）；阿法纳西照旧当小工。我的差事，在拌混凝土和沥青的各种配料上，颇要些学问。

阿法纳西那份低下的悟性，已够我受；他那一身酗酒，更是我的大苦。他打我打成了习惯，越来越无法忍受。我还不到十八岁，眼前却好像只有一长串灰暗的年头。逃跑的念头在我心里越扎越深。我到底拿定主意：从阿法纳西身边逃走。

成了家的姐姐搬去了巴尔瑙尔，同姐夫在一条江轮上帮工。我攒下约二十卢布，决意去投姐姐；只是需要一张护照。在俄国没有护照寸步难行，我便拿了母亲的。

路上，在一个小车站，一个警察拦住了我。

「去哪儿，丫头？」他粗声问，狐疑地打量我。

「巴尔瑙尔，」我心头一沉，答。

「有护照么？」他喝问。

「有，」我说着从包里掏出来。

「叫什么名字？」下一问。

「玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃。」

慌乱里我忘了那是母亲的护照，写的是奥莉加·弗罗尔科娃。警官展开扫了一眼姓名，猛地转过身来：

「博奇卡列娃，啊，原来你叫这个？」

===Original===

It dawned upon me then that I had committed a fatal mistake. Visions of prison, torture and eventual return to Afanasy flashed before me. “I am lost,” I thought, falling upon my knees before the officer to beg for mercy, as he ordered me to follow him to headquarters. In an outburst of tears and sobs, I told him that I had escaped from a brutal husband, and since I could not possibly obtain a passport of my own, I was forced to make use of my mother’s. I implored him not to send me back to Afanasy, for he would certainly kill me.
My simple peasant speech convinced the officer that I was not a dangerous political, but he would not let me go. He decided that I should go with him. “Come along, you will stay with me, and tomorrow I will send you to Barnaul. If you don’t, I’ll have you arrested and sent by *étape*2 back to Tomsk.”
I was as docile as a sheep. This was my first contact with the authorities, and I dared not protest. If I had any power of will it must have been dormant. Had I not found the world full of wrong since my childhood? Was not this one of the ordinary events of life? We muzhiks were created to suffer and endure. They, the officials, were created to punish and maltreat. And so I was led away by the guardian of peace and law, and made to suffer shame and humiliation.﻿ ﻿…
I was then free to go to Barnaul, and I resumed my journey. When I arrived there, my sister quickly found employment for me on the steamship. The work was comparatively easy, and my life rapidly took a happier turn. It was an immense relief to be away from my drunken, brutal husband.
But the relief was short-lived. Afanasy came to my mother after my disappearance to inquire concerning my whereabouts. She showed surprise upon hearing of my flight, and denied all knowledge of my destination. He returned to our house again and again. One day in his presence the postman delivered a letter from Shura. He seized it, and through it learned that I was in Barnaul.
One morning, as I was standing on the deck of the ship, which was anchored in the harbour, my eyes suddenly fell on a figure approaching the wharf. It was a familiar figure. In another moment I recognized it as that of Afanasy. My blood froze and my flesh crept as I realized what was coming.
“Once fallen into his hands my life would be one of continuous torture,” I thought. “I must save myself.”
But how could I escape?

===Chinese===

我这才醒悟自己犯了致命的错。监狱、刑罚、末了押回阿法纳西——种种前景在眼前闪过。「完了，」我想，一面跪倒在警官面前讨饶——他已吩咐我跟他去警察分所。我一把鼻涕一把泪，告诉他我从残暴的丈夫那里逃出来，自己的护照实在办不到，才不得不拿母亲的用。我哀求他别把我送回阿法纳西，他准会打死我的。

我这农妇的一口实话，叫警官信了我不是什么危险的政治犯，可他不肯放我走。他断定我该跟他走。「来吧，你先住我这儿，明天我送你去巴尔瑙尔。不听的话，我就抓起你，按站押解2回托木斯克。」

我绵羊一样听话。这是我头一回同官府打交道，不敢吭声。我若有过什么主见，那时也准是睡着。我从小见的世道不就充满不公么？这不过是生活里一桩寻常事。我们庄稼汉生来受苦、忍耐；他们，官老爷们，生来惩罚、糟践。就这样，这位和平与法律的守护人把我领了去，叫我受了羞辱。……

随后放我去巴尔瑙尔，我重新上路。到了那里，姐姐很快在轮船上替我找了个差事。活计比较轻省，我的日子很快有了起色。离开那个酗酒的、残暴的丈夫，是天大的松快。

可松快没多久。我一失踪，阿法纳西就寻到我母亲那里打听下落。母亲听了我的出逃很吃惊，说不知道我去了哪里。他一趟又一趟回我们家来。有一天，当着他的面，邮差送来一封舒拉的信。他抢了过去，由此得知我在巴尔瑙尔。

一天早晨，我正立在停泊在港里的轮船甲板上，眼睛忽然落在走向码头的一个身影上。是个熟身影。再一眼，我认出是阿法纳西。明白了来的是什么，我的血冻住，皮肉发麻。

「再落进他手里，我的日子就是连绵的酷刑，」我想。「必须自救。」

可我怎么逃？

===Original===

If I were on land I might still have a chance. Here all avenues are closed. There he is already approaching the gate to the wharf. He is stopping to ask a question of a guard, who nods affirmatively. Now, he is walking a little faster. His face wears a grin that strikes terror into my heart. I am trapped.﻿ ﻿… But no, just wait a moment, Afanasy. Don’t be sure of your triumph yet. I rush to the edge of the deck, cross myself and jump into the deep waters of the Ob. Ah, how thrilling it is to die! So I have outwitted Afanasy, after all. It’s cold, the water is cold. And I am going down, down.﻿ ﻿… I am glad. I am triumphant. I have escaped from the trap﻿ ﻿… into the arms of death.
I awoke, not in heaven, but in the hospital. I was observed jumping into the river, dragged out unconscious, and revived.
The authorities questioned me as to the cause of my attempted suicide, and drew up a protocol. I told them of my husband, of his brutality, and the utter impossibility of living with him.
Afanasy was waiting in the anteroom, to see me. My attempt at suicide had seriously upset him. It aroused a sense of shame in him. Touched by my story, the authorities went out and angrily rebuked him for his maltreatment of me. He admitted his guilt, and swore that he would be gentle to me in the future.
He was then admitted to the ward in which I lay. Falling on his knees, he begged my forgiveness, repeating his oath to me and professing his love for me in the most affectionate terms. His entreaties were so moving that I finally consented to return home with him.
For a while Afanasy was truly a different man. In spite of his coarse habits, I was deeply touched by his efforts to be kind. However, that did not last long. We resumed our life of drudging toil. And vodka resumed its grip on him. Once drunk, he became just as brutal again.
Gradually life with Afanasy grew as insufferable as it had been before my escape. That summer I turned nineteen, and I saw ahead of me nothing but a long series of dreary years. Afanasy wanted me to take to drink. I resisted, and that infuriated him. He made it a habit to torment me daily. He would hold a bottle of vodka to my face, and scoffing at me for my efforts to lift myself above my condition, he would endeavour by blows and kicks to force the bitter drink down my throat.

===Chinese===

若在岸上，兴许还有一线机会。这里条条路都堵死了。瞧，他已经走到码头门口，停下问一个看守，看守点头应是。现在，他走得快些了。他脸上挂着一副笑，笑得我心里发寒。我落网了。……不，慢着，阿法纳西。先别急着庆你的功。我奔到甲板边上，画了个十字，跳进鄂毕河深水。啊，死多么痛快！这样，我到底赢过了阿法纳西。水冷，水真冷。我在往下沉，往下沉。……我高兴。我得意。我逃出了罗网……投进了死亡的怀抱。

我醒来，不在天堂，在医院里。我跳河时被人看见，捞起来时不省人事，救活了。

当局盘问我自杀的缘由，立了笔录。我讲了丈夫，讲他的暴虐，讲同他一起生活怎样全然不可能。

阿法纳西在前厅候着见我。我自杀这一下把他狠狠吓着了，也勾起了他的羞耻。当局被我的陈述打动，出去狠狠训了他糟践我。他认了错，起誓往后好好待我。

随后放他进我躺的病房。他跪倒在地，求我饶恕，把誓又起了一遍，拿最温存的话诉说对我的爱。他求得那样动人，我到底应了随他回家。

有一阵，阿法纳西真像换了个人。尽管一身粗习难改，他讨好的那些努力深深感动了我。然而好景不长。我们重新过起做牛做马的日子，伏特加也重新攥住了他。一醉，他照旧变畜生。

渐渐地，同阿法纳西的日子又熬不下去了，同我出逃以前一样。那年夏天我满十九，望见的眼前只是一长串灰暗的年头。阿法纳西要我学喝酒。我不肯，他恨透了。他把天天折磨我当成了功课：拿一瓶伏特加怼着我的脸，讥笑我不自量力想高出自己的命，拳打脚踢，硬把那苦水往我喉咙里灌。

===Original===

One day he even stood over me with a bottle of vodka for three whole hours, pinning me down to the ground so that I was unable to move a muscle. Still I refused to give in.
Winter came. I baked bread for a living. On Sundays I went to church to pray God to release me from my bondage. Again the thought of escaping took root in my mind. The first requisite was, of course, a passport, so I went secretly to a lawyer for advice, and he undertook to obtain one for me legally. But ill-luck attended me. When the police-constable called to deliver the passport to me, Afanasy was at home. My scheme was discovered and my hopes were dashed to the ground. Afanasy hurled himself at me and bound me hand and foot, deaf to my entreaties and cries. I thought my end had come. In silence he carried me out of the house and tied me to a post.
It was cold, very cold. He flogged me, drank, and flogged me again, cursing me in the vilest terms.
“That’s what you get for trying to escape,” he bawled, holding the bottle to my mouth. “You won’t escape any more. You will drink or you will die!”
I was obdurate and implored him to leave me alone. He continued his flogging, however, keeping me for four hours tied to the post, till I finally broke down and drank the vodka. I became intoxicated, staggered out into the street, and fell on the pavement in front of the house. Afanasy ran after me, cursing and kicking me. We were quickly surrounded by a crowd. My neighbours, who knew of his cruelty to me, came to my help. Afanasy was roughly handled, so roughly, indeed, that he left me in peace for some time afterwards.
Christmas was drawing near. I had saved, little by little, fifty roubles (about £5 5s. 7d.). Every kopek of that money had been earned by extra toil during the night. It was all the earthly possession that I had, and I guarded it jealously. Somehow, Afanasy got wind of its hiding-place and stole it. He spent it all on drink.
I was mad with fury upon discovering the loss. What the money meant to me in the circumstances is difficult to describe. It was my blood, my sweat, a year of my youth. And he, the beast, squandered it in one orgy. The least I could do to my torturer was to kill him.
In a frenzy, I ran to my mother, who was struck by the expression of my face.
“Marusia, what ails you?”
“Mother,” I gasped, “let me have an axe. I am going to kill Afanasy.”

===Chinese===

有一天，他甚至拿一瓶伏特加在我头顶上站了整整三个钟头，把我摁在地上，动一动也不许。我仍不肯依。

冬天来了。我烤面包谋生。星期天去教堂，求上帝解我出这枷锁。逃跑的念头又在我心里扎下根。头一件要紧的自然是护照，我便悄悄去请教一位律师，他答应替我合法办一张。可我时运不济：警察送护照上门那天，阿法纳西正好在家。我的盘算败露，指望砸了个粉碎。阿法纳西朝我扑来，把我手脚捆住，任我哭喊哀求，全不理会。我以为我的死期到了。他一言不发，把我抱出屋子，绑在一根柱子上。

天冷，冷极了。他抽我，喝酒，再抽我，用最脏的话咒我。

「想逃，这就是下场，」他吼着，把瓶子怼到我嘴上。「看你还逃不逃。喝，不然就是死！」

我硬扛着，求他放过我。他照旧抽，把我绑在柱子上整整四个钟头，直到我最后垮下来，喝了那伏特加。我醉了，踉跄着冲上街，栽倒在屋前的人行道上。阿法纳西追出来，边骂边踢。人群很快围拢。邻居们知道他待我的狠，上来帮我。阿法纳西挨了一顿好收拾——收拾得着实不轻——此后倒安静了我好一阵。

圣诞节近了。我一点一点攒下了五十卢布（约合五镑五先令七便士），那一文一文，全是夜里加班挣的。那是我全部的家当，我看得紧紧的。不知怎么，阿法纳西探到了藏钱的地方，偷了，一个子儿不剩，喝了个精光。

发觉钱没了，我气得发了疯。那笔钱在那样的处境里于我是什么，很难说得清。它是我的血，我的汗，我一年的青春。而他，这头畜生，一场荒唐就挥霍光了。对折磨我的人，我至少做得出的一件事，是杀了他。

我一阵疯狂，跑到母亲那里；她一看我的脸色就怔了。

「玛露霞，你怎么了？」

「妈，」我喘着说，「给我一把斧头。我要杀了阿法纳西。」

===Original===

“Holy Mother, have mercy!” she exclaimed, raising her hands to Heaven, and falling on her knees, she implored me to come to my senses. But I was too frantic with rage. I seized an axe and ran home.
Afanasy returned, drunk, and began to taunt me with the loss of my precious savings. I was white with wrath and cursed him from the depth of my heart. He gripped a stool and threw it at me. I caught up the axe.
“I will kill you, you bloodsucker!” I screamed.
Afanasy was stupefied. He had not expected that from me. The desire to kill was irresistible. Mentally, I already gloated over his dead body and the freedom that it would bring me. I was ready to swing the axe at him.﻿ ﻿…
Suddenly the door flew open and my father rushed in. He had been sent by my mother.
“Marusia, what are you doing?” he cried out, gripping my arm. The break was too abrupt, my nerves collapsed, and I fell unconscious to the floor. Upon awakening I found the police in the house, and I told them everything. Afanasy was taken to the police-station, while the police-officer, a very kindhearted man, advised me to leave the town to get away from him.
I got my passport, but my money was gone. I could not afford to buy a ticket to Irkutsk, where Shura had moved from Barnaul. Determined to go at all costs I boarded a train without a ticket. The conductor discovered me on the way, and I cried and begged him to allow me to proceed. He proposed to hide me in the baggage car and take me to Irkutsk, upon his own conditions. Enraged, I pushed him violently from me.
“I will put you off at the next station,” he shouted at me, running out of the car. And he kept his word.
Nearly all the distance to Irkutsk was yet before me, and I wanted to get there without selling myself for the price of a ticket. There could be no thought of going back. I had to get to Irkutsk. I boarded the next train, and stealthily crouched under a seat, as it moved out of the station.
Ultimately I was discovered, but this conductor was an elderly man and yielded to my tears and entreaties. I told him of my experience with the first conductor and of my total lack of money. He allowed me to proceed, and whenever an inspector boarded the train, he would signal to me to hide under the seat. Sometimes I would spend several hours at a stretch there, concealed by the legs of some kind passengers.

===Chinese===

「圣母啊，慈悲！」她扬手向天喊起来，跪下去求我醒醒。可我气疯了。我抓起一把斧头，跑回家去。

阿法纳西喝醉回来，拿我丢钱的事嘲弄我。我气得煞白，从心底里咒他。他抓起一只板凳朝我砸来。我抄起了斧头。

「我要杀了你，你这吸血鬼！」我尖叫。

阿法纳西呆住了。他没料到我会这样。杀的欲望压不住。我心里已经在快意他僵冷的尸首和它将带来的自由。斧头已经抡得起来。……

忽然门撞开了，父亲冲了进来，是母亲叫他来的。

「玛露霞，你在做什么？」他喊着一把握住我的胳膊。这一断断得太骤，我的神经垮了，昏倒在地板上。醒来时屋里是警察，我把一切照实说了。阿法纳西被带去了警察所；那位警官是个心肠很好的人，劝我离开这座城，躲开他。

护照拿到了，钱却没有了。舒拉已从巴尔瑙尔搬去伊尔库茨克，可我买不起去那里的车票。我不计代价也要去，便不买票上了车。半路列车员发现了我，我哭求他让我坐到底。他提出把我藏进行李车带去伊尔库茨克——自有他的条件。我怒起，狠狠把他推开。

「下一站把你撵下去，」他冲我嚷着跑出车厢。他说到做到。

去伊尔库茨克的路程几乎整个还在前头，而我不想为一张车票的价钱卖掉自己。回头是想也不必想的。必须到伊尔库茨克。我搭上下一班车，趁车开出站时，悄悄缩到一个座位底下。

末了还是被发现了，可这一位列车员上了年纪，让我的眼泪和哀求说动了。我告诉他头一个列车员怎样待我、我怎样身无分文。他放我走；查票员一上车，他就朝我打信号，我便缩回座位底下。有时我能在那里一气待上几个钟头，靠好心的旅客的腿遮着。

===Original===

In this manner I journeyed for four days, finally reaching my destination﻿—Irkutsk.

===Chinese===

我就这样走了四天，终于到了目的地——伊尔库茨克。

## I My Childhood of Toil / 一 我的苦役童年
===Original===

My father, Leonti Semenovitch Frolkov, was born into serfdom at Nikolsko, a village in the province of Novgorod, some two hundred miles north of Moscow. He was fifteen when Alexander II emancipated the serfs in 1861, and remembers that historic event vividly, being fond even now of telling of the days of his boyhood. Impressed into the army in the early seventies, he served during the Russo-Turkish War of 1877﻿–﻿78, and distinguished himself for bravery, receiving several medals. When a soldier he learned to read and write, and was promoted to the rank of sergeant.
Returning home at the end of the war, he passed through Tcharanda, a fishermen’s settlement on the shore of a lake, in the county of Kirilov, within thirty miles of Nikolsko. No longer dressed as a muzhik, military in gait and bearing, with coins jingling in his pocket, he cut quite a figure in the poor hamlet of Tcharanda. There he met my mother, Olga, the eldest daughter of Elizar Nazarev, perhaps the most destitute inhabitant of the place.
Elizar, with his wife and three daughters, occupied a shabby hut on the sandy shore of the lake. So poor was he that he could not afford to buy a horse to carry his catch to the city, and was compelled to sell it, far below the market price, to a travelling buyer. The income thus derived was not sufficient to keep the family from hunger. Bread was always a luxury in the little cabin. The soil was not tillable. Elizar’s wife would hire herself to the more prosperous peasants in the vicinity for ten kopeks (about 2½d.) a day to labour from sunrise to sunset. But even this additional money was not always to be had. Then Olga would be sent out to beg for bread in the neighbouring villages.
Once, when scarcely ten years old, little Olga underwent a harrowing experience, which she could never later recall without horror. Starting home with a basketful of bread, collected from several villages, she was fatigued but happy at the success of her errand, and hurried as fast as she could. Her path lay through a forest. Suddenly she heard the howling of a pack of wolves. Olga’s heart almost stopped beating. The dreadful sounds drew nearer. Overcome by fright, she fell unconscious to the ground.
When she regained her senses, she found herself alone. The wolves apparently had sniffed her prostrate body and gone their way.

===Chinese===

我的父亲，列昂季·谢苗诺维奇·弗罗尔科夫（Leonti Semenovitch Frolkov），生在尼科利斯科耶（Nikolsko）的农奴家里——那是诺夫哥罗德省的一个村子，在莫斯科以北约二百英里。一八六一年亚历山大二世解放农奴时他十五岁，那件历史大事他记得清清楚楚，至今还爱讲少年时代的日子。七十年代初他被征入军队，在一八七七至七八年的俄土战争中服役，以勇武出众，得过几枚奖章。当兵时他学会了读写，升到中士。
战争结束还乡，他路过恰兰达（Tcharanda）——基里洛夫县境内一个湖岸上的渔村，离尼科利斯科耶不出三十英里。他不再是庄稼人的装束，行止一副军人气派，口袋里钱币叮当作响，在贫穷的恰兰达小村出足了风头。就在那里他遇见了我的母亲奥莉加（Olga）——叶利扎尔·纳扎列夫（Elizar Nazarev）的长女；叶利扎尔恐怕是那地方最穷的人了。
叶利扎尔同妻子和三个女儿住在湖边沙岸上一间破棚屋里。他穷得买不起一匹马把鱼获驮进城，只得贱卖给上门的鱼贩——价钱远低于市价。这点进项不够一家人糊口，小屋里面包从来是稀罕物。地又种不了。叶利扎尔的妻子出去给近旁宽裕些的庄稼人帮工，从日出干到日落，一天挣十戈比（约两便士半）。可连这点额外的钱也常有没处挣的时候。于是便打发奥莉加去邻村讨面包。
有一回，小奥莉加不过十岁上下，经历了一场惨痛的事，后来一想起来就发怵。她挎着满满一篮从几个村子讨来的面包回家，又累又欢喜——差事办得顺——只顾快走。路要穿过一座树林。忽然，她听见一片狼嚎。奥莉加的心几乎停跳。那可怕的声音越来越近。她吓昏过去，倒在了地上。
等她醒来，四下只有她一个人。狼群显然嗅了嗅她摊在地上的身子，自去了。

===Original===

Her basket of bread was scattered in all directions, trampled in the mud. Out of breath, and without her precious burden, she arrived home.
It was in such circumstances that my mother grew to be nineteen, when she attracted the attention of Leonti Frolkov, who was then stopping in Tcharanda on his way home from the war. She was immensely flattered when he courted her. He even bought her a pair of shoes for a present, the first shoes she had ever worn. This captivated the humble Olga completely. She joyously accepted his marriage proposal.
After the wedding the young couple moved to Nikolsko, my father’s birthplace, where he had inherited a small tract of land. They tilled it together, and with great difficulty managed to make ends meet. My two elder sisters, Arina and Shura, were born here, increasing the poverty of my parents. My father, about this time, took to drinking, and began to maltreat and beat his wife. He was by nature morose and egotistical. Want was now making him cruel. My mother’s life with him became one of misery. She was constantly in tears, always pleading for mercy and praying to God.
I was born in July, 1889, the third girl in the family. At that time many railroads were being built throughout the country. When I was a year old, my father, who had once been stationed at Tsarskoye Selo, the Tsar’s place of residence near the capital, decided to go to Petrograd to seek work. We were left without money. He wrote no letters. On the brink of starvation, my mother somehow contrived, with the aid of kind neighbours, to keep herself and her children alive.
When I was nearly six years old a letter came from my father, the first he had written during the five years of his absence. He had broken his right leg and, as soon as he was able to travel, had started home. My mother wept bitterly at the news, but was glad to hear from her husband whom she had almost given up for dead. In spite of his cruelty toward her, she still loved him. I remember how happy my mother was when my father arrived, but this happiness did not last long. Poverty and misery cut it short. My father’s harsh nature asserted itself again. Hardly had a year gone by when a fourth child, also a girl, arrived in our family. And there was no bread in the house.

===Chinese===

那一篮面包撒得满地，踩进了泥里。她上气不接下气，空着两手到了家。
我母亲就是在这样的境地里长到十九岁，引来了列昂季·弗罗尔科夫的注目——那时他正战后还乡，歇在恰兰达。他来向她求爱，她受宠若惊。他还给她买了一双鞋作礼物——她平生穿的头一双鞋。卑微的奥莉加被彻底俘虏了，欢欢喜喜应了婚事。
婚后小两口迁到尼科利斯科耶，我父亲的出生地，他在那里继承了一小块地。两人一齐耕种，勉强掘得住日子。我的两个姐姐阿林娜和舒拉生在这里，父母的贫困又添一层。大约这时，父亲喝上了酒，开始作践、殴打妻子。他天性明沉又自私，匮乏如今又把他变得残忍。母亲同他的日子成了一场苦难，她整天抹泪，只会讨饶、祷告上帝。
我生在一八八九年七月，是家里第三个女儿。那时全国正到处修铁路。我一岁那年，父亲——他从前在皇村（Tsarskoye Selo）驻防过，那是首都附近沙皇的驻地——决意去彼得格勒找工作。我们娘几个被撂下，没有钱，他也不来信。濒于饿死的母亲，靠好心的邻居们帮衬，总算把孩子们拉活着。
我快六岁时，父亲来了信——离家五年间的头一封。他摔断了右腿，一能上路便动身回家。母亲听了放声痛哭，却又欢喜：几乎当死了的丈夫，终于有了音讯。无论他怎样待过她，她仍然爱他。我记得父亲到家时母亲多么欢喜，可这欢喜没有多久，穷苦把它掐断了。父亲那副狠性子又冒了头。不出一年，家里添了第四个孩子，还是个女孩。而家里没有面包。

===Original===

From all parts of our section of the country peasants were migrating that year to Siberia, where the Government allowed them large grants of land. My father wanted to go, but my mother was opposed to it. However, when our neighbour, Verevkin, who had left sometime before for Siberia, wrote glowingly of the new country, my father made up his mind to go, too.
Most of the men would go alone, obtain grants of land, till them, build homesteads, and then return for their families. Those of the peasants who took their families with them had enough money to tide them over. But we were so poor that by the time we got to Chelyabinsk, the last station in European Russia, and the Government distribution point, we had not a penny left. At the station my father obtained some hot water to make tea, while my two elder sisters were sent to beg for bread.
We were assigned to Kuskovo, eighty miles beyond Tomsk. At every station my sisters would beg food, while father filled our teakettle with hot water. Thus we got along till Tomsk was reached. Our grant of land was in the midst of the taiga, the virgin Siberian forest. There could be no thought of immediately settling on it, so my father remained in Tomsk, while the rest of us were sent on to Kuskovo. My sisters went to work for board and clothing. My mother, still strong and in good health, baked bread for a living, while I took care of the baby.
One day my mother was expecting visitors. She had baked some cakes and bought a pint of vodka, which she put on the shelf. While she was at work I tried to lull the baby to sleep. But baby was restless, crying incessantly. I did not know how to calm her. Then my eyes fell on the bottle of vodka.
“It must be a very good thing,” I thought, and decided to give a glass to baby. Before doing so I tasted it myself. It was bitter, but I somehow wanted more. I drank the first cup and, the bitterness having somewhat worn off, I drained another. In this manner I disposed of the entire bottle. Drowsy and weak, I took the baby into my arms and tried to rock it to sleep. But I myself began to stagger, and fell with the child to the floor.
Our mother found us there, screaming at the top of our voices. Presently the visitors arrived, and my mother reached for the bottle, only to discover that it had been emptied. It did not take her long to find the culprit.

===Chinese===

那一年，我们那一带四面八方的庄稼人都在往西伯利亚迁，官府在那里拨给他们大块的地。父亲想去，母亲不肯。可邻人韦列夫金（Verevkin）先前去了西伯利亚，来信把那新地方夸得天花乱坠，父亲便也拿定了主意。
多数人是单身先去，领了地、耕种起来、搭起农庄，再回头接家眷；带家眷同去的，都是有几个钱撑过渡口的。我们却穷得还没到车里雅宾斯克——俄国欧洲部分最后一站，也是官府的分地点——便一文不剩了。在站上，父亲讨了些热水冲茶，两个姐姐被打发去讨面包。
我们被拨往库斯科沃（Kuskovo），在托木斯克以东八十英里。每到一站，姐姐们讨吃的，父亲灌满我们的茶炊。就这样支撑着到了托木斯克。分给我们的地在泰加林——西伯利亚原始林——的深处，立刻安家谈也谈不上。于是父亲留在托木斯克，我们其余人被送往库斯科沃。姐姐们去帮工，挣吃住；母亲身子还结实，烤面包卖为生；我看孩子。
有一天，母亲等客人来。她烤了些饼，又买了一品脱伏特加，搁在架上。她干活时，我哄小妹妹睡觉。可孩子闹觉，哭个不停，我不知道怎么安抚她。这时我的眼睛落在了那瓶伏特加上。
「那必是顶好的东西。」我想，便决意给孩子喝一杯。倒之前，我先自己尝了尝。苦，可不知怎么还想喝。我喝了第一杯，苦味淡了些，又干了一杯。就这样，我把一瓶全报销了。我晕晕乎乎、浑身发软，抱起孩子摇她睡。可我自己站不稳了，抱着孩子一起摔在地板上。
母亲找到我们时，我们正扯着嗓子嚎。客人随后来到，母亲伸手去拿酒瓶，才发现早已空了。不用多久，她就找出了罪魁。

===Original===

I shall always remember the whipping I got on that occasion.
Toward winter my father arrived from Tomsk. He brought little money with him. The winter was severe, and epidemics were raging in the country. We fell ill one by one, father, mother, then all the girls. As there was no bread in the house, and no money to buy anything, the community took care of us till the spring, housing and feeding us. By some miracle all of us escaped death, but our clothes had become rags. Our shoes fell to pieces. My parents decided to move to Tomsk, where we arrived barefoot and tattered, finding shelter at a poor inn on the outskirts of the town.
My father would work only a couple of days a week. He was lazy. The remainder of the week he idled away and drank. My sisters served as nursemaids, while my mother worked in a bakery, keeping the baby and me with her. We slept in the loft of a stable, with the horses stamping below us. Our bed was of straw laid on the floor, which consisted of unshaven planks thrown across logs. Soon the baker’s wife began to object to feeding an extra mouth, which belonged to me. I was then over eight years old.
“Why don’t you send her to work? She can earn her own bread,” she argued.
My mother would draw me to her breast, weep and beg for mercy. But the proprietress became impatient, threatening to throw us all out.
Finally my father came to see us, with the good tidings that he had found a place for me. I was to care for a five-year-old boy, in return for my board and eighty-five kopeks a month.
“If you do well,” my father added, “you will by and by receive a rouble.”
Such was the beginning of my career in life. I was eight and a half years old, small and very thin. I had never before left my mother’s side, and both of us wept bitterly at parting. It was a grey, painful, incomprehensible world into which I was being led by my father. My view of it was further blurred by a stream of tears.
I took care of the little boy for several days. One afternoon, while amusing him by making figures in the sand, I myself became so engrossed in the game that I quarrelled with my charge, which led to a fight. I remember feeling keenly that I was in the right. But the child’s mother did not inquire into the matter. She heard his screams and whipped me for it.
I was deeply hurt by the undeserved whipping administered by a strange woman.
“Where was my mother?

===Chinese===

那回挨的鞭子，我一辈子记得。
入冬，父亲从托木斯克来了，带来的钱寥寥。那年冬天奇寒，乡间瘟病流行。我们一个接一个病倒：父亲、母亲，然后所有的女孩。家里没有面包，也没有钱买任何东西，全靠村坊养活到开春，供我们住、供我们吃。奇迹般地，一家人谁也没死，只是衣裳都成了破片，鞋子散了架。父母决计迁往托木斯克。到时人人赤脚褴褛，在城边一家下等客栈里安了身。
父亲一个星期只做两三天工——他懒，一礼拜剩下的日子闲混、喝酒。姐姐们出去当保姆，母亲进了一家面包房做工，把小妹妹和我带在身边。我们睡在马厩的阁楼上，马在底下刨着蹄子。床是铺在地板上的稻草，地板不过是搭在圆木上的几块没刨过的板。不久，面包房老板娘开始嘀咕多喂一张嘴——我这张。那时我八岁多。
「怎么不打发她去做工？她挣得了自己的面包。」她这样数落。
母亲把我搂在怀里，哭着讨情。可老板娘不耐烦了，威胁要把我们全部赶出去。
末了父亲来看我们，带来好消息：替我寻了个去处。我去照看一个五岁的男孩，管吃住，一个月八十五戈比。
「干得好，」父亲补一句，「往后加到一个卢布。」
我的人生就是这样开的头。我八岁半，又小又瘦，从没离开过母亲身边，分别时我们两个哭得伤心。父亲幸着我走进的，是一个灰暗、痛楚、叫人不懂的世界；一串泪水又把它在我眼前糊得更花。
我照看了那小男孩几天。一天下午，我在沙地上画人形逗他玩，自己也玩得入了迷，同我的小主人吵起来，动了手。我记得心里明明委屈得很，觉得占理的是我。可孩子的母亲不问情由，一听他尖叫，便拿我是问，抽了我一顿。
一个陌生女人这样无缘无故打我，我伤心透了。
「我的母亲在哪里？

===Original===

Why did not she come to avenge me?”
My mother did not answer my cries. Nobody did. I felt miserable. How wrong was the world, how unjust. It was not worth while living in such a world.
My feet were bare. My dress was all in rags. Nobody seemed to care for me. I was all alone, without friends, and nobody knew of the yearning in my heart. I would drown myself, I thought. Yes, I would run to the river and drown myself. Then I would go up, free of all pain, into the arms of God.
I resolved to slip out at the first chance and jump into the river, but before the opportunity presented itself my father called. He found me in tears.
“What’s the matter, Manka?” he asked.
“I am going to drown myself, papa,” I answered sadly.
“Great Heavens! What’s happened, you foolish child?”
I then poured out my heart to him, begging to be taken to my mother. He caressed me and talked of my mother’s distress if I left my place. He promised to buy me a pair of shoes, and I remained.
But I did not stay long. The little boy, having seen his mother punish me, began to take advantage of me, making my life quite unbearable. Finally I ran away and wandered about town till dark, looking for my mother. It was late when a policeman picked me up crying in the street and carried me to the police-station. The officer in charge of the station took me to his home for the night.
His house was rather large. I had never been in such a house before. When I awoke in the morning it seemed to me that there were a great many doors in it and all of them aroused my curiosity. I wanted to know what was behind them. As I opened one of the doors, I beheld the police-officer asleep on a bed, with a pistol by his side. I wanted to beat a hasty retreat, but he awoke. He seized the pistol and, still dazed from sleep, threatened me with it. Frightened, I ran out of the room.
