Petrukhin then told me that Peter had aroused such compassion for me among the members of the Investigation Committee that they had agreed to go with him to Sablin, and petition the Commander-in-Chief to send me to Moscow for trial by a military tribunal. About fifty soldiers were also won over to my side by Peter’s accounts of Yashka’s work in the trenches and No Man’s Land, and of my reputation among all the men. Petrukhin had remained at my bedside till I recovered consciousness, but he now wished to join the deputation. I thanked him gratefully for his kindness towards me and his desperate efforts to save my life.
Before he left, word reached him that Pugatchov had incited some of the men against me, threatening to kidnap and lynch me before I was taken away. Petrukhin placed five loyal friends of his at my compartment, with orders not to surrender me at any cost.
I prayed to God for Petrukhin, and hearing my prayer he said:
“Now, I, too, believe in God. The appearance of this man, Peter, was truly miraculous. In spite of all my efforts, you would have been executed but for him.”
“But what are my chances of escaping death now?” I asked.
“They are still very small,” he answered. “Your record is against you. You do not deny being a friend of Kornilov.

===Chinese===

他们围住我，我被打出行列，带出了刑场。普加乔夫气得发白，疯子似的咆哮，牙咬得咯咯响。我们走开时，他野兽般的嗓门在背后吼：

「朝膝盖打！」

一阵排枪响起。哭喊和呻吟顿时充满空气。我回头，看见那群野兽端着刺刀扑向那一堆受害，把刺刀深深捅进几分钟前还是我同伴的身体，再用靴跟碾出最后一点生命的迹象。

可怕，可怕得没法说。那呻吟太钻心、太瘆人，我踉跄了一下，直挺挺栽倒在地，晕了过去。

我昏了四个钟头。醒来时，我在一节火车车厢的包间里。彼得鲁欣坐在我身边，握着我的手，流着泪。

一想到我昏倒前的那一切，普加乔夫的身影便浮上眼前，我当场立誓：若能逃出布尔什维克的陷阱，一有机会就杀了他。

彼得鲁欣随后告诉我：彼得在调查委员会的委员们中间唤起了那样深的怜悯，他们同意同他一起去见萨布林，呈请总司令把我送莫斯科交军事法庭审判。彼得讲述雅什卡在堑壕和无人地带的作为、讲述我在全体官兵中间的名声，还赢来了约五十名士兵。彼得鲁欣在我床边一直守到我苏醒，此刻他要去同代表团会合。我深深谢过他的善意、谢过他救我性命的那些拼命的努力。

他临走前，有话传来：普加乔夫煽动了一些士兵，聁言在我被带走前劫走我、私刑处死我。彼得鲁欣派了五个可靠的友人守在我包间外，命令他们无论如何不得把我交出去。

我为彼得鲁欣向上帝祷告；他听了我的祷告，说：

「如今我也信上帝了。彼得这个人的出现，真正是奇迹。任凭我怎样尽力，若没有他，你已经枪毙了。」

「可我现在逃出死地的指望呢？」我问。

「还是极小，」他答。「你的履历对你不利。你是科尔尼洛夫的朋友，这你不否认。

===Original===

Your strict discipline in the Battalion and your fighting the Germans at a time when the whole front was fraternizing, are known here. Besides, the death penalty has become so customary here that it would be very unusual for one to escape it. Only the other day a physician and his wife, on their way to Kislovodsk to the springs, somehow arrived in Zverevo. They were arrested, attached to a party about to be shot, and executed without any investigation. Afterwards papers from their local Soviet were found in their clothes, certifying that they were actually ill, the physician suffering from a cancer, and requesting that they should be allowed to proceed to Kislovodsk.”
Petrukhin kissed my hand, and left, warning me:
“Wait here till I return. Nobody will harm you in my absence.”
He locked the door behind him. I took out the little bottle of holy water, given to me by my youngest sister, Nadia, and drank it. On my knees before the little icon, I prayed long and devoutly to God, Jesus, and the Holy Mother. My ears caught a noise outside the car: it came from several menacing soldiers who wanted to get in and kill me on the spot. I prayed with greater fervour than before, pleading for my life in the name of my mother, my father and my little sister. My heart was heavy with sorrow and despair.
As I was hugging the little icon, tears streaming from my eyes, I suddenly heard a voice, a very tender voice, say to me:
“Your life will be saved.”
I was alone in the compartment. I realize that it is a daring statement to make. I do not seek to make anyone believe it. It may be accepted or not. But I am satisfied that I did hear the voice of a divine messenger. It was soothing, elevating. Suddenly I felt happy and calm. I thanked the Almighty for His boundless grace and vowed to have a public prayer offered at the Moscow Cathedral of Christ the Saviour at the first opportunity, in commemoration of His miraculous message to me.
Then I fell asleep, and rested calmly till the arrival of Petrukhin. His face was wreathed in smiles, he clasped my hand joyfully, saying:
“Thank God! Thank God! You are at least saved from the mob. Sablin has ordered you to be sent to Moscow. The necessary papers are being prepared now.”
At this point Peter came in, followed by some members of the investigation committee. All were happy. It was a wonderful moment.

===Chinese===

你营里那套铁的纪律、全前线都在联欢时你却同德国人打仗——这里全知道。再说，死刑在这里已经家常便饭，谁能逃掉反倒稀奇。前几天就有一位医生带着妻子去基斯洛沃茨克疗养，不知怎么到了兹韦列沃，被抓起来，绑进一批待枪毙的人里，没经任何调查就毙了。事后从他们衣袋里查出当地苏维埃的证件，证明他们确实有病——医生患着癌——并请求准他们前往基斯洛沃茨克。」

彼得鲁欣吻了我的手，临走叮嘱我：

「在这儿等我回来。我不在，没人敢动你。」

他随手锁了门。我取出小妹妹娜佳给我的那一小瓶圣水，喝了。我跪在那尊小小的圣像前，久久虔诚地祷告，向上帝、向耶稣、向圣母。我的耳朵捕到车外的嘈杂：是几个凶横的士兵，想闯进来当场杀死我。我祷告得比先前更炽热，以母亲、父亲和小妹妹的名分求饶我这条命。我的心沉满悲哀和绝望。

我正抱着那尊小圣像，泪从眼里涌流，忽然听见一个声音，一个极温柔的声音，对我说：

「你的命会得救。」

包间里只有我一个人。我知道这话胆大。我不求任何人相信；信不信由人。可我确信：我听见的，是一位神圣使者的声音。它抚慰，它提升。忽然间我快活了、安宁了。我感谢全能者无边的恩典，并许愿：一有机会，就在莫斯科救世主大教堂做一场公开的祈祷，纪念他这奇迹般的传谕。

随后我睡着了，安安稳稳睡到彼得鲁欣回来。他满脸堆笑，欢喜地攥住我的手，说：

「谢天谢地！谢天谢地！你总算从暴民手里救下来了。萨布林已下令送你去莫斯科。文书正在办。」

这时彼得进来了，后面跟着调查委员会的几名委员。人人都快活。那是一个奇妙的时刻。

===Original===

How an act of humanity transforms men’s countenances! Peter and his comrades congratulated me, and I was too overcome to express all the gratefulness that I felt toward these men.
Petrukhin then narrated how he had dealt with the infuriated soldiers, who had clamoured for my life. He told them that I was being led away to Moscow in the hope that I would there deliver up several counterrevolutionary Generals, associated with Kornilov.
“Will she be shot afterwards?” they inquired.
“Of course,” Petrukhin declared. The lynchers went away satisfied.
I was curious to know what would be done to me in Moscow. Petrukhin replied to my inquiries that among the papers relating to my case, which my escort would take with them to Moscow, the chief document was the protocol. That protocol had been drawn up by himself, in the capacity of chairman of the Investigation Committee. He described in it how I had lost my way while going to Kislovodsk, getting stranded at Zverevo, and how I had reported of my own free will to the authorities, adding that I had with me a ticket to Kislovodsk, an invitation from Princess Tatuyeva to come to the Caucasus, *and a certificate from a physician certifying to my ill-health*. The last was, of course, an invention. Petrukhin sent the ticket and the letter from Tiflis, adding that he had misplaced the physician’s certificate and would send it on later.
“It is unlikely,” he said to me, “that you would be punished with death in the face of such evidence. I should expect your release, sooner or later. But in any event, here is a poison pill. I prepared it for you originally to take in case the mob got their way, so that you should escape torture at the hands of these savages. I hope you will not need to resort to it in Moscow.”
I still carry with me that poison pill wherever I go.﻿ ﻿…
Petrukhin gave me forty roubles for expenses, as I was penniless. I thanked him and asked him to write a letter to my people, telling them where I was. We then took leave of one another. Petrukhin and Peter exchanged kisses with me, and I again and again reiterated how much I owed to them, swearing that in any future emergency, whatever happened, I would always be ready to do everything within my power for them. We all realized that many a change was still in store for Mother Russia, before she settled down to a peaceful existence.

===Chinese===

一桩仁慈的行为，能把人的脸变个样子！彼得同他的同志们向我道贺，我激动得说不出对这些人满腔的感激。

彼得鲁欣随后讲了他怎样应付那伙嚷着要我命的狂怒士兵：他告诉他们，把我押去莫斯科，是指望我在那里供出几个同科尔尼洛夫勾结的反革命将军。

「那她往后还是要毙？」他们问。

「当然，」彼得鲁欣宣告。要私刑的人们满意地散了。

我急于知道莫斯科会怎样处置我。彼得鲁欣答：随解送的卫队带去莫斯科的本案文书里，头一件是笔录。那份笔录是他以调查委员会主席的身份亲自写的，里面描述：我如何在去基斯洛沃茨克的路上迷了路、困在兹韦列沃；如何自愿向当局报到；又附言我身边带着去基斯洛沃茨克的车票、塔图耶娃公主邀我去高加索的请帖，*还有一份医生开具的病情证明*。最后这件当然是编造。彼得鲁欣把车票和第比利斯的信附了上去，又说医生的证明放失了地方，随后补寄。

「有这样的证据摆在面前，」他对我说，「你被处死，多半不像了。我指望你迟早获释。可无论如何，这有一粒毒药。我原本备下给你，是防暴民得手时用的——免得你落进这些野人手里受刑。我希望你在莫斯科用不着它。」

那粒毒药，我至今走到哪儿带到哪儿。……

彼得鲁欣给了我四十卢布做盘缠，我身无分文。我谢了他，又请他给我家里人写封信，告诉他们我在哪里。随后我们互道珍重。彼得鲁欣和彼得同我吻别；我一再重申我欠他们的情，起誓说：日后不论出了什么急难，只要用得着我，我随时为他们尽力。我们都明白：俄罗斯母亲要安顿到太平的日子里，前头还有的是变故。

===Original===

Accompanied by my friends and surrounded by four armed guards, forming my escort, I was led to an empty railway coach, attached to an engine. On this train, consisting of cattle-trucks and my coach, I was taken to Nikitino. There I was brought before the Commandant, with a request to provide accommodation for the party on an ordinary train. It was the very Commandant who had helped me so generously to get to Zverevo on the munition train. Of course, he did not recognize the Sister of Mercy in Bochkareva.
On the platform I had another striking encounter. The news that Bochkareva had been seized and was being taken to Moscow became known in the station and a number of Red Guards and soldiers gathered about me, showering upon me insults, curses and threats. Among these, in the foremost rank, was the repulsive-looking man who was in charge of the train on which I went to Zverevo and who had proposed marriage to me.
The beast did not recognize me now. He sneered in my face, and repeated my name syllable by syllable, taking a peculiar joy in distorting it and railing generally at my appearance and reputation.
“The slut! We have got her, the harlot!” he raved. “Only I can’t understand why they didn’t shoot her there. Why bother with such a slut!”
I could not help laughing. I laughed long, without restraint. It was so amusing. I was almost tempted to disclose to him how I had duped him. He still has no idea that Alexandra Smirnova, whose fictitious address at Kislovodsk he, in all probability, cherishes yet, was Maria Bochkareva!
For three days I travelled with my escort from Nikitino to Moscow. I was treated with consideration, but always as a prisoner. The guards would get food for me and themselves at the stations on the way. Upon our arrival at Moscow I was taken in a motorcar to the Soldiers’ Section of the Soviet, established in what was formerly the Governor’s mansion. My guards delivered me to a civilian, with all the documents of the case, and left.
“What, coming from Kornilov?” the official asked me gruffly.
“No, I was on my way to take the cure at Kislovodsk,” I replied.
“Ah, yes, we know those cures! What about the epaulets? Why did you take them off?”
“Because I am a plain peasant woman. I have defended my country bravely for three years. I am not guilty.”
“Well, we will see about that later,” he interrupted and ordered me to be led away to prison.

===Chinese===

朋友们陪着，四名武装卫兵围着我充作押送，我被带上一节挂在机车后的空车厢。就是这一列车——几节货车皮加我这节车厢——把我载去尼基季诺。到了那里，我被带到卫戍司令面前，请求他为这一行人安排普通客车的铺位。这位司令，正是慷慨帮我搭军火车去兹韦列沃的那一位。当然，他没有把博奇卡列娃同那个修女认作一人。

站台上我又有一场不寻常的遭遇。博奇卡列娃被抓、正被押解莫斯科的消息传遍了车站，一群赤卫队和士兵围住我，侮辱、咒骂、威胁，劈头盖脸。冲在最前头的，就是那副叫人作呕的面孔——我搭去兹韦列沃那趟车的管事，向我求过婚的那个。

这畜生此刻没认出我。他冲我的脸冷笑，把我名字一个音节一个音节地念，从蹂躪它的发音里取乐，顺带把我的人样和名声骂了个遍。

「这烂货！逮住她了，这娼妇！」他狂言。「就是不明白，怎么不就地毙了她。这种烂货，费什么事！」

我忍不住笑起来，笑了很久，毫不收敛。太可乐了。我几乎想把被我耍了的经过说给他听。他至今多半还揣着「亚历山德拉·斯米尔诺娃」在基斯洛沃茨克的那个假地址，做梦也不知道那就是玛丽亚·博奇卡列娃！

从尼基季诺到莫斯科，我同押送走了三天。他们待我有礼，却始终当我是囚犯。沿途各站，卫兵们给我和他们自己买吃的。到了莫斯科，我被一辆汽车送到苏维埃士兵部——设在从前的总督府里。卫兵们把我连同本案全部文书移交给一个文职人员，走了。

「怎么，从科尔尼洛夫那儿来的？」那官员粗声问我。

「不，我是去基斯洛沃茨克疗养的，」我答。

「啊，是，这种『疗养』我们知道！那肩章呢？为什么扯了？」

「因为我是个普通农妇。我为国打了三年仗，打得很勇敢。我没有罪。」

「哼，这个回头再说，」他打断我，下令把我押进监狱。

===Original===

I was locked up in a small cell, in which there were already about twenty prisoners, officers and civilians, all arrested by agents who had overheard them talk against the Bolshevik regime! A fine reincarnation of the worst methods of Tsarism.
The cell was in a frightful condition. There was no lavatory in it, and the inmates were not permitted to leave the room! The stench was indescribable. The men smoked incessantly. The prisoners were not even allowed to take the short, daily promenade outside, which was granted by the old regime.
Apparently in order to make me confess, I was subjected to a new form of torture, never practised by the Tsar’s jailers. I was denied food! For three days I did not receive even the niggardly ration given to the other prisoners. My companions were all kind to me, but the portions that they received were barely enough to sustain life in their own bodies. So for three days and three nights, I lay on the bunk, in a heap under a cover, on the point of suffocation, starved, feverish, and thirsty, as no water was allowed me.
During these days the Commandant of the prison, a sailor, would come in several times daily to torment me with his tongue.
“What are you going to do to me?” I asked.
“What? You will be shot!” was his answer.
“Why?”
“Ha, ha. Because you are a friend of Kornilov’s.”
Those were the hours when I hugged the pill given me by Petrukhin, expecting every moment an order to face a firing squad.
Soon one of the arrested officers, who had been caught cursing Bolshevism while drunk, was set free. Before he went some of his companions entrusted him with messages to their relatives. I thought of the Vasilievs, who had so kindly taken me from the hospital to their home in the autumn of 1916, and begged the officer to visit them and tell them of my plight. He promised to do so and carried out his pledge. I sent them a message that I expected to be executed and asked their help.
When Daria Maximovna got the message she was horrified and immediately set out to get permission to see me. But when she called at the Soldiers’ Section for a pass to see Bochkareva, she was taken for a friend of Kornilov’s and would have been badly mauled if not for the fact that her son Stepan, who had belonged to my Company and who had brought about the friendship between his mother and myself, was now one of the Bolshevik chiefs.

===Chinese===

我被关进一间小牢房，里面已有约二十名囚犯，军官和平民都有，全是被密探听见讲布尔什维克政权坏话抓进来的！沙皇时代最恶劣的那套手段，就这样转世投胎。

牢房的状况可怕。屋里没有厕所，人却不许出屋！恶臭没法形容。烟抽个不停。连旧政权准许的每日那点放风，也不给囚犯。

大约是要逼我招供，我被加了一种新式的刑罚，沙皇的狱卒从没用过的：不给饭吃！整整三天，连别的囚犯那点少得可怜的口粮，我也没有领到。难友们待我都好，可他们自己那一份，勉强够吊住自己的命。于是三天三夜，我躺在铺上，蒙在被子里蜷作一堆，憋得要窒息，饿着，烧着，渴着——水也不给。

这几天里，监狱长——一个水兵——一天几趟进来，拿舌头磨我。

「你们要把我怎样？」我问。

「怎样？枪毙你！」这就是回答。

「为什么？」

「哈，哈。因为你是科尔尼洛夫的朋友。」

那些时辰，我都攥着彼得鲁欣给我的那粒药，时刻等着被拉去面对行刑队。

不久，一个被捕的军官——喝醉了骂布尔什维主义被抓住的——获释了。临走，几个同伴托他给家里捎话。我想起瓦西里耶夫一家：1916年秋，是他们好心把我从医院接到家里去的，便求那军官去他们家，讲讲我的处境。他应承了，也办到了。我捎去口信：我等着被处决，求他们相助。

达丽娅·马克西莫夫娜得了信，吓坏了，立刻动身去弄探视许可。可她到士兵部申请探望博奇卡列娃的通行证时，被当成了科尔尼洛夫的朋友，若不是她的儿子斯捷潘——他曾在我的连里当兵，正是他促成了他母亲同我的交情——如今是布尔什维克的头目之一，她准要挨一顿好打。

===Original===

Daria Maximovna cried out that she was the mother of Stepan Vasiliev, of such and such a department, and he was brought to identify his mother.
This rescued her from a severe punishment. She appealed to her son to intervene in my behalf, but he refused, saying that he could not come to the aid of an avowed friend of Kornilov’s. He, however, obtained a pass to my cell for his mother. Later he responded to her entreaties and did say a few good words for me, telling the proper authorities that I was a simple peasant woman with no understanding of politics.
On the fourth day of my imprisonment I received a quarter of a pound of bread, some tea and two cubes of sugar. The bread was black, consisting partly of straw. I could not even touch it and had to satisfy myself with three cups of tea. Later in the day a sailor came in, and, addressing me as comrade, informed me that one Vasilieva was waiting to see me. I was weak, so weak that I could not move a few feet without assistance. As soon as I got up and made a step, I sank back on the bed in a helpless condition.
“Are you ill?” the sailor asked.
“Yes,” I murmured.
He took me by the arm and led me to a chair in the office. I was bathed in perspiration after the little walk and so dizzy that I could not see anything. When Daria Maximovna saw me she fell on my neck and wept. Turning to the officials, she cried out bitterly:
“How could you ever have such a woman arrested and subjected to torture? A woman who was so kind to the soldiers, and suffered so much for your own brothers!”
She then opened a package, took out some bread and butter, and handed them to me with these words:
“Manka, here is a quarter of a pound of bread. All we got today was three-eighths of a pound. And this is a quarter of a pound of butter, our entire ration.”
I was full of gratitude to this dear woman and her children, who had sacrificed their own portions for me. The bread was good. The difficulty was, according to Daria Maximovna, to get enough for them all. Even their meagre ration was not always obtainable.
I then told her my troubles and the punishment I was expecting, begging her to write to my mother in case of my execution.
I spent two weeks in that abominable cell before I was taken before the tribunal. I was marched along the Tverskaya, Moscow’s chief thoroughfare, and recognized on the way by the crowds.

===Chinese===

达丽娅·马克西莫夫娜喊出她是斯捷潘·瓦西里耶夫的母亲——在某处某处办事的——斯捷潘被叫来认他的母亲。

这才救她免了一场重罚。她求儿子为我说项，他不肯，说帮不了一个自认的科尔尼洛夫的朋友。不过他替母亲办了到我牢房的探视证。后来他拗不过母亲的恳求，也的确替我说了几句好话，告诉有关方面：我是个简单农妇，全然不懂政治。

监禁的第四天，我领到四分之一磅面包、一点茶和两块方糖。面包是黑的，掺着稻草，我碰也没法碰，只喝了三杯茶算数。晚些时候一个水兵进来，称我「同志」，说一位瓦西里耶娃等着见我。我弱得没人撩扶走不了几步。刚起身一迈步，便无助地跌回床上。

「你病了？」水兵问。

「是，」我低声说。

他搀着我的胳膊，把我扶到办公室的一把椅子上。走这么几步，我汗透了一身，头晕得什么都看不见。达丽娅·马克西莫夫娜一见我，扑上来搂住我的脖子就哭。她转向那些官员，悲愤地喊：

「你们怎么能逮捕这样的女人、这样折磨她？一个待士兵那样好、为你们的亲弟兄吃了那么多苦的女人！」

她随后打开一个纸包，拿出面包和黄油，递给我，说：

「曼卡，这是四分之一磅面包。我们今天一共领了八分之三磅。这四分之一磅黄油，是我们全家的定量。」

我满心感激这位亲爱的妇人同她的孩子们——他们把自己的份省给了我。面包是好面包。难处，照达丽娅·马克西莫夫娜说，是一家人要吃饱不容易，连那点微薄的定量也常领不齐。

我随后把自己的遭难和等着我的刑罚讲给她听，求她万一我被处决，写信给我的母亲。

我在那间肮脏的牢房里关了两个星期，才被提去见法庭。我被押着走过特维尔大街——莫斯科的头号大道——一路被人群认出。

===Original===

The tribunal was quartered in the Kremlin. For a couple of hours I waited there, at the end of which time I was surprised to see Stepan Vasiliev come in and approach me.
“Marusia, how did you ever get into this?” he asked me, shaking my hand and inviting me to sit down.
I told him the story of my going to Kislovodsk to take the cure.
“But how did you ever get to Zverevo?” he inquired.
“I had a ticket to Kislovodsk. I did not know that Zverevo was such a forbidden place. Once they sold me a ticket, I thought it all right to follow the regular route,” I answered excitedly.
“I spent a couple of hours yesterday examining your case and the documents relating to it, but I could not quite understand how you got to Zverevo,” Stepan said. “Perhaps you really did go to see Kornilov?”
“I do not deny my friendship for Kornilov,” I declared, glad at heart that Stepan had turned up in such a position of authority. “But you know that I am almost illiterate and understand no politics and do not mix with any party. I fought in the trenches for Russia and it is Mother Russia alone that interests me. All Russians are my brothers.”
Stepan answered that he knew of my ignorance of political matters. He then went out to report to the tribunal, and shortly afterwards I was called in. There were six men, all common soldiers, seated at a long table covered with a green cloth, in the middle of a large hall, richly decorated. I was asked to sit down and tell my story, and how I got into Zverevo. The six judges were all young men, not one of them over thirty.
I was about to rise from the chair to tell my story, but was very courteously asked to remain seated. I then told of my wound in the back, of the operation that I still needed for the extraction of a piece of shell, and of my consulting a Petrograd physician who had advised me to go to the Springs at Kislovodsk. I said that I had heard of the fighting between Kornilov and the Bolsheviks, at Novocherkassk, but had no idea what a civil war was like and had never thought of a front in such a struggle. I, therefore, continued my journey to Nikitino, where the Commandant had sent me on to Zverevo. Of course, I failed to mention the fact that the Commandant had sent on to Zverevo, not Bochkareva, but Smirnova, a Sister of Mercy.

===Chinese===

法庭设在克里姆林宫里。我在那里等了两个来钟头，末了惊喜地看见斯捷潘·瓦西里耶夫走进来，朝我走来。

「玛露霞，你怎么会落到这一步？」他握着我的手，请我坐下。

我把去基斯洛沃茨克疗养的经过讲给他听。

「可你怎么会到了兹韦列沃？」他问。

「我买的是去基斯洛沃茨克的票。我不知道兹韦列沃是那样的禁地。人家既卖了我票，我只当照正常的路线走没有错，」我激动地答。

「昨天我花了两个钟头审你的案子和有关文书，可总弄不明白你怎么到的兹韦列沃，」斯捷潘说。「莫非你真是去看科尔尼洛夫的？」

「我不否认同科尔尼洛夫的交情，」我宣告，心里庆幸斯捷潘竟处在这样有权的位置上。「可你知道我几乎不识字，不懂政治，不沾任何党派。我在堑壕里为俄国打过仗，我心里只有俄罗斯母亲。一切俄国人都是我的弟兄。」

斯捷潘回答，我于政治一无所知，这他知道。他随后出去向法庭报告，不多时我被叫了进去。六个人，全是普通士兵，坐在大厅正中一张铺绿呢的长桌后；大厅装饰华丽。他们请我坐下，讲我的经历，讲我怎么到的兹韦列沃。六位法官全是年轻人，没有一个过三十岁。

我刚要从椅子上起身讲述，被客客气气地请坐着讲。我便讲了我背上的伤，讲还等着做取出弹片的那场手术，讲彼得格勒一位医生嘱我去基斯洛沃茨克矿泉疗养。我说：科尔尼洛夫同布尔什维克在诺沃切尔卡斯克打仗的事，我听说了；可内战是什么样子，我全无概念，也没想过那样的仗还会有「前线」。于是我继续赶路，到了尼基季诺；那里的卫戍司令把我往兹韦列沃送。当然，我没有提：司令送往兹韦列沃的不是博奇卡列娃，而是修女斯米尔诺娃。

===Original===

I concluded with the statement that as soon as I reached Zverevo I realized that I was in a dangerous situation, and had surrendered to the local authorities.
I was informed that it would take a week for my case to be cleared up and a decision reached. Instead of sending me to the Butirka, the prison in which I had spent the last two weeks, I was taken to the military guard house, opposite the Soldiers’ Section. Drunken sailors and Red Guards were usually confined there. The room in which I was put was narrow and long, the windows were large but closely grated. There were about ten prisoners in it.
“Ah, Bochkareva! Look who’s here!”
I was met with these words as soon as I crossed the threshold. They quickly turned into phrases of abuse and ridicule. I was quiet, and sought seclusion and rest in a corner, but in vain. The inmates were Bolsheviks of the lowest sort, degenerates and former criminals. I was the object of their constant ill-treatment, so that torturing me day and night became their diversion. If I tried to sleep, I soon found someone near me. When I ate or drank, the beasts assembled about me, showering insults on me and playing dirty tricks. Weeping had no effect on them. Night after night I was forced to stay awake, sometimes throwing myself upon an intruder with my teeth in an effort to drive him away. I implored the warder to give me a cell to myself.
“Let it be a cold, gloomy hole. Give me no food. But take me away from these drunken brutes!” I would plead.
“We will take you away soon﻿—to shoot you!” the warder would joke in reply, amid the uproarious laughter of my tormentors.
The appointed week elapsed and still there was no decision in my case. The days﻿—long, cruel, agonizing days﻿—passed slowly by. The impossibility of sleeping was above all so torturing that it drove me to a state actually bordering on insanity. Two and a half weeks I lived in that inferno, seventeen days without a single full night’s sleep!
Then one morning the warder, who had delighted daily in telling me stories of what would be done to me, very vivid stories of frightfulness, came in with some papers in his hand.
“Bochkareva!” he called out to me. “You are free.” And he opened the door facing me.
I was so surprised that I thought at first that this was another trick to torture me.
“Free?” I asked. “Why?”

===Chinese===

我末了说：一到兹韦列沃，我就明白自己陷进了险境，随即向当地当局投案自首。

他们通知我：案件查清、作出裁决，需要一个星期。我没有被送回这两周住着的布蒂尔卡监狱，而是被送进了士兵部对面的军人禁闭室。那里通常关喝醉的水兵和赤卫队。关我的那间屋子又窄又长，窗大却钉着密密的栅。屋里约有十名囚犯。

「嗬，博奇卡列娃！瞧瞧这是谁！」

我一跨进门檻，迎头就是这一句，很快变成成串的辱骂和嘲弄。我安静，躲到角落里寻个清静，也是徒劳。这些囚犯是最低一等的布尔什维克、堕落分子和前科犯。我成了他们日夜虐待的对象，折磨我成了他们的消遣。我想睡，不一会儿就觉出身边有人；我吃喝，那些畜生便围拢来，泼我一身侮辱，使尽肮脏的捉弄。哭对他们没用。一夜又一夜，我被迫醒着，有时只好带着牙扑向凑上来的人，把他赶开。我哀求看守给我一间单人牢房。

「冷的、黑的窖洞也行。不给饭也行。只求把我从这些醉畜生身边带走！」我这样哀告。

「很快就带你走——去枪毙！」看守这样打趣，引得虐待我的人哄堂大笑。

指定的一个星期过完了，我的案子还是没有裁决。日子——漫长的、残忍的、煎熬的日子——慢慢地捱。不能睡觉最是磨人，磨得我几乎到了疯狂的边上。那个地狱我熬了两个半星期，十七天没有囫囵睡过一夜！

随后一天早晨，那个天天以给我讲我的下场为乐、讲得活龙活现的看守，手里拿着几张纸进来了。

「博奇卡列娃！」他朝我喊。「你自由了。」说着推开了我面前的门。

我惊得先当这又是折磨我的又一个把戏。

「自由？」我问。「为什么？」

===Original===

I had grown to believe the warder’s tales of what awaited me, and I could not imagine him as the carrier of such tidings.
“Am I free for good?” I asked.
“Yes,” was the answer. “You will go with a guard to the Soldiers’ Section, where you will get the necessary papers.”
I bade farewell, with a sigh of relief, to the chamber of horrors, and went immediately to get the document from the tribunal, which stated that I had been arrested but found innocent of the charge and that, as I was ill, I was to be allowed complete freedom of movement in the country. With this passport in my pocket I was set at liberty.

===Chinese===

看守的那些等着我的故事，我已听得入了骨，想不出这样的喜讯竟出自他的口。

「是真放，一放到底？」我问。

「是，」回答是。「有卫兵带你去士兵部，领必要的文书。」

我长出一口气，同这座恐怖之屋道了别，立刻去法庭领取文书。上面写着：我曾被捕，经查无罪；又因我患有疾病，准予在全国完全自由通行。揣着这张护照，我获得了自由。

## Part / 部
===Original===

IV Terror

===Chinese===

四 恐怖

## XV The Army Becomes a Savage Mob / 十五 军队变成兽群
===Original===

My women were enthusiastic over the return of their Commander. I reported to the Commander of the Corps and was invited to luncheon with the Staff. The officers were interested to know what was going on in the rear. I did not tell them the details of the quarrel between the Prime Minister and the Commander-in-Chief, but I did indicate in general terms that a difference had arisen.
Toward the end of the meal it was reported that the Chairman of the Corps Committee had come to see the Commander on important business. It appeared that the corps in the trenches was to be relieved at seven in the evening and orders had been issued to the corps in reserve, some miles behind, to move toward the trenches at five in the morning. However, they had not moved. The Chairman now came to explain the cause of the delay. He was himself a patriotic and intelligent soldier and was asked to sit down by the General while he told the story.
“The rascals!” he said of the men who had elected him as their leader, “they wouldn’t move. They have been holding meetings all the morning and refuse to go to relieve their comrades.”
We were all shocked. The General became excited.
“What the devil!” he exclaimed angrily. “That passes all bounds! If the soldiers refuse to relieve the very men who had relieved them a couple of weeks ago, then there is no use in continuing at the front, making a pretence of war. It’s a farce! It’s no use staying here, let them lay down their arms and go home and save the Government the trouble of keeping up the semblance of an army. The villains! Just shoot a few of them, and they will learn to do their duty! At seven o’clock the trenches will be empty. Go and tell them that I command them to move immediately!”
The Chairman returned to the billets and told his soldiers that the General ordered them into the trenches under penalty of death. This incensed the men.
“Aha, he is threatening to shoot!” cried one.
“He’s of the old regime,” exclaimed another.
“He wants to practise on us the Tsar’s methods!” shouted several voices.
“He is a blackguard!” suggested another.
“He ought to be killed! He wants to rule us with an iron hand!” the men roared, working themselves up to a fever.
Meanwhile, the news came from the trenches that the men were holding meetings there, proclaiming their determination not to remain in their position after seven o’clock.

===Chinese===

女兵们见统帅回来，欢腾一片。我向军团司令报到，应邀同参谋们共进午餐。军官们急于知道后方的事。总理同总司令争吵的细节，我没有讲，只笼统点了一句：两人之间生了分歧。

饭快吃完时，有人报：军团委员会主席来见司令，有要事。原来：堑壕里的这个军今晚七点该换防，命令已下给几英里外预备位置的另一个军，今辇五点向堑壕开拔。可他们没动。主席现在来解释耽搁的缘由。他自己是个爱国而明白事理的士兵，将军请他坐下讲。

「这些无赖！」他说起选他当头头的那些人，「他们不肯动。开了一上午的会，拒绝去换自己同志的防。」

我们全都震惊了。将军激动起来。

「见鬼！」他怒道。「这还有边么！士兵们若不肯换防——换的是两星期前刚换过他们的人——那前线还有什么待头，还打什么仗的样子？这是闹剧！待在这儿没用，叫他们撂下枪回家，也替政府省了养一支空架子军队的麻烦。这些恶棍！毙他几个，他们就懂得尽本分了！七点一到，堑壕就要空了。去告诉他们：我命令他们立刻开拔！」

主席回了营房，对士兵们说：将军命令他们进堑壕，违者处死。这一下把士兵们惹翻了。

「啊哈，他聁言要枪毙！」一个喊。

「他是旧派，」另一个喊。

「他要拿沙皇那一套整我们！」几个嗓子齐喊。

「他是个恶棍！」又有人出主意。

「该杀了他！他要拿铁腕骑在我们头上！」人们吼，越吼越热。

与此同时，堑壕里传来消息：士兵们在那里开会，宣布七点一过决不留在阵地上。

===Original===

The General was in great difficulty. He was faced with the probability of his section of the front being left entirely open to the enemy. He telephoned to the reserve billets and asked the Chairman of the Committee what was going on there.
Suddenly the General grew pale, dropped the receiver and said:
“They want to kill me.”
Chief of Staff Kostayev took up the receiver and in a trembling voice inquired what the trouble was. I listened to the answer.
“They are in an ugly mood. They have mutinied and threaten to mob the General. The excitement is spreading, and some of them have already started out for Headquarters.”
The voice of the Chairman at the other end of the wire was clearly expressive of his alarm. In reply to questions what the General could do to calm the mob he said that the committee admired and respected the General, that its members were doing their best to allay the passions that had been aroused, but seemed helpless.
A few minutes later several officers and men ran into the house, greatly agitated.
“General, you are lost if you don’t get away in time!” one of them said.
Shortly afterwards Colonel Belonogov, a man of sterling heart, beloved by his soldiers even before the revolution, rushed in. He brought the same tidings, asking the General to hide. I joined in, imploring the Commander to conceal himself till the storm had passed. But he refused.
“Why should I hide?” he exclaimed. “What wrong have I done? Let them come and kill me! I have only done my duty.”
He went into his study and locked himself in.
The mob was moving nearer and nearer. There was a deathly pallor on the faces of all those present. Every minute or so someone would dash in breathlessly, with eyes full of horror, to herald the approaching tempest.
The tide of tumultuous humanity reached the house. There were cries and howls. For a second we were all in suspense. Then Colonel Belonogov said he would go out and talk to them and try to make them see reason. The Colonel had a gentle voice and a gentle heart. He never addressed even his own orderly in the ordinary fashion. When a little time before he had asked to be transferred to another position, his own soldiers persuaded him into staying where he was.
In a word the Colonel was an exceptional man. Without question there was no other officer in the Corps as fit as he to undertake the task of mollifying an excited mob.

===Chinese===

将军陷入了极大的困境：他这一段前线眼看要整个敞给敌人。他打电话到预备营房，问委员会主席那边出了什么事。

忽然，将军脸白了，听筒从手里滑落，说：

「他们要杀我。」

参谋长科斯塔耶夫抄起听筒，声音发抖地问出了什么乱子。我听着那头的回答。

「他们情绪很凶。兵变了，聁言要围攻将军。火气在漫延，有些人已经朝司令部出发了。」

电话那头主席的声音，惊惶听得明明白白。问到将军能做什么平息暴民，他说委员会敬重将军，委员们正尽力压下这股火，只怕压不住。

几分钟后，几名军官和士兵冲进屋来，惊惶失色。

「将军，不赶紧走，您就完了！」其中一个说。

随后别洛诺戈夫上校冲了进来——一个心地纯金的人，革命前就受士兵爱戴。他带来的是同一消息，求将军躲一躲。我也加入恳求，求司令避到风头过去。他不肯。

「我为什么要躲？」他喊。「我做错了什么？叫他们来杀我好了！我只是尽了本分。」

他进了书房，把门锁上了。

暴民越逼越近。在场人人脸上都是死灰。每隔一两分钟，就有人眼含惊怖地撞进来，报着那场逼近的风暴。

喧嚣的人潮到了屋前。哭喊嚎叫响成一片。有一秒我们全都悬着心。随后别洛诺戈夫上校说，他出去同他们讲讲，试试能不能讲醒他们。上校嗓音温厚，心也温厚，连对自家的勤务兵也从不摆长官的谱。不久前他请调别的职位，还是他自己的士兵劝他留下来的。

一句话，上校是个例外的人。毫无疑问，全军团再找不出第二个像他那样适合去安抚一群暴怒的人的军官。

===Original===

He went out on the porch and calmly faced the steadily increasing multitude.
“Where is the General? Where is he? We want to kill him!” the savage chorus bawled.
“What are you thinking of?” the Colonel began. “Come to your senses and consider the order. It was an order to relieve your own comrades, soldiers like yourselves. Now, you know that this was no more than fair. The General simply wanted you to take the places of your comrades.”
“But he threatened to shoot us!” interrupted the men.
“You did not quite understand. He only said generally that to get obedience one must shoot.﻿ ﻿…”
“Shoot!” a hundred voices went up from every side, catching the word but not the meaning.
“Shoot! Aha, he wants to shoot! He’s for the old regime himself!” a thousand voices roared, without even giving the ashen-faced Colonel a chance to explain.
“Kill him! Show him what shooting is!” raged the vast throng, while the speaker tried vainly to raise his voice and get a hearing.
Suddenly someone jerked the stool from under his feet. In an instant a hundred heavy heels had trampled the life out of that noble body. It was a horrible, terrifying scene. Several thousand men had turned into beasts. The lust of blood was in their eyes. They swayed backwards and forwards as if intoxicated, crushing the last signs of life out of their victim, stamping on the corpse in a frenzy. The mob’s thirst for blood became inflamed. The officers realized that every moment was precious. Kostayev thought that the only way to save ourselves was to escape through the rear of the house.
“I will go out to them,” I declared.
The remaining officers thought me mad and tried to dissuade me.
“Belonogov was the idol of his regiment, and see what’s become of him. If you go it is certain death,” they said. Colonel Kostayev disappeared and several of the Staff followed him.
I could not see how the situation would be saved by escaping. It might save a couple of lives, although even that was unlikely, but the mutiny would extend and might grow beyond control. “I will go out,” I resolved, crossed myself and dashed into the infuriated mob.
“What is the matter?” I shouted at the top of my voice. “What has happened to you? Let me pass!”
The crowd separated and made a way for me to the stool.
“Look at her!” jeered some voices.
“Eh, eh, look at this bird!” echoed others.
“Your Excellency!” scoffed one man.

===Chinese===

他走到门廊上，静静面对那越聚越多的人群。

「将军呢？他在哪儿？我们要杀他！」兽群合唱似的咆哮。

「你们在想什么？」上校开腔。「醒一醒，想想那道命令。那是一道换防的命令——换你们自己的同志、同你们一样的士兵的防。你们自己知道，这再公道不过。将军不过是要你们接一接同志的位置。」

「可他要枪毙我们！」人群打断他。

「你们没听全。他只是笼统说了一句：要人服从，总得枪毙几个。……」

「枪毙！」四面八方上百个声音接住了这个词，没接住那层意思。

「枪毙！啊哈，他要枪毙人！他自己就是旧派！」千百个声音咆哮，那脸色灰白的上校连解释的空也得不到。

「杀了他！叫他尝尝枪毙的滋味！」大群人狂怒，上校想提嗓门讨个听话，全是白费。

忽然有人从他脚下抽走了小凳。一眨眼，上百只沉重的靴跟，把那高贵的躯体踩没了气。那是一场可怖的、嚇人的场面。几千个人变成了兽。嗜血的欲在他们眼里。他们前仰后合，像喝醉了，把牺牲最后一点生命踩熄，疯了一样在尸首上跺。暴民的血渴越烧越旺。军官们明白每一刻都金贵。科斯塔耶夫想，自救唯一的路是从屋子后头逃。

「我出去，」我宣告。

剩下的军官当我疯了，竭力劝阻。

「别洛诺戈夫是他全团的偶像，落得个什么下场？你去就是送死，」他们说。科斯塔耶夫上校不见了，几名参谋也跟了去。

我看不出逃能救出什么局面。也许能逃出一两条命——连这也未必——而兵变会漫延，闹到收不住。「我要出去，」我定了，画了个十字，一头扎进狂怒的兽群。

「怎么了？」我扯开嗓子喊。「你们出了什么事？让我过去！」

人群分开，给我让出一条通向小凳的路。

「瞧她！」有人讥。

「嚯，嚯，瞧这鸟！」另一些应和。

「大人阁下！」一个人啮笑。

===Original===

“Now,” I began sharply, as soon as I had jumped on the stool. “I am no ‘your Excellency!’ but plain Yashka! You can kill me right away, or you can kill me a little later, five, ten minutes later. But Yashka will not be afraid.
“I will have my say. Before you slay me I must speak my mind. Do you know me? Do you know that I am one of you, a plain peasant soldier?”
“Yes, we do,” the men answered.
“Well,” I resumed, “why did you kill this man?” and I pointed at the disfigured body at my feet. “He was the kindest officer in the Corps. He never beat, never punished a soldier. He was always courteous, to privates and officers alike. He never spoke contemptuously to anyone. Only a month ago he wanted to be transferred and you insisted on keeping him. That was four weeks ago. Had he changed, could he have changed, in such a short time?
“He was like a father to his men. Weren’t you always proud of him? Didn’t you always boast that in his regiment the food was good, the soldiers were well shod, the baths were regular? Didn’t you, of your own accord, reward him with a Soldiers’ Cross, the highest honour that the free Russian army has to offer?
“And now you have killed, with your own hands, this noble soul, this rare example of human kindness. Why?
“Why did you do it?” I turned fiercely on the men.
“Because he was of the exploiting class,” came one answer.
“They all suck our blood!” shouted some others.
“Why let her talk? Who is she that she should question us?” somebody cried out.
“Kill her! Kill her, too! Kill them all! We have shed enough of our blood! The bourgeois! The murderers! Kill her!” was shouted from many throats.
“Scoundrels!” I screamed. “You will kill me yet, I am at your mercy, and I came out to be killed. You ask why I should be allowed to talk. You ask who I am. As if you didn’t know me! Who is Yashka Bochkareva?
“Who sent delegates to present icons to me, if not you? Who had me promoted to the rank of an officer, if not you? Who sent me this testimonial to Petrograd only a couple of weeks ago, if not you?”
Here I drew out from my breast pocket the resolution passed and signed by the Corps Committee and despatched to me while I was in the Petrograd Hospital. I had brought it with me. Pointing to the signatures, I cried:
“You see this? Who signed it, if not you yourselves?

===Chinese===

「听着，」我一跳上小凳便厉声开腔。「我不是什么『大人』！我就是雅什卡！你们可以马上杀我，也可以晚一点杀，五分钟、十分钟以后。雅什卡不会怕。

「我有话要讲。杀我之前，我得把心里话讲完。你们认得我么？知道我是你们中的一个、一个普通庄稼人士兵么？」

「知道，」人们答。

「那好，」我接着说，「你们为什么杀了这个人？」我指着脚边那具不成形的尸首。「他是全军团最仁厚的军官。他从不打士兵，从不罚士兵。他对列兵同对军官一样客气，从没对谁说过一句轻慢话。就在一个月前，他还想调走，是你们执意留住了他。那是四个星期前的事。四个星期，他变了么，可能变了么？

「他待士兵像父亲。你们不是一向以他为荣么？不是一向夸他团里伙食好、士兵鞋脚齐整、澡按时洗么？不是你们自己主动授他士兵十字勋章——自由的俄国军队所能给出的最高荣誉么？

「可如今，你们亲手杀了这颗高贵的灵魂，这个世间仁厚的罕有的榜样。为什么？

「为什么？」我猛地转向人群。

「因为他是剥削阶级，」一个回答。

「他们都吸我们的血！」一些人喊。

「由她讲什么？她算老几，敢来审我们？」有人叫。

「杀了她！也杀了她！全杀了！我们的血流得够了！资产阶级！刽子手！杀了她！」许多条喉咙齐喊。

「恶棍们！」我尖叫。「你们是会杀我的，我在你们手心里，我出来就是来死的。你们问我凭什么讲话、问我是谁。装什么不认得我！雅什卡·博奇卡列娃是谁？

「给我送圣像代表的是谁，不是你们吗？保我升了军官的是谁，不是你们吗？不过两个星期前，把嘉奖状送到彼得格勒我手上的，不是你们又是谁？」

说到这里，我从胸袋里抽出军团委员会通过、签名、在我住彼得格勒医院时寄给我的那份决议。我把它带来了。我指着签名，喊：

「看见没有？签名的不是你们自己，是谁？

===Original===

It is signed by the Corps Committee, your own representatives, whom you, yourselves, elected!”
The men were silent.
“Who suffered, fought with you, if not I? Who saved your lives under fire, if not Yashka? Don’t you remember what I did for your comrades at Naroch, when, up to my armpits in mud, I dragged dozens of you to safety and life?”
Here, I turned abruptly on a gaping fellow, looked directly at him and asked:
“Suppose the rank and file were to elect their own officers. Now, what would you do in the Commander’s place, if you were chosen? You are a plain soldier, of the people. Tell me what you would do!” I thundered.
The man looked foolish, making an effort to laugh.
“Ha, I would see,” he said, “once I got there.”
“That is no answer. Tell me what you would do if our Corps were in the trenches and another one refused to relieve it. What would you do? What?” I demanded of the whole crowd.
“Would you hold the trenches indefinitely or leave? Answer me that!”
“Well, we would leave, anyhow,” replied a number of men.
“But what are you here for,” I shouted fiercely, “to hold the trenches or not?”
“Yes, to hold,” they answered.
“Then how could you leave them?” I fired back.
There was silence.
“That would be treason to Free Russia!” I continued.
The men bowed their heads in shame. Nobody spoke.
“Then why did you kill him?” I cried out bitterly. “What did he want you to do but hold the trenches?”
“He wanted to shoot us!” several sullen voices replied.
“He never said anything of the sort. What he wanted to say was to explain that the General did not threaten you either, but remarked that in other circumstances your action would be punished by shooting. No sooner did Colonel Belonogov mention the word ‘shoot’ than you threw yourself upon him without even giving the man a chance to finish what he was saying.”
“That was not what we understood. We thought he threatened to shoot us,” the men weakly defended themselves.
At this point the orderlies and friends of the murdered Colonel rushed up. They raised such a cry of grief when they saw the mutilated corpse that all speech was silenced. They cursed and wept and threatened the mob, although they were few and the crowd numbered thousands.
“Murderers! Bloodthirsty ruffians! Whom have you killed? Our little father! Did ever soldiers have a better friend than he was?

===Chinese===

签名的是军团委员会——你们自己选出来的代表！」

人们沉默了。

「同你们一起吃苦、一起打仗的是谁，不是我吗？炮火底下救你们性命的是谁，不是雅什卡吗？你们不记得纳罗奇那一仗，我为你们的同志做了什么——泥没到腋窝，我拖出来你们几十个人，拖向安全、拖向活路？」

说到这里，我猛地转向一个張着嘴发呆的家伙，直直盯着他，问：

「假定说，士兵们自选长官。现在换你坐司令的位子，选着了你，你怎么办？你是个普通士兵，人民的人。说说你会怎么办！」我雷一般地问。

那人傻了眼，挤出一个笑。

「哈，等我坐上去，」他说，「再瞧。」

「这不算回答。我问你：我们军团在堑壕里，另一个军团拒绝来换防，你怎么办？你怎么办？」我逼问全群。

「你们是无限期守下去，还是走？回答我！」

「这个……总要走的，」不少人答。

「那你们在这儿是为了什么，」我狠狠地喊，「守堑壕，还是不守？」

「守，」他们答。

「那怎么能走？」我回敬。

一片沉默。

「那是背叛自由的俄罗斯！」我接下去。

人们羞愧地垂下头。没人说话。

「那你们为什么杀了他？」我悲愤地喊。「他要你们做的，除了守堑壕，还有什么？」

「他要枪毙我们！」几条悻悻的声音答。

「他根本没有那样说。他要说的，本是解释：将军也没有聁言你们，只是说了一句，换在别的情形下，你们这样做要吃枪毙。别洛诺戈夫上校一出口那个『毙』字，你们就扑了上去，连人家把话说完的机会都不给。」

「我们不是那样听的。我们当他聁言要毙我们，」人们无力地分辩。

这时，被害上校的勤务兵和朋友们奔了过来。一见那具残破的尸首，他们放声痛哭，一切言语都被压了下去。他们咒骂、流泪、朝暴民声讨，虽然他们只有几个人，而人群数以千计。

「凶手们！嗜血的暴徒！你们杀的是谁？我们的亲父亲！士兵们何曾有过比他更好的朋友？

===Original===

Was there ever a commander who took greater care of his men? You are worse than the Tsar and his hangmen. You are given freedom, and you act like cutthroats. You devils!”
And the mourners broke out in even louder lamentations. The wailing rent the air. It gripped everybody’s throat. Many in the mob wept. As the dead man’s friends began to relate the various favours they had received from him, I could not choke down my tears and stepped down from the stool, convulsed with sobs.
Meanwhile, in response to calls for help, a division from a neighbouring corps arrived to quell the mutiny. The Committee of the Division came forward and demanded the surrender of the ringleaders of the movement that had resulted in the soldiers’ refusal to return to the trenches and in the murder of Colonel Belonogov. There were negotiations between the two committees, which finally ended in the surrender by the mob of twenty agitators, who were placed under arrest.
The officers who had fled and the General now reappeared, although the latter was still afraid to order the soldiers to relieve the corps in the trenches. He asked me to broach the subject.
I first addressed the men about the funeral.
“We must have a coffin made. Who will do it?” I asked.
Several volunteered to get some timber and make one.
“How about a grave? We must bury him with full military honours,” I went on. Some soldiers offered their services as gravediggers.
An officer went to look for a priest. I sent a soldier to the woods to make a wreath. Then I turned and asked:
“Now, will you go to the trenches to relieve your comrades?”
“Yes,” the men answered meekly.
It was an unforgettable scene. These five thousand men, all so docile and humble, some with tears still fresh on their cheeks, were like a forlorn flock of sheep that had lost its shepherd. It seemed impossible to believe that these men were capable of murder. You could curse them now, you could even strike them, and they would bear it without protest. They were conscious, deeply conscious of a great crime. Quietly they stood, from time to time, uttering a word of regret, engrossed in mourning. And yet these same lambs were ferocious beasts two hours ago. All the gentleness now mirrored in their faces was then extinguished by a hurricane of savage passion. These obedient children had actually been inhuman a short time ago.

===Chinese===

何曾有过更疼士兵的长官？你们比沙皇和他的刽子手还坏。给了你们自由，你们却干出了刀客的勾当。你们这些魔鬼！」

哭的人愈发嚎啕。哀声撕着空气，攫住每个人的喉咙。暴民里许多人哭了。死者的人们开始细数从他手里得过的一桩桩恩惠，我再也忍不住泪，从小凳上下来，抽泣得浑身发抖。

这其间，应求援的呼唤，邻近一个军团的一个师开到，来平这场兵变。师的委员会出面，要求交出这场运动的头目——正他们，才有士兵拒回堑壕，才有别洛诺戈夫上校的被杀。两个委员会谈判，末了暴民交出了二十名鼓动者，被看押起来。

逃走的军官们同将军重新露面，只是将军仍不敢下令换防。他把这桩事托给了我。

我先同士兵们讲丧事。

「得打一口棺材。谁来做？」我问。

几个自愿去弄木料、去打。

「墓穴呢？要按全副军礼安葬他，」我接着说。几个士兵应承挖墓。

一名军官去找神父。我派一个士兵去林子里扎花圈。随后我转身问：

「现在，你们去堑壕换同志们的防么？」

「去，」人们顺从地答。

那是一个忘不掉的场面。这五千个人，个个这样驯顺、谦卑，有的脸颊上还挂着新鲜的泪，像一群失去了牧人的凄惨的羊。说这些人杀得了人，简直没法信。此刻你可以骂他们，甚至打他们，他们受着，一声不吭。他们明白，深深地明白自己犯下了一桩大罪。他们静静地站着，不时吐一句悔恨的话，沉浸在哀悼里。可正是这些羔羊，两个钟头前还是凶残的野兽。此刻他们脸上映出的全部温顺，那时被一阵野性的狂飙扑灭了。这些听话的孩子，方才真的没了人性。

===Original===

It was incredible, and still it was the truth.
Such is the character of the Russian people.
The coffin, an oblong box of unshaven boards, draped inside and out with a white sheet, was brought at four o’clock. The body had been washed, but it was impossible to restore the face to its normal appearance. It was disfigured beyond recognition. With the help of some of the men, I wrapped the body in canvas and placed it in the coffin. Instead of one there were four green wreaths made. The priest began to read the service but could not control himself and burst into sobs. The General, the Staff, and I, with candles in our hands, were sobbing too. Immediately behind the coffin, as the procession started, the dead officer’s orderly wailed in heartrending tones, recalling aloud the virtues of his master. Behind us marched almost the whole Corps, including the Regiment commanded by the dead man. The weeping was so general and so increased with every step that by the time the procession reached the grave the wailing could be heard for miles around. As the body was laid to rest everybody dropped a handful of sand into the grave. The lips of all were moving in prayer.
The order was given that by seven o’clock the Corps should be moved to relieve the soldiers at the fighting line. I went to my girls and gave the word for them to be ready too. They had heard of the disturbance and had passed some anxious moments, and therefore they gave me a hearty welcome. The General had telephoned to the front line that the Corps was a few hours late and asked the soldiers there to remain in the trenches for the night. The distance that we had to cover was about ten miles, and we arrived at the front before dawn.
The Battalion, now consisting of only some two hundred women, occupied a small sector to itself, opposite the town of Kreva. There was no sign of actual warfare at the fighting line. Neither the Germans nor the Russians used their arms. Fraternization was general. There was a virtual, if not formal, truce. The men met every day, indulged in long arguments and drank beer brought by the Germans.
I could not tolerate such war and ordered my women to conduct themselves as if everything were as usual. The men became very irritated by our militant attitude toward the enemy. A group of them, with the Chairman of the Regimental Committee, came over to our trench to discuss the matter.

===Chinese===

这叫人不能信，却又是真的。

俄罗斯人民的性子，就是这样。

棺材——一口未刨平的木板钉成的长方箱，里外蒙着白床单——四点钟抬来了。遗体洗过了，脸却再无法复原：毁得认不出了。在几个士兵帮衬下，我用帆布裹好遗体，安放进棺材。说好扎一个的花圈，扎成了四个。神父开始诵经，念不下去，放声哭了。将军、参谋们和我，手里擎着蜡烛，也都在哭。起灵时，死者的勤务兵紧跟棺后，声声泣诉，大声数着他主人的德行。我们后面走着几乎整个军团，包括死者统率过的那个团。哭声那样普遍，一步比一步重，送葬的队伍到墓前时，嚎哭声几英里外都听得见。遗体安放下去，人人抓一把沙撒进墓穴，人人嘴唇翕动着祷告。

命令下来了：七点前，全军团开拔，换火线上士兵的防。我回到姑娘们那里，吩咐她们也作好准备。这场风波她们已有所闻，焦灼了一阵，见了我热烈欢迎。将军已打电话通知前线：军团晚到几个钟头，请那里的士兵在堑壕再守一夜。我们要走的路约十英里，天亮前到了前线。

全营——如今只剩约两百名女兵——独自占了对着克列瓦镇的一小段阵地。火线上没有一点真打仗的样子。德国人不打，俄国人也不打。联欢成了通例，是一场虽未成文的休战。双方的人天天见面，长篇大论地争辩，喝着德国人送来的啤酒。

这样的仗我看不下去，命令我的女兵们一切照常行事——照打真仗的样子。男人们被我们对敌人的战斗姿态惹恼了。一批人跟着团委员会主席到我们堑壕来理论。

===Original===

“Who are our enemies?” began the Chairman. “Surely, not the Germans who want peace. It’s the bourgeoisie, the ruling class, that is the real enemy of the people. It’s against them that we ought to wage war, for they would not listen to the German peace proposals. Why does not Kerensky obtain peace for us? Because the Allies will not let him. Well, we will very soon drive Kerensky out of his office!”
“But I am not of the ruling class. I am a plain peasant woman,” I objected. “I have been a soldier since the beginning of the war and have fought in many battles. Don’t agitate here against officers.”
“Oh, I don’t mean you,” he replied; trying to win me over to the pacificist idea. Several German soldiers joined the Russian group. The discussion became heated. They repeated the old argument that the Germans had asked for peace and that the Allies had not accepted it. I replied that the Germans could have peace with Russia if they withdrew from the invaded parts of our country. So long as they kept our land, it was the duty of every Russian to fight and drive them out.
Thus life dragged on. Nights and days passed in discussions. Kerensky had almost entirely lost his hold on the men, who were drifting more and more toward Bolshevism. Finally, the feud between Kerensky and Kornilov reached a crisis. Kerensky asked the Commander-in-Chief by telephone to send some loyal troops to Petrograd, apparently realizing that his days were numbered. Kornilov replied with a message through Alexeiev, requesting a written certificate from Kerensky, investing the Commander-in-Chief with full authority to restore discipline in the army. It would seem that Kornilov was willing to save Kerensky, provided the latter allowed him to save the front.
But Kerensky evidently saw in this an opportunity of restoring his fallen prestige and securing his position. He therefore turned against Kornilov, publicly declaring that the latter was aiming at supreme power and he appealed to the workmen and soldiers to rise against the Commander of the army. The result was the brief encounter between the revolutionary masses and Kornilov’s Savage Division. Kornilov was defeated. Kerensky triumphed, and for the moment it looked as if he had attained his object.

===Chinese===

「谁是我们的敌人？」主席开腔。「当然不是要和平的德国人。资产阶级、统治阶级，才是人民真正的敌人。该打的是他们，因为德国人的和平提议，他们不肯听。克伦斯基为什么不给我们弄来和平？因为协约国不让他。哼，我们很快就把克伦斯基赶下台！」

「可我不属于统治阶级。我是个普通农妇，」我反驳。「开战头一天我就是兵，打过多少仗。别在这儿挑唆人反对军官。」

「哦，我不是说你，」他答，想把我拉到和平主义那边去。几个德国兵凑进俄国人群。辩论热起来。他们又搬出那套老话：德国人求过和平，协约国不接受。我答：德国人肯从他们侵占的我国土地上撤走，同俄国自然有和平可讲；他们占着我们的土地一天，每一个俄国人就有打他们、赶他们的本分。

日子就这样拖着。日日夜夜都在辩论里过。克伦斯基在士兵中间几乎完全失了把力，人心一日日漂向布尔什维主义。末了，克伦斯基同科尔尼洛夫的怨仇到了爆发的关头。克伦斯基打电话给总司令，要他调几支可靠的部队到彼得格勒——他显然知道自己的日子屈指可数了。科尔尼洛夫托阿列克谢耶夫回话：要克伦斯基出一纸书面凭证，授总司令恢复军纪的全权。看那意思，只要克伦斯基容他救前线，科尔尼洛夫是愿意救克伦斯基的。

可克伦斯基显然把这看作一个机会：正好重振他跌落的威望、坐稳他的位子。于是他倒向科尔尼洛夫的对头，公开宣布科尔尼洛夫图谋最高权力，号召工人和士兵起来反对军队的总司令。结果便是革命群众同科尔尼洛夫的野蛮师那场短短的相遇。科尔尼洛夫败了。克伦斯基得胜，一时间，他好像真的达到了目的。

===Original===

All the radical forces were united and Kerensky, as the saviour of the revolution from an attempt at a counterrevolution, again became the idol of the soldiers and the working class.
The larger part of the army sided with Kerensky when he appealed for support against Kornilov. But this did not last long. Kerensky little by little lost the confidence of the masses which he had suddenly acquired, because he did not bring them the much desired peace.
Those of the soldiers and officers who sided with Kornilov were nicknamed *Kornilovetz*. To call a man by this name was equivalent to calling him a counterrevolutionary, an advocate of the old regime, or an enemy of the people.
The inactivity of life in the trenches became wearisome. One rainy day I sent out a listening party into No Man’s Land, with instructions to shoot at the enemy in case of his approach. I watched the party go forward. Suddenly, a group of Germans, numbering about ten, came in the direction of our trenches. They walked along at their ease with their hands in their pockets, some whistling, others singing. I aimed my rifle at the leg of one of the troop and wounded him.
The whole front was in an uproar in a second. It was scandalous! Who dared do such a thing! The Germans and the Russians were seething with rage. Several of my women came running up to me greatly alarmed.
“Commander, why did you do that?” they asked, seeing me with a smoking rifle in hand.
A number of soldiers who were friends of mine next hastened into our trench to warn me of the men’s ugly temper and threats. I told them that I saw the Germans approach my girls and make an effort at flirtation. But this defence did not appease the soldiers. They placed machine guns in the first trench and were preparing to slaughter us all. Fortunately, we were informed in time and were hidden in a side trench. The machine guns raked our position, without causing any casualties. The firing was finally interrupted by the sharp orders of the Chairman of the Regimental Committee. I was called before him to give an explanation. I bade farewell to my girls, telling them that there would probably be a repetition of the episode of Colonel Belonogov’s lynching.
I was received by the men with threats and ugly words.
“Kill her!”
“She’s a Kornilovka!”
“Make an end of her!”
I was surrounded by the members of the committee, who kept back the mob.

===Chinese===

一切激进势力拧成了一股；克伦斯基作为从反革命企图中救下革命的人，重又成了士兵和工人阶级的偶像。

克伦斯基求援反科尔尼洛夫时，军队的大头站到了他一边。可这没有维持多久。他骤然到手的人心，又一点点失掉了，因为他没有带来众人切盼的和平。

站在科尔尼洛夫一边的士兵和军官，得了个绰号：*科尔尼洛夫派*（Kornilovetz）。叫一个人这个名字，等于叫他反革命、旧制度的鼓吹者、人民的敌人。

堑壕里无所事事的日子，熬得人烦。一个雨天，我派出一支听音队进无人地带，嘱咐：敌人一靠近就开枪。我望着队伍上前。忽然，一小群德国人——约莫十个——朝我们堑壕的方向来了。他们信步走着，手插在口袋里，有的吹口哨，有的唱歌。我举枪瞄准其中一个的腿，打伤了他。

一瞬间，整条前线炸了窝。骇人听闻！谁敢干这种事！德国人俄国人一齐冒火。我的几个女兵惊慌地奔到我面前。

「统帅，您为什么打？」她们问——见我手里还攥着冒烟的枪。

随后几个同我要好的士兵也急忙跑进我们堑壕，警告我人心凶险、聁言要报复。我说：我见德国人朝我的姑娘们凑过来、动手动脚。这番辩解没有平下火去。他们在第一道堑壕架起机枪，准备把我们全都扫倒。幸好消息来得及时，我们躲进一条支壕。机枪扫过我们的阵地，没有造成伤亡。射击最后叫团委员会主席一声厉喝止住。我被叫到他面前，要一个解释。我同姑娘们道别，告诉她们：别洛诺戈夫上校私刑的那一出，多半要重演。

迎接我的是威胁和脏话。

「杀了她！」

「她是科尔尼洛夫婆娘！」

「了结了她！」

委员会的委员们围住我，把暴民拦在后头。

===Original===

Several speakers rose in my defence, but hardly succeeded in appeasing the crowd. Then an officer got up to talk in my behalf. He was a popular speaker. But this time his popularity did not avail him. He said that I was right. He would have done the same thing had he been in my place. That was as far as he got.
“Aha, so you are a Kornilovetz too!” shouted the crowd. “Kill him! Kill him!”
In an instant the man was thrown off the chair and struck on the head. In another instant he was crushed to death under a thousand heels.
Then the mob swayed in my direction. But the committee seized me and carried me off to the rear, hiding me in a dugout. One of my girls, Medvedovskaya, was placed at the entrance to guard it.
Meanwhile, my girls heard what had happened and hurried to my aid. The mob dispersed to look for me and some of the men came to the dugout in which I was concealed.
“Where is Bochkareva? Let us in to see if she is there!” they shouted. The girl sentry said she had orders to shoot if they approached near her. They did. She-fired, wounding one in the side.
The poor girl was bayoneted by the brutes.
The committee and my friends, numbering about one hundred, insisted that I should be given a trial and not lynched. My girls were ready to die for me to the last one. I was taken out from the dugout by my defenders, who made an effort to lead me to safety for an open trial.
The mob, which had now increased, pressed closer and closer. The two sides were fighting for me. It was agreed that no weapons were to be used in the scramble. The mass of humanity swayed back and forth, my girls fighting with the strength of infuriated wild beasts to stave off the mob. Now and then a man would get close enough to strike a blow at me. As the struggle developed these blows increased in number till I was knocked senseless. In that state my friends dragged me away from the scene of the struggle.
My life was saved, although I was badly knocked about. It cost the lives of a loyal girl and an innocent friend. I was sent to Molodechno, a couple of my girls going with me to look after me. The Battalion was taken from the front to the reserve billets. But even there their lives were not safe. They were insulted, annoyed, and dubbed Kornilovki. There were daily tumults. The windows of their dugouts were broken. The officers were powerless and seldom showed their faces.

===Chinese===

几个人起身为我辩，几乎压不住人群。随后一名军官起立替我说话。他是位受欢迎的演说家，可这一回，他的名望救不了他。他说我在理，换了他处在我的位子，他也会这样做。话说到这里就断了。

「啊哈，原来你也是科尔尼洛夫派！」人群喊。「杀了他！杀了他！」

一眨眼，那人被从椅子上掼下来，头上挨了打；再一眨眼，他在上千只靴底下被踩死了。

随后暴民朝我这边涌来。委员会一把抢下我，把我抢到后方，藏进一个掩蔽部，我的一个女兵梅德韦多夫斯卡娅（Medvedovskaya）守在洞口。

这其间，我的姑娘们得了信，赶来救我。暴民散开四处找我，一些人到了我藏身的掩蔽部前。

「博奇卡列娃在哪里？放我们进去搜！」他们喊。女哨兵说：谁走近她就奉令开枪。他们还是逼近，她开了枪，打伤一个人的肋。

可怜的姑娘，被那些畜生捅了刺刀。

委员会同我的朋友们——约有百人——坚执要给我正式审判，不许私刑。我的姑娘们人人准备为我死。护着我的人把我从掩蔽部里带出来，竭力要把我送到安全地方受公开审判。

暴民这时更众了，越逼越紧。两边为我相争。讲定了这场争夺不许动兵器。人潮前仰后合，我的姑娘们像发狂的野兽那样拼力气，顶住暴民。不时有人挤得近了，朝我击来一拳。争夺越久，拳脚越多，直到把我打得没了知觉。就在那副样子里，朋友们把我从争斗场里拖了出去。

命保住了，虽然我被打得够呛。代价是一条忠心的姑娘和一个无辜的朋友的命。我被送往莫洛杰奇诺，两个姑娘随行照看我。全营从前线撤到预备营房。可在那里她们的性命也不安稳：受辱骂，受搅扰，被叫作科尔尼洛夫婆娘。天天有骚动，掩蔽部的窗子叫人砸了。军官们无能为力，轻易不敢露面。

===Original===

My instructors did their best to defend me and the Battalion, explaining that we were non-party.
One morning a car came for me from Headquarters at Molodechno. There I met the Commanding General of my Corps, who described the unbearable conditions in which my girls were placed. They were waiting for me, refusing to go home, unless I disbanded them. He had sent them to dig reserve trenches in order to keep them away from the men. They did splendid work, he said, but as soon as they returned the men began to molest them. Only the previous night a gang of soldiers made an assault on the dugouts in which my girls were billeted. They beat the sentry and broke in with the intention of attacking the women. There was a panic. Some of the girls seized their rifles and fired in the air. The noise attracted the attention of my instructors and several other soldiers, among whom there were numerous decent men. The situation was saved by the latter.
But what was to be done? Life for the Battalion was becoming absolutely unbearable, at least at this part of the front. It was difficult to understand the change which had come over the men in a few months. How long ago was it that they almost worshipped me, and I loved them? Now they seemed to have lost their senses.
The General advised me to disband the Battalion. But that would be to admit failure and despair as to my country’s condition. I was not ready to make such admissions. No, I would not disband my unit. I would fight to the end. The General could not understand my point of view. Was not the case hopeless since the soldiers had turned machine guns on the Battalion? Wouldn’t I have been lynched but for the desperate struggle of my girls and the soldiers who were my friends? So I resolved to go to Petrograd and ask Kerensky to transfer me to a fighting sector.
I went to see my girls before leaving for the capital. It was a pathetic meeting. They were glad to learn of my intended journey. They could not stand it much longer where they were. They were prepared to fight the Germans, to be tortured by them, to die at their hands or in prison camps. But they were not prepared for the torments and humiliation that they were made to suffer by our own men. That had never entered into our calculations at the time the Battalion was formed.

===Chinese===

我的教官们竭力护着我和全营，解释我们是超党派的。

一天早晨，莫洛杰奇诺司令部来了汽车接我。在那里，我见到了军团司令，他讲了我的姑娘们所处的难以忍受的境况。她们等着我，说我不解散她们，她们决不回家。他派她们去挖预备堑壕，好避开那些男人。她们干得极漂亮，他说；可一回来，男人们又开始搅扰她们。就在前一晚，一伙士兵袭击了姑娘们宿的掩蔽部：打了哨兵，破门而入，存心行凶。一时大乱。几个姑娘抓起步枪朝天上放枪。枪声惊动了我的教官们和另一些士兵——那里面正派人也还多。局面是后者救下的。

可怎么办呢？全营的日子，至少在前线这一段，变得完全过不下去了。男人们几个月里起的这个变化，叫人难以明白。不久之前，他们还差不多把我当神敬，我也爱他们；如今他们像失了魂。

将军劝我解散全营。可那就等于认输，等于对国家的景况绝望。我不肯认这些。不，我不解散我的队伍，我要斗到底。将军不懂我的心。士兵们把机枪都对准全营了，这案子还不算没救么？若不是我姑娘们和我那些朋友拼死相争，我不早被私刑了？于是我决意去彼得格勒，求克伦斯基把我调到一段真打仗的正面去。

动身去都城前，我去看我的姑娘们。那是一场心酸的会面。她们听说我此行，都高兴：她们在原地再也撑不下去了。同德国人打，受德国人的刑，死在他们手里、死在战俘营里，她们都有准备；可是自家的男人加给她们的折磨和羞辱，她们没有准备。组建全营的时候，谁也没把这算进去。

===Original===

I took my documents with me and left the same evening, telling my soldiers that I would not stay away longer than a week, which was the limit that they set on their endurance. Upon my arrival in Petrograd I went to the quarters occupied by the Battalion while in training. It was evident at a glance that an atmosphere of depression weighed heavily on the Russian capital. The smiles and rejoicings were gone from the streets. There was gloom in the air and in everybody’s eyes. Food was very scarce. Red Guards were plentiful. Bolshevism walked the streets openly and defiantly, as if its day had already come.
My friends, who had taken an interest in the Battalion, were horrified to learn of conditions at the front. Their accounts of the state of affairs at the capital depressed me greatly. Kerensky, after his dispute with Kornilov, had cut himself off completely from his friends and acquaintances of the upper classes. I went to General Anosov, telling him of my mission. But he would not accompany me anywhere, although he placed his motorcar at my disposal. I drove to the Commander of the Military District, General Vasilkovsky, a Cossack, who looked impressive and strong, but was actually a weakling. He received me cordially and asked the purpose of my visit to the city. He had heard of the rough handling I had endured and expressed his sympathy.
“But,” he added, “no one is safe in these days. I, myself, expect to be thrown out at any time. It is a matter of days, of hours, for the Government. Another revolution is ripening and is close upon us. Bolshevism is everywhere, in the factories and in the barracks. And how are things at the front?”
“The same or even worse,” I answered, and I told him of all my trials and troubles, and the help I expected to obtain from him and the War Minister.
“Nothing can help you now,” he said. “The authorities are powerless. Orders are not worth the paper on which they are issued. I am going now to Verkhovsky, the new War Minister. Would you like to come with me?”
On the way we discussed Verkhovsky’s appointment. He was the same man who, as Commander of the Moscow Military District, had rescued me from the mob at Moscow some weeks before. He was a very popular leader and had considerable influence with the soldiers.
“Perhaps if he had been appointed some months ago he might have saved the army.

===Chinese===

我带上文书，当晚动身，告诉姑娘们最多一星期就回——那是她们耐性的极限。到了彼得格勒，我先去全营训练时住过的营房。一眼便看得出：一片消沉的大气沉沉压着俄国都城。街上的笑脸和欢腾没有了，空气里、人人的眼里都是阴霾。吃食极缺，赤卫队极多。布尔什维主义在街上大摇大摆、旁若无人地走，仿佛它的日子已经到了。

那些关心过全营的朋友们，听了前线的情形惊骇不已；他们讲的都城局面，也叫我大大消沉。克伦斯基同科尔尼洛夫闹翻之后，把上流社会里的旧交整个斩断了。我去见阿诺索夫将军，说明来意。他哪里也不肯陪我去，只把他的汽车借给我用。我去见军区司令瓦西里科夫斯基将军——一个哥萨克，看着气派、结实，其实是个软蛋。他热络地接待我，问我进城有何贵干。我受的那顿苦他听说了，表示同情。

「不过，」他添一句，「如今谁也不安全。我自己就等着随时被人撵走。对政府来说，论天、论钟点了。又一场革命正在成熟，已经逼到跟前。布尔什维主义无处不在——在工厂里，在营房里。前线怎么样？」

「一样，或者更糟，」我答；把我的种种遭难、指望从他同陆军部长那里得到的帮助，都对他讲了。

「如今谁也帮不了你，」他说。「当局毫无办法。命令不值印发它的那张纸。我这就要去见新任陆军部长韦尔霍夫斯基。愿意同我一道去么？」

路上我们议论起韦尔霍夫斯基的任命。他正是几星期前以莫斯科军区司令身份、在莫斯科从暴民手里救下我的那个人，很得军心，在士兵中颇有影响。

「倘若几个月前就任用他，也许还救得了军队。可如今太迟了，」瓦西里科夫斯基说。

===Original===

But it is too late now,” said Vasilkovsky.
When we arrived at the War Ministry, we found that Kerensky was in Verkhovsky’s study. We were announced, and I was asked to come in first. As I opened the door I saw immediately that all was lost. The Prime Minister and the War Minister were both standing. They presented a pathetic, heartbreaking sight. Kerensky looked like a corpse. There was not a vestige of colour in his face. His eyes were red as if he had not slept for nights. Verkhovsky seemed to me like a man who is drowning, reaching for help. My heart sank. War had made me callous, and I was seldom shocked. But this time I was nearly overcome by the sight of these two agonized figures. I saw the agony of Russia reflected in their despairing faces.
They made an effort to smile, but it was a failure. The War Minister then inquired how things were at the front. “We heard you were roughly treated,” he said.
I gave a detailed account of everything that I had myself witnessed and experienced. I told them in detail about the lynching of Colonel Belonogov, of the officer who tried to defend me, of the bayoneting of my girl, of the machine guns that were turned on me because I wounded one of the enemy.
Kerensky seized his head in his hands and cried out:
“Oh, horror! horror! We are perishing! We are drowning!”
There was a tense, painful pause.
I ended my story with the suggestion that action was urgently needed or all would be wrecked.
“Yes, action is needed, but what action? What is to be done now? What would you do if you were to be given authority over the army? You are a common soldier, tell me what you would do?”
“It is too late now,” I answered after thinking a little time. “Two months ago I could have accomplished a great deal. Then they still respected me. Now they hate me.”
“Ah!” exclaimed the War Minister. “Two months ago I might have saved the situation myself, if I had only been here then!”
We then discussed the purpose of my journey. I asked for a transfer to a more active part of the front and for a certificate that the Battalion was to be run without committees. This certificate I obtained from the War Minister without delay, and I still have it in my possession. He also agreed to my first request and promised to look into the matter and issue orders for my transfer.
Kerensky was silent during the conversation.

===Chinese===

我们到了陆军部，得知克伦斯基正在韦尔霍夫斯基的书房里。通名之后，请我先进去。一开门我就明白：一切都完了。总理同陆军部长都站着，那是一副可怜的、叫人心碎的样子。克伦斯基像一具尸首，脸上没有一丝血色，眼睛红得像熬了多少夜。韦尔霍夫斯基在我眼里像一个正在没顶、伸手求援的人。我的心沉了下去。战争把我磨硬了，我很少震惊；可这一回，这两个人痛苦的身影险些把我压倒。我在他们绝望的脸上，看见了俄国的痛苦。

他们勉强做出笑，不成。陆军部长随后问前线的情形。「听说你受了亏待，」他说。

我把亲见亲历的一切，原原本本讲了一遍：别洛诺戈夫上校怎样被私刑，替我辩护的那位军官怎样惨死，我的姑娘怎样挨刺刀，我打伤一个敌人之后机枪怎样对准了我。

克伦斯基双手抱头，喊：

「啊，可怕！可怕！我们在灭亡！我们在沉没！」

一阵紧绷的、痛苦的沉默。

我讲完时说：必须刻不容缓地行动，不然一切都要砸。

「是，要行动，可怎样行动？如今还有什么可做？倘若把军队交给你，你会怎样做？你是个普通士兵，说说你会怎样做？」

「如今太迟了，」我想了片刻，答。「两个月前，我本可做成许多事。那时他们还敬我。如今他们恨我。」

「啊！」陆军部长叹。「两个月前，倘我在这儿，我自己兴许还救得了局面！」

随后我们谈我此行的目的。我请求调往正面更活跃的一段，并求一纸凭证：全营不设委员会。凭证我当场就从陆军部长手里拿到了，至今还保存着。第一个请求他也应允，答应过问此事，下令给我调动。

谈话间，克伦斯基始终沉默。

===Original===

He stood like a ghost, the symbol of once mighty Russia. Four months before he was the idol of the nation. Now almost all had turned against him. As I looked at him I felt I was in the presence of that immense tragedy which was rending my country into fragments. Something seemed to clutch my throat and shake me. I wanted to cry, to sob. My heart dripped blood for Mother Russia. What would I not have done to avert that impending catastrophe? How many deaths would I not have died at that moment?
Here was my country drifting towards an abyss. I could see it sliding down, down, down.﻿ ﻿… And here were the heads of the Government powerless, helpless, clinging hopelessly to the doomed ship, despairing of salvation, abandoned, forlorn, stricken.﻿ ﻿…
“God only knows the future﻿—shall we ever meet again?” I asked the two men in a stifled voice, as I bade them farewell.
Kerensky, livid, motionless, answered in a hoarse whisper:
“Hardly.”

===Chinese===

他像一具幽魂立在那里——那个曾经强大的俄国的象征。四个月前，他还是全国的偶像；如今几乎人人都倒向了他的对面。望着他，我觉得自己正站在那场把我祖国撕成碎片的大悲剧跟前。有什么攫住我的喉咙摇撼我。我想哭，想啜泣。我的心为俄罗斯母亲滴血。为拦住那场将临的大祸，什么我不肯做？那一刻，多少个死我死不得？

我的国家正漂向深渊。我看得见它在滑下去，滑下去，滑下去。……而政府的首脑们毫无办法、无能为力，绝望地扒着这条注定沉没的船，绝了得救的望，被抛弃，孤零，被打垮。……

「只有上帝知道将来——我们还能重逢么？」道别时，我噎着嗓子问这两个人。

克伦斯基铁青着脸，一动不动，哑着嗓子低声答：

「难。」

## XIII The Battalion at the Front / 十三 营在前线
===Original===

The same morning on which the telegram came from General Polovtzev there also arrived a banner, with an inscription that read something like this:
“Long live the Provisional Government! Let Those Who Can, Advance! Forward, Brave Women! To the Defence of the Bleeding Motherland!”
We were to march with this banner in the demonstration, that had been organized in opposition to the Bolshevik demonstration fixed for the same day. The Invalids were to march in the same procession. I talked matters over with their chief when we met at Morskaya.
The air was charged with alarming rumours. The Captain of the Invalids placed fifty revolvers at my disposal. I distributed them among the instructors and my other officers, reserving a pair for myself.
The band of the Volynski regiment headed the Battalion of Death, as half the soldiers of that regiment had refused to march against the Bolsheviks, having already been contaminated with Bolshevist ideas, although it was only June.
Mars Field, our destination, was about five versts from our barracks. The whole route was lined with enormous crowds which cheered us and the Invalids, of whom there were about five hundred. Many women on the pavements wept, grieving for the girls whom I was leading into what seemed a conflict with the Bolsheviks. Everybody said: “Something is going to happen today.”
As we approached the Mars Field, where the opposing demonstration was held, I ordered my soldiers to sit down and rest for fifteen minutes.
“Form ranks!” I ordered at the end of that time. We were all more or less nervous, as if on the eve of an offensive. I addressed a few words to the Battalion, instructing them to support me to the end, not to insult anybody, not to run away at the least provocation, in order to avoid a panic. They all pledged themselves to fulfil my instructions.
Before resuming the march the Captain of the Invalids, several of his subordinate officers, and all my instructors came forward and asked to march in the front row with me. I objected, but they insisted, and I finally had to give way, in spite of my desire to show the Bolsheviks that I was not afraid.
The crowds on the Mars Field were indeed enormous. A long procession, with Bolshevist banners, flowed into the great square. We stopped within fifty feet of a Bolshevist cart and were met promptly by a hail of jokes and curses.

===Chinese===

波洛夫采夫（Polovtzev）将军电报到来的同一个早晨，还送来了一面旗帜，题词大略是：
「临时政府万岁！能者奋进！前进，勇敢的妇女们！保卫流血的祖国！」
我们要举着这面旗参加游行——那是对同日举行的布尔什维克游行对抗而组织起来的。伤兵们也参加同一队形。我们在莫尔斯卡娅碰头时，我同他们的头领商议了事务。
空气里弥漫着不祥的谣言。伤兵的上尉拔出五十支左轮枪任我调派。我把它们分发给教官和我的军官们，自己留下一对。
沃伦斯基团的军乐队走在敢死营前头——那个团的士兵有一半已受布尔什维克思想浸染，拒绝去对抗布尔什维克的游行，而那还只是六月。
目的地马尔斯校场离营房约五俄里。一路两旁人山人海，为我们和伤兵们——约五百人——欢呼。人行道上许多妇女落泪，为我领着姑娘们去同布尔什维克似乎要拼一场而悲伤。人人都说：「今天要出事。」
临近对方集会的马尔斯校场，我下令士兵坐下休息一刻钟。
「集合！」时辰一到我下令。人人或多或少地紧张，像进攻前夜。我对全营讲了几句：支持我到底；不得辱骂任何人；不可稍有挑衅就跑散，免生恐慌。她们全体起誓遵守。
开拔前，伤兵的上尉、他的几名下级军官和我全部教官走上前来，请求同我并肩走在头排。我不同意，他们坚持，我只好依了——尽管我本想叫布尔什维克瞧瞧我一人当先的气概。
马尔斯校场上人潮确实浩大。一长列举着布尔什维克旗帜的队伍灌进大广场。我们在离一辆布尔什维克的马车五十英尺处停下，立刻迎头浇来一阵嘲骂。

===Original===

There were jeers at the expense of the Provisional Government and shouts of: “Long live the revolutionary democracy! Down with the war!”
Some of the women could not suppress their indignation and began to answer back, provoking heated argument.
“When you cry, ‘Down with the war!’ you are helping to destroy Free Russia,” I declared, stepping forward and addressing my turbulent neighbours. “We must beat the Germans first and then there will be no war.”
“Kill her! Kill her!” several voices threatened.
Greatly excited, I rushed a few steps nearer to the crowd. My fingers gripped the two pistols, but in all the tumult that followed, the idea was fixed in my mind that I must not shoot at my own people, common workers and peasants.
“Wake up, you deluded sons of Russia! Think what you are doing! You are destroying the Motherland! Scoundrels!” I concluded as their jeers continued.
My instructors tried to hold me back as the throng swarmed round me, but I tore myself out of their arms and plunged into the thick of it. I worked myself up to such a state of frenzy that I did not cease talking even when a volley of shots was sent into our midst. Then my officers ordered the Battalion to fire. There followed a terrible scuffle.
Two of my instructors were killed, one while defending me. Two others were wounded. Ten of my women were also wounded. Many bullets grazed me, but I escaped till struck unconscious by a blow on the head with an iron bar, from behind. Many of the onlookers were drawn into the scrimmage and the result was a panic.
I recovered consciousness in the evening. I was in my own bed with a physician beside it. He told me that although I had lost a good deal of blood my wound was not serious, and that I should be able to resume my duties soon.
Late in the evening the officer in charge reported that Michael Rodzianko had come to see me. The physician went out to meet him and I heard the two conversing in the room next to mine. Rodzianko’s first question was whether I had been killed. It appeared that rumours were being spread in the town that I had been struck dead on the Mars Field. The doctor’s account of my condition apparently came as a joyful relief to the President of the Duma.
He then came in and smilingly approached my bed and kissed me.
“My little heroine, I am very glad that you escaped serious injury. There were many alarming reports about you.

===Chinese===

有嗤笑临时政府的，有喊「革命民主万岁！打倒战争！」的。
一些姑娘压不住火，回起嘴来，激起一场激烈争辩。
「你们喊『打倒战争』，是在帮着毁掉自由的俄国，」我上前一步，对着喧腾的邻人们说。「先打垮德国人，战争自然就没有了。」
「杀了她！杀了她！」几个声音威吓。
我激动极了，朝人群抢近几步。手指扣住两支手枪；可在随后的乱局里，我脑子里只钉着一条：绝不能朝自己人开枪——那都是工人和庄稼人。
「醒醒吧，俄罗斯迷了路的儿子们！想想你们在干什么！你们在毁掉祖国！恶棍！」他们的嗤笑不停，我便这样收了尾。
人群朝我涌来，教官们想拉住我，我挣脱他们的手臂，扎进人堆。我激奋到狂乱，连一排枪打进我们中间时都没住口。我的军官们随即下令开枪。接着是一场可怕的混战。
我的教官死了两个，其中一个是为护我死的；另两个受了伤。我的女兵也伤了十个。多少颗子弹擦过我，我都躲过了，直到后脑挨了一记铁棍，昏死过去。许多看热闹的也被卷进械斗，全场一片惊乱。
恢复知觉已是晚上。我躺自己床上，床边坐着医生。他说我血虽然流了不少，伤却不重，不久便可复职。
深夜，值日军官报告：米哈伊尔·罗将柯（Michael Rodzianko）来看我。医生出去迎接，我听见两人在隔壁屋里交谈。罗将柯头一句便是问：我被打死了没有？城里正流传着我在马尔斯校场丧命的消息。医生述说的病情，显然叫这位杜马主席松了一大口气。
随后他进来，含笑走到床前，吻了我。
「我的小女英雄，你逃过了大难，我非常高兴。关于你，可有不少吓人的传闻。

===Original===

It was a brave act to march straight into the midst of the Bolsheviks. Nevertheless, it was foolish of you and the wounded men to oppose such tremendous odds. I have heard of your victory in the fight against the introduction of the committee system in the Battalion. Well done! I wanted to call and congratulate you earlier, but I have been very busy.”
I sat up in bed to show my visitor that I was quite well. He told me of the appointment of General Kornilov to the command of the southwestern front, and of a luncheon to be given the following day at the Winter Palace, at which Kornilov would be present. Rodzianko inquired if I should be strong enough to attend it, and the physician thought that I probably should. Rodzianko then took his leave, assuring me of his readiness to help me at all times and wishing me a speedy recovery.
The following morning I spent at the window, with my head bandaged, watching my women drill. I felt strong enough to go with Rodzianko to the luncheon. He called before noon and drove me to the Winter Palace. In the reception-room there I was introduced by the President of the Duma to General Kornilov.
Middle-aged, with a spare, manly, vigorous frame, a keen face, grey moustache, Mongol eyes, semi-Mongol cheekbones: this was Kornilov. He spoke little, but every word he uttered rang out clearly. One felt instinctively that here was a man of powerful character and of dogged perseverance.
“I am very glad to meet you,” he said, shaking my hand. “I congratulate you on your determined fight against the committees.”
“General,” I replied, “I was determined because my heart told me that I was in the right.”
“Always follow the advice of your heart,” he said, “and you will do right.”
At this moment Kerensky appeared. We rose to greet him. He shook hands with Kornilov, Rodzianko and me. The War Minister was in a good humour and smiled benignly at me.
“Here is an obstinate little person. I never saw her like,” Kerensky said, pointing at me. “She took it into her head not to form a committee, and nothing could break her will. One must do her justice. She is a diehard, holding out all alone against us all. She foolishly persisted in maintaining that no such law existed.”
“Well,” said Rodzianko in my defence, “she isn’t such a fool. She is perhaps wiser than you and me together.”
We were then asked into the dining-room.

===Chinese===

径直走进布尔什维克中间，是勇敢之举。可你带着伤兵同那样悬殊的人数对抗，也是莽撞。你们营里抵制设立委员会那一仗，我听说了。干得好！我早想登门道贺，只是太忙。」
我在床上坐起身，向客人显示自己全好了。他告诉我科尔尼洛夫将军出任西南前线司令的消息，又说明天冬宫有一场午宴，科尔尼洛夫也出席。罗将柯问我撑不撑得起赴宴，医生认为多半可以。罗将柯随即告辞，再三说随时愿帮我，祝我早日康复。
第二天早晨，我裹着头坐在窗前，看我的女兵操练。我觉得力气够随罗将柯赴宴了。他中午前来接我，驱车同往冬宫。在会客室里，杜马主席把我引见给科尔尼洛夫将军。
中年，瘦削、魁健、有男子气的身架，一张敏锐的脸，灰胡须，蒙古式的眼睛，半蒙古式的颗骨：这就是科尔尼洛夫。他话少，可说出的每一个字都掷地有声。人本能地觉出：这是一个性格强悍、百折不回的人。
「很高兴见到你，」他握住我的手说。「祝贺你同委员会们的那场坚决斗争。」
「将军，」我答，「我坚决，因为我的心告诉我，我在理上。」
「永远听你心的指教，」他说，「你就不会错。」
这时克伦斯基（Kerensky）来了。我们起身相迎。他同科尔尼洛夫、罗将柯和我一一握手。陆军大臣心情很好，朝我慈祥地笑。
「瞧这位睪姑娘。这样的家伙我没见过第二个，」克伦斯基指着我。「她打定了主意不设委员会，谁也拗不过她的意志。得给她句公道话：她是块硬骨头，一个人顶住我们所有人。她还愚蠢地一口咬定，根本没有那样一条法令。」
「哼，」罗将柯护着我，「她可不是傻子。她说不定比你加我还高明。」
随后请我们入席。

===Original===

Kerensky was seated at the head of the table, I at its opposite end. Rodzianko was on Kerensky’s right, Kornilov was on my right. There were also three Allied Generals present. One was on my left, and the other two were between Kerensky and Kornilov.
The conversation was carried on mostly in a foreign tongue and I understood nothing. Besides, I had my troubles with the dishes and table etiquette. I did not know how to deal with the unfamiliar dishes, and blushed deeply several times, while I watched my neighbours from the corners of my eyes.
Now and then I engaged in conversation with Kornilov. He approved my decided views about the necessity of discipline in the army, and declared that if discipline were not restored, then Russia was lost. The burden of Kerensky’s conversation at the table was, that in spite of the considerable disintegration that was thinning the ranks of the army, it was not too late as yet. He was contemplating a trip to the front, feeling certain that it would lead to our troops taking the offensive.
Finally Kerensky got up, and the luncheon was over. He told me before leaving that there would be a solemn presentation to me of the two standards and icons sent by the soldiers from the front. I replied that I did not deserve such honours, but hoped to be able to justify his trust in me.
Kornilov parted from me cordially, inviting me to call on him at his headquarters when I arrived at the front. Rodzianko then escorted me home and asked me to come to see him before leaving for the front.
The time remaining before the date fixed by Kerensky for the dedication of the Battalion’s battle flag was spent in intensive training and rifle practice. The women were almost ready to go to the front and awaited June 25 with impatience.
Finally that day arrived. The women were in high spirits. My heart was filled with expectation. The Battalion arose early. Every soldier had a new uniform. The rifles were spick and span. There was a holiday-feeling in the air. We were all cheerful, though nervous under the weight of responsibility which the day was to bring.
At nine in the morning two bands arrived at our gates. They were followed by Captain Kuzmin, assistant Commander of the Petrograd Military District, with instructions for the Battalion to be at the St. Isaac’s Cathedral at ten o’clock in full military array.

===Chinese===

克伦斯基坐主位，我坐对面末端。罗将柯在克伦斯基右手，科尔尼洛夫在我右手。席上还有三位协约国将军，一位在我左手，另两位在克伦斯基与科尔尼洛夫之间。
谈话多半用外语，我一句不懂。何况我同菜式和席规也搏斗了一番：那些陌生菜不知怎么下手，好几次脸红到耳根，眼角却瞟着邻座学样。
我时时同科尔尼洛夫交谈几句。他赞成我对军队必须重振纪律的决断看法，断言纪律若不恢复，俄国就完了。克伦斯基席间的话大意是：军队减员固然严重，为时尚未为晚；他正打算巡祝前线，深信此行将促成我军转入进攻。
末了克伦斯基起身，午宴结束。临别他对我说，前线士兵送来的两面军旗和圣像，将为我举行一场隆重的授赠礼。我答道：我不配这样的荣誉，只盼不辜负他对我的信任。
科尔尼洛夫同我亲热作别，请我到前线后去他司令部看他。罗将柯送我回家，嘱我赴前线前来见他一面。
离克伦斯基定下的营旗祝圣日还有一段日子，全用在强化训练和射击练习上。姑娘们差不多可以开赴前线了，焦急地等着六月二十五日。
这一天终于来了。姑娘们情绪高涨，我心里满是期待。全营早早起身，人人一套新军装，步枪锃亮，空气里有节日的味道。人人欢喜，又因这一天将压上的责任而紧张。
上午九点，两支军乐队开到我们门口。随后是库兹明（Kuzmin）上尉——彼得格勒军区副司令——带来命令：全营十点整军容开赴圣以撒大教堂（St. Isaac’s Cathedral）。

===Original===

We started out almost immediately, led by the two military bands.
The throng of people moving in the direction of the Cathedral was enormous. The entire neighbourhood was lined up with units of the garrison. There were troops of all kinds. There was even a body of Cossacks, with flags on the points of their spears. A group of distinguished citizens and officers stood on the steps leading to the entrance of the church. It included Kerensky, Rodzianko, Milyukov, Kornilov, Polovtzev and others. The Battalion saluted as we marched into the huge building.
The officiating clergy were two bishops and twelve priests. The church was filled to overflowing. A hush fell on the vast gathering as I was asked to step forward and give my name. I was seized with fear, as if in the presence of God Himself. The standard that was to be consecrated was placed in my hand and two old battle flags were crossed over it, hiding me almost completely in their folds. The officiating bishops then addressed me, dwelling upon the unprecedented honour implied in the dedication of an army standard for a woman.
It was not customary to inscribe the name of a Commander on the flag of a military unit, he explained, but the name of Maria Bochkareva was emblazoned on this standard, which, in case of my death, would be returned to the Cathedral and never used by another Commander. As he spoke and said the prayers, in the course of which he sprinkled me three times with holy water, I prayed to the Lord with all my heart and might. The ceremony lasted about an hour, after which two soldiers, delegates from the First and Third armies presented to me two icons, given by fellow-soldiers, with inscriptions on the cases, expressing their trust in me as the woman who would lead Russia to honour and renown.
I was humbled. I did not consider myself worthy of such honours. When asked to receive the two icons I fell on my knees before them and prayed for God’s guidance. How could I, an ignorant woman, justify the hope and trust of so many brave and enlightened sons of my country?
General Kornilov, representing the army, then presented me with a revolver and sword with handles of gold.
“You have deserved these gallant weapons, and you will not disgrace them,” he said, and kissed me on the cheek.
I kissed the sword, and pledged myself never to disgrace the weapons and to use them in the defence of my country.

===Chinese===

我们几乎立刻出发，两支军乐队前导。
涌向大教堂的人潮浩大无比。教堂左近整段街面都站满了卫戍部队，各兵种都有，甚至还有一队哥萨克，矛尖上挑着小旗。教堂门前的台阶上立着一群显贵和军官，其中有克伦斯基、罗将柯、米留可夫（Milyukov）、科尔尼洛夫、波洛夫采夫等。我们开进那座巨殿时，全营行注目礼。
主礼的是两位主教和十二位司祭。教堂挤得水泄不通。叫我出列报名时，全场一片肃静。我心生敬畏，如在上帝亲面前。待祝圣的军旗放进我手中，两面旧战旗交叉覆在它上面，几乎把我整个人掩在旗影里。主礼的主教随后向我致辞，细说为一名女子祝圣军旗是何等空前的殊荣。
